4

Figure 3.3
:
Left: Pendant graph. Right: 5-cycle graph.

Variants of the GM model to the case of graphical systems with reneging are investigated, respectively in
[
21
,
39
,
46
]
and
[
29
]
(see also
[
8
]
).

3.5
Optimizations of the matching model

In another line of research, such stochastic matching architectures are addressed from the point of view of stochastic optimization in
[
11
]
,
[
21
]
and
[
39
]
, among others.

Buke and Chen
[
11
]
have focused on the
infinite-horizon average-cost optimal control problem
. In which, they considered a
control policy
determines which are matched at each time by considering a discrete-time bipartite matching model with random arrivals of units of
supply
and
demand
that can wait in queues located at the nodes in the network.
For a parameterized family of models in which the network load approaches capacity, a new matching policy for the relaxation admits a closed-form expression is shown to be approximately optimal, with bounded regret, even though the average cost grows without bound.

Figure 3.4
:
A queueing network view of a system with four input streams and two matchings.

Gurvich and Ward
[
21
]
have considered a model in which each item arrive in a dedicated queue, and wait to be matched with items that exist in other queues (possibly multiple). Once a decision has been made to match, the match itself is instantaneous and the corresponding items leave the system. Upon arrival, an item may find several possible matching, in this cases, an
exsisting controller
must decide which matchings to execute given multiple options. They considered the problem of
minimizing finite-horizon cumulative holding costs
. In principle, the controller may choose to wait until some “inventory” of items builds up to facilitate more profitable matches in the future.

In the example depicted in Figure
3.4
, there are 4 classes of items, and items of class
i
arrive according to a time-varying Poisson process
A_{i}
having instantaneous rate
\lambda_{i}(t),\;i=1,\,2,\,3,\,4.
Items of class 1 can be matched to items of class 2. Items of class 2 can be also matched with items of classes 3 and 4. This matching structure is reflected in the graph in Figure
3.4
where each rectangle corresponds to an item class and each of the circles
A
and
B
to matching types. When a class 1 item is matched with a class 2 item they both leave the system: matchings are instantaneous. An item of class 4 must be matched to both a class 3 and a class 2 item to depart.

Nazari and Stolyar
[
39
]
have introduced an algorithm that is a variant of the “Primal-dual algorithm", allowing to achieve stability if this is feasible at all, for a very large class of models. The proposed algorithm furthermore optimizes utility functions that are convex functions of the average matching rates.
[
21
]
and
[
39
]

allow idling policies
, i.e., scheduling algorithms
allowing to perform no matchin
g at all despite the presence of matchable items in the system,
to wait for more profitable future matches.
Allowing idling policy makes sense in applications such as assemble-to-order systems, advertisement or operations scheduling, but is much less suitable to kidney transplant networks, in which case the practitioners always perform a transplant whenever one is possible.

3.6
Other extensions of matching models

Specific comparison results concerning single-server queueing systems with impatient customers are also provided in
[
34
]
and
[
35
]
. Moreover, it is well known since the seminal work of Propp and Wilson
[
42
]
, that coupling from the past algorithms, which mostly use backward coupling convergence, provides a powerful tool for simulating in many cases (monotonicity, stochastic bounds of Markov chains) the steady state of the system.

Along these lines, the above is devoted to the explicit construction of a stationary queue with
S_{r}
servers
(S_{r}\geq 1)
and impatient customers, by a scheme à la Loynes. Models with impatience (or abandonment, reneging) have been introduced in the queueing literature to represent a strong real-time constraint on the system: the requests have a due date, before which their treatment must be initiated, or completed. Specifically, assume hereafter that any incoming customer is either served if a server becomes available before its deadline or else eliminated forever once the deadline has elapsed. Observe that a loss system of
S_{r}
systems (i.e., there is no waiting room, so the incoming customers are either served provided that a server is immediately available or immediately lost otherwise) is a particular case of the present model, for identically null patience. There are
S_{r}
servers obeying the First Come, First Served (
fcfs
) rule to serve impatient customers, and the sequences of inter-arrival times, service times, and patience times of the customers are assumed stationary and ergodic, but not necessarily independent.

Buke and Chen
[
10
]
have introduced a new queueing model, called
probabilistic matching system
, to model the traffic in web portals. This queueing model consists of two user classes, in which users wait in the system to match a candidate from the other class, instead of accessing a resource. They have stabilized
four admissible matching policies
for the probabilistic matching systems which are:

1-

the simple threshold policy,

2-

accept-the-shortest-queue policy,

3-

functional threshold policy,

4-

the one-sided threshold policy.

This study of
[
10
]
is followed by
[
11
]
which have proposed approximation methods and analyzed its properties based on fluid and diffusion
limits. They performed numerical experiments to gain insight into probabilistic matching systems.
Also, they showed that some performance measures are insensitive to the matching probability, agreeing with the existing results.

Adan & al.
[
4
]
have considered
three parallel service models
in which customers of several types are served by several types of servers subject to a
bipartite graph
, and the
service policy is First Come, First Served
. Two of the models have a fixed set of servers.

•

The first is a queueing model in which arriving customers are
assigned to the longest idling compatible server
if available, or else queue up in a single queue, and servers that become available pick the longest waiting compatible customer as studied in
[
5
]
.

•

The second is a
redundancy service mode
l where arriving customers split into copies that queue up at all the compatible servers, and are served in each queue on
fcfs
basis, and leave the system when the first copy completes service.

•

The third model is a matching queueing model with a random stream of arriving servers. Arriving customers queue in a single queue and arriving servers match with the first compatible customer and leave immediately with the customer, or they leave without a customer.

They
studied the relations between these models
, and showed that they are closely related to the
fcfs
infinite bipartite matching model, in which two infinite sequences of customers and servers of several types are matched
fcfs
according to a bipartite compatibility graph. They also introduced a directed bipartite matching model in which they embed the queueing systems. This leads to a generalization of Burke’s theorem to parallel service systems.

3.7
Problem statement and positioning

A stochastic matching model, as we said before, is a system of components and each of these components could have more than one state of functioning.

The main purpose of this thesis is devoted to three contexts:

First, we study the long-run stability of stochastic matching models, in the sense defined above, on a hypergraphical compatibility matching structure generalizing the approach of
[
33
]
to hypergraphs instead of graphs. By doing so, the two closest references to the present work are
[
21
]
and
[
39
]
: in both cases, a general matching model is addressed on a hypergraphical matching structure (notice that
[
39
]
also allows matchings including several items of the same class). The first reference addresses continuous-time models; the second considers discrete-time models, however, most of the results therein can easily be extended to the continuous-time settings. In
[
21
]
a matching control is introduced, that asymptotically minimizes the holding cost of items in an unstable system (we justify the instability of such systems under the assumptions of
[
21
]
in Remark
4.3
). In the present thesis all the matching policies we consider are non-idling, i.e., entering items are always matched right away if this is possible at all. Thus, the model studied in Chapter
4
is a special case of the model studied in
[
39
]
, for simple arrivals,
no same-class matchings
, and
non-idling matching policies
.

Secondly, we showed how several stability results of
[
33
,
36
,
29
]
can be generalized to the case of a multigraphical matching structure which is motivated again by concrete applications, among which dating websites and peer-to-peer interfaces, it is natural to assume that items
of the same class
can be matched together. Hence, the need to generalize the previous line of research to the case where the matching architecture is a
multigraph
(a graph admitting
self-loops
, that is, edges connecting nodes to themselves), rather than just a graph.

On other hand, we study in continuous-time different examples of multigraph
G=({\mathcal{V}},{\mathcal{E}})
and hypergraph
\mathbb{H}=({\mathcal{V}},{\mathcal{H}})
corresponding respectively matching queues
(G,\Phi,\lambda)_{C}
and
(\mathbb{H},\Phi,\lambda)_{C}.
We deduce the precise stability regions of the corresponding stochastic matching models using the fluid limit techniques.

Finally, we present an application for organ transplantation of a stochastic model on hypergraphs, in which we compare the behavior in the long-run stability of the model of complete 3-uniform hypergraphs matching (three-by-three) with the model of complete 3-partite graph matching (two-by-two). Then, according to the distribution of the items, we deduce what is the best matching procedure between two-by-two and three-by-three matchings.

Part II
Contributions

Chapter 4
Hypergraphs

Introduction

In the Stochastic Matching model (introduced in the bipartite case by Caldentey and al. in
[
16
]
and generalized by Mairesse and Moyal in
[
33
]
), items enter the system randomly and may be matched or not according to their classes. The compatibility between classes is given by a fixed matching structure. In this chapter, we study the long-run stability of stochastic matching
models, in the sense defined above, on a hypergraphical compatibility matching structure.

Several applications should naturally incorporate the possibility of matching items by groups of more than two. Let us exemplify this on a concrete example: in organ transplants, (in)-compatibility between givers and receivers are given by a variety of factors, and
mostly by blood types and immunological factors.
In kidney exchange programs, items represent intra-incompatible couples
(A,B)

(e.g., a patient
A
waiting for a transplant and
B
a parent of his/hers, incompatible with
A
for a potential organ donation),
entering a system to find another intra-incompatible couple
(A^{\prime},B^{\prime})
that is compatible with it, in the sense that

A
can receive an organ from
B^{\prime}
and
A^{\prime}
can receive from
B
. Then the ability of such a system to accommodate all requests and to maximize
the number of successful transplants and avoid congestion, is translated into the positive recurrence of a stochastic process representing the stochastic system over time.
Then if we view the items as the
couples
, and translate the “cross-compatibility”
(i.e.,
A
can receive from
B^{\prime}
and
A^{\prime}
can receive from
B
) into the existence of an edge between node
(A,B)
and node
(A^{\prime},B^{\prime})
, such a system is a typical application of the GM introduced in
[
33
]
.

Let us now consider the case where such exchanges
(A,B)\leftrightarrow(A^{\prime},B^{\prime})
and
(A^{\prime},B^{\prime})\leftrightarrow(A^{\prime\prime},B^{\prime\prime})

cannot be realized, but
A
can receive from
B^{\prime}
,
A^{\prime}
can receive from
{B^{\prime\prime}}
and
{A^{\prime\prime}}
can receive from
B
.
Then it is natural to consider the possibility of executing the three transplants contemporarily, i.e., to match the
triplet
(A,B)
,
(A^{\prime},B^{\prime})
and
(A^{\prime\prime},B^{\prime\prime})
altogether.
In several countries including the U.S., such “exchanges” by groups of 3 (or more) are allowed, which raises the issue of maximizing “matchings” that do not coincide with sets of edges, but of sets of subsets of nodes of cardinality
3
or more. Hence the need to consider matching models on compatibility matching structures that are
hypergraphs
rather than graphs, i.e., a set of nodes
{\mathcal{V}}
equipped with a set of subsets of
{\mathcal{V}}
of cardinality 3 or more.

The hypergraphical stochastic matching model addressed in this chapter is formally defined as follows: items enter the system by single arrivals and get matched by groups of 2 or more, following compatibilities that are represented by a given hypergraph.
A matching policy determines the matchings to be executed in the case of a multiple-choice, and the unmatched items are stored in a buffer, waiting for a future match.

At the border between discrete mathematics and probability theory, the main scientific aim of this chapter is to study this widely applicable class of models. In the first step, addressing the crucial question of stability of the system will lead us to study the structural properties of hypergraphs (connectivity, independent sets, rank, anti-rank, degree, size of the transversals, existence of cycles, and so on.) In a second step, we will address the weak approximation of the natural Markov process of the model, to better apprehend its main characteristics in steady state, its long-run simulation, and possibly, the estimation of its parameters.

This chapter is organized as follows: we start in Section
4.1
by providing necessary conditions of stability for the present class of systems:
as will be developed therein, and unlike the particular case of the GM on graphs (see
[
33
]
), for which a natural necessary condition could be obtained, we introduce various necessary conditions that depend on distinct geometrical properties of the considered hypergraphs. We then deduce from this, classes of hypergraphs for which the corresponding matching model cannot be stable, see Section
4.2
. Finally, in Section
4.3
, we provide the precise stability region in the particular cases where the compatibility hypergraph is complete
3
-uniform, complete
k
-partite
3
-uniform and then complete up to a partition of its hyperedges (see the precise definitions of these objects below).
We conclude and discuss this chapter in Section
4.4
.

Throughout this chapter, let us consider that the matching structure
\mathbb{S}
be a hypergraph
\mathbb{H}=({\mathcal{V}},{\mathcal{H}}).
Recall that
V_{n}
is an item which enters the system from the measure
\mu
on
{\mathcal{V}}
at time
n
and
H(k)
is the realized matching at time
k
.

4.1
Necessary conditions of stability

Fix a matching model
(\mathbb{H},\Phi,\mu)
on a hypergraph
\mathbb{H}=({\mathcal{V}},{\mathcal{H}})
.
Denote for any
n
,
B\subset{\mathcal{V}}
and
{\mathcal{B}}\subset{\mathcal{H}}
, by
A_{n}(B)
the number of arrivals of elements in
B
and by
M_{n}({\mathcal{B}})
the number of matchings of hyperedges in
{\mathcal{B}}
realized up to
n
, i.e.,

\displaystyle A_{n}(B)

\displaystyle=\sum_{k=1}^{n}{\mathchoice{\rm 1\mskip-4.0mul}{\rm 1\mskip-4.0mul}{\rm 1\mskip-4.5mul}{\rm 1\mskip-5.0mul}}_{\{V_{k}\in B\}};

\displaystyle M_{n}\left({\mathcal{B}}\right)

\displaystyle=\sum_{k=1}^{n}{\mathchoice{\rm 1\mskip-4.0mul}{\rm 1\mskip-4.0mul}{\rm 1\mskip-4.5mul}{\rm 1\mskip-5.0mul}}_{\{H(k)\in{\mathcal{B}}\}};

and with some abuse, denote
A_{n}(i)=A_{n}(\{i\})
and
M_{n}(H)=M_{n}(\{H\})
for any
i\in{\mathcal{V}}
and
H\in{\mathcal{H}}
.
Observe that the following key relation holds for all
B\subset{\mathcal{V}}
,

X_{n}(B)=A_{n}(B)-\sum\limits_{H\in{\mathcal{H}}}\left|H\cap B\right|M_{n}\left(H\right)\geq 0,\,\,n\in{\mathbb{N}},

(4.1)

since the number of items of classes in
B
at any time
n
is precisely the number of arrivals of such items up to time
n
, minus
the number of these items that leave the system upon each matching of a hyperedge that intersects with
B
.

4.1.1
General conditions

We start by introducing several ‘universal’ stability conditions. Fix a hypergraph
\mathbb{H}=({\mathcal{V}},{\mathcal{H}})
throughout the section.

Definition 4.1
.

We say that
I\subset{\mathcal{V}}
is an independent set of
{\mathbb{H}}
if
I
does not include any hyperedge of
{\mathbb{H}}
, i.e, for any

H\in{\mathcal{H}}
,
H\cap\bar{I}\neq\emptyset
. We recall that
\mathbb{I}({\mathbb{H}})
be the set of all independent sets of
{\mathbb{H}}
.

Let us define for any
\mu\in\mathscr{M}({\mathcal{V}})
, and any
B\subset{\mathcal{V}}
, the set

L_{\mu}(B)=\mbox{argmin}\left\{\mu(j)\,:\,j\in B\right\},\quad B\subset{\mathcal{V}}.

(4.2)

To clarify the exposition of the Lemma
4.1
(stated below), we need to introduce the following notion,

Definition 4.2
.

For any
\mu\in\mathscr{M}({\mathcal{V}})
we say that the independent set

I\in\mathbb{I}({\mathbb{H}})
is
\mu
-minimal if the intersection of any hyperedge
H\in{\mathcal{H}}
with
I
is either empty,
or reduced to a singleton
\{v_{H}\}
that is such that:

•

v_{H}
is of degree 1, i.e.,
H
is the only hyperedge
v_{H}
belongs to;

•

L_{\mu}(H)=\{v_{H}\}
, i.e.,
v_{H}
is the only minimum of
\mu
over the set
H
.

An independent set
I\in\mathbb{I}({\mathbb{H}})
that is not
\mu
-minimal is said non-
\mu
-minimal.
We let
\mathbb{I}_{\mu}({\mathbb{H}})
be the set of
\mu
-minimal independent sets of
{\mathbb{H}}
, and
{\mathbb{I}^{\prime}_{\mu}({\mathbb{H}})}
be
the set of non-
\mu
-minimal independent sets of
{\mathbb{H}}
, that is, the complement set of
\mathbb{I}_{\mu}({\mathbb{H}})
in
\mathbb{I}({\mathbb{H}})
.

In other words, a
\mu
-minimal independent sets gathers nodes that are the only minimum of
\mu
over the only hyperedge they belong to.
Notice that the collection
\mathbb{I}_{\mu}({\mathbb{H}})
can be empty. This is the case if and only if all nodes are of degree at least 2, or all nodes
of degree 1 are not the only minimum of
\mu
on the single hyperedge they belong to. Observe the following characterization,

Lemma 4.1
.

Let
{\mathbb{H}}=({\mathcal{V}},{\mathcal{H}})
be a hypergraph, and denote
{\mathcal{H}}=\{H_{1},...,H_{m}\}
.
An independent set
I=\{v_{1},...,v_{p}\}
is
\mu
-minimal if and only if for all
n
and all
k_{1},...,k_{m}
such that
k_{j}\in L_{\mu}(H_{j})
for all
j
,

A_{n}(I)=\sum_{j=1}^{m}|H_{j}\cap I|A_{n}(k_{j}).

(4.3)

Proof.

First, it is clear that if
I\in\mathbb{I}_{\mu}({\mathbb{H}})
, then
m\geq p
and the mapping

\varphi:\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}\left\{j\in\llbracket 1,m\rrbracket\,:\,I\cap H_{j}\neq\emptyset\right\}&\longrightarrow\llbracket 1,p\rrbracket\\
j&\longmapsto i\,:\,L_{\mu}(H_{j})=H_{j}\cap I=\{v_{i}\}\end{array}\right.

(4.4)

is bijective.
Thus we have a.s. for all
n
,

A_{n}(I)=\sum_{j=1}^{m}A_{n}(v_{\varphi(j)})=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}j\in\llbracket 1,m\rrbracket:\\
H_{j}\cap I\neq\emptyset\end{subarray}}|H_{j}\cap I|A_{n}(v_{\varphi(j)})=\sum_{j=1}^{m}|H_{j}\cap I|A_{n}(k_{j}).

Let us now assume that
I\in\mathbb{I}^{\prime}_{\mu}({\mathbb{H}})
. Then,

•

If for some hyperedge
H_{j}
is such that
|H_{j}\cap I|\geq 2
, then upon each arrival of an element of class
k_{j}
, the
right-hand side of (
4.3
) increases by
|H_{j}\cap I|
while the left-hand side increases by
1 if
k_{j}\in I
, or 0 else;

•

If for some hyperedge
H_{j}
intersecting with
I
, there exists
k_{j}\in L_{\mu}(H_{j})\cap\bar{I}
, then upon each arrival of a class
k_{j}
-item the
right-hand-side of (
4.3
) increases while the left-hand side does not;

•

Finally, if for all
j\in\llbracket 1,m\rrbracket
,
|H_{j}\cap I|\leq 1
and for all
j
such that
|H_{j}\cap I|=1
,
L_{\mu}(H_{j})=\{v_{\varphi(j)}\}
(defining again
\varphi
by (
4.4
)), then if
\varphi(j)=\varphi(l)
for some
l\neq j
,
upon each arrival of a class
v_{\varphi(j)}
-item, the right-hand side of (
4.3
) increases by 2 while the left-hand side increases by 1.

In all cases, (
4.3
) cannot hold for all
n
, which concludes the proof.
∎

Now define the following set of measures,

\mathscr{N}_{1}(\mathbb{H})=\left\{\mu\in\mathscr{M}({\mathcal{V}}):\mbox{\small{for all }}I\in\mathbb{I}^{\prime}_{\mu}({\mathbb{H}}),\,\mu(I)\;<\sum_{H\in{\mathcal{H}}}\left|H\cap I\right|\min_{k\in H}\mu(k)\right\}.

We have the following result,

Proposition 4.1
.

For any connected hypergraph
\mathbb{H}
and any admissible matching policy
\Phi
,

\textsc{Stab}({\mathbb{H}},\Phi)\subset\mathscr{N}_{1}(\mathbb{H}).

Proof.

Fix
{\mathbb{H}}=({\mathcal{V}},{\mathcal{H}})
and an admissible policy
\Phi
. Denote by
H_{1},...,H_{m}
the hyperedges of
{\mathbb{H}}
.
Suppose that
\mu\in\mathscr{M}({\mathcal{V}})
is such that there exists an independent set
I\in\mathbb{I}^{\prime}_{\mu}({\mathbb{H}})
such that

\mu(I)>\sum\limits_{H\in{\mathcal{H}}}\left|H\cap I\right|\min_{k\in H}\mu(k).

(4.5)

For any
i\in\llbracket 1,m\rrbracket
and any
k_{i}\in L_{\mu}(H_{i})
we have that

M_{n}(H_{i})\leq\min_{k\in H_{i}}A_{n}(k)\leq A_{n}\left(k_{i}\right),\quad n\geq 0.

Thus, from the equality in (
4.1
), for any
k_{1},...,k_{m}
such that
k_{i}\in L_{\mu}(H_{i})
for all
i
, we have that

{X_{n}(I)\over n}\geq{A_{n}(I)\over n}-\sum\limits_{i=1}^{m}\left|H_{i}\cap I\right|{A_{n}\left(k_{i}\right)\over n},\quad n\geq 1.

(4.6)

Applying the SLLN to the right-hand side of (
4.2.2
) implies that for any such
k_{1},...,k_{m}
,

\limsup_{n}{X_{n}(I)\over n}\geq\mu(I)-\sum\limits_{i=1}^{m}\left|H_{i}\cap I\right|\mu\left(k_{i}\right)=\mu(I)-\sum\limits_{H\in{\mathcal{H}}}\left|H\cap I\right|\min_{k\in H}\mu\left(k\right)>0,

implying that
X_{n}(I)
goes a.s. to infinity and thereby (as
X_{n}=[W_{n}]
for all
n
), the transience of
\left\{W_{n};\,n\in{\mathbb{N}}\right\}
.

Assume now that
\mu
is such that for some independent set
I\in\mathbb{I}^{\prime}_{\mu}({\mathbb{H}})
, an equality holds in (
4.5
).
Then, for any
k_{1},...,k_{m}
such that
k_{j}\in L_{\mu}(H_{j})
for all
j
,
the Markov chain
\left\{Y_{n};\,n\in{\mathbb{N}}\right\}
defined as

Y_{n}=A_{n}(I)\;-\;\sum\limits_{j=1}^{m}\left|H_{j}\cap B\right|A_{n}\left(k_{j}\right),\quad n\in{\mathbb{N}},

is a random walk with drift 0 that is different from the identically null process, in view of Lemma
4.1
.
Hence
\left\{Y_{n};\,n\in{\mathbb{N}}\right\}
is null recurrent. Would the chain
\left\{W_{n};\,n\in{\mathbb{N}}\right\}
be positive recurrent, the sequence

\left\{X_{n};\,n\in{\mathbb{N}}\right\}
would visit the state

\mathbf{0}
infinitely often, with inter-passage time at
\mathbf{0}
of finite expectation.
Thus from (
4.2.2
), the sequence
\left\{Y_{n};\,n\in{\mathbb{N}}\right\}
would be positive recurrent, an absurdity. This concludes the proof.
∎

Define the following sets of measures,

\displaystyle\mathscr{N}^{\tiny{+}}_{1}(\mathbb{H})

\displaystyle=\left\{\mu\in\mathscr{M}({\mathcal{V}}):\,\forall I\in\mathbb{I}({\mathbb{H}}),\,\mu(I)\;<\sum_{H\in{\mathcal{H}}}\left|H\cap I\right|\min_{k\in H\cap\bar{I}}\mu(k)\right\};

\displaystyle\mathscr{N}_{1}^{\tiny{++}}({\mathbb{H}})

\displaystyle=\left\{\mu\in\mathscr{M}({\mathcal{V}}):\,\forall B\subset{\mathcal{V}},\,\mu(B)\;\leq\sum_{H\in{\mathcal{H}}}\left|H\cap B\right|\min_{k\in H}\mu(k)\right\}.

We have the following result,

Corollary 4.1
.

For any connected hypergraph
\mathbb{H}
and any admissible matching policy
\Phi
,

\textsc{Stab}({\mathbb{H}},\Phi)\subset\mathscr{N}_{1}^{\tiny{+}}(\mathbb{H})\cap\mathscr{N}_{1}^{\tiny{++}}(\mathbb{H}).

Proof.

We just show that
\mathscr{N}_{1}(\mathbb{H})
is included in
\mathscr{N}_{1}^{\scriptsize{+}}(\mathbb{H})\cap\mathscr{N}_{1}^{\tiny{++}}(\mathbb{H})
. Set again
{\mathcal{H}}=\{H_{1},...,H_{m}\}
and fix
\mu\in\mathscr{N}_{1}(\mathbb{H})
.
To show that
\mu\in\mathscr{N}_{1}^{\tiny{+}}(\mathbb{H})
, first observe that for any independent set
I=\{v_{1},...,v_{p}\}\in\mathbb{I}_{\mu}({\mathbb{H}})
, for any hyperedge
H_{j}

intersecting with
I,
we have that
\min_{k\in H_{j}}\mu(k)=\mu(v_{\varphi(j)})<\min_{k\in H_{j}\cap\bar{I}}\mu(k).
Therefore,

\mu(I)=\sum_{i=1}^{p}\mu(v_{i})=\sum_{j=1}^{m}|H_{j}\cap I|\mu(v_{\varphi(j)})<\sum_{H\in{\mathcal{H}}}|H\cap I|\min_{k\in H\cap\bar{I}}\mu(k),

whereas if
I\in\mathbb{I}^{\prime}_{\mu}({\mathbb{H}})
, as
\mu\in\mathscr{N}_{1}(\mathbb{H})
we have that

\mu(I)<\sum_{H\in{\mathcal{H}}}|H\cap I|\min_{k\in H}\mu(k)\leq\sum_{H\in{\mathcal{H}}}|H\cap I|\min_{k\in H\cap\bar{I}}\mu(k),

hence
\mu\in\mathscr{N}_{1}^{\tiny{+}}(\mathbb{H})
.

It remains to show that
\mu\in\mathscr{N}_{1}^{\tiny{++}}(\mathbb{H})
, and for this, we first observe that

\mbox{for all }I\in\mathbb{I}({\mathbb{H}}),\,\mu(I)\;\leq\sum_{H\in{\mathcal{H}}}\left|H\cap I\right|\min_{k\in H}\mu(k).

(4.7)

To see this, it suffices to observe that for any independent set
I\in\mathbb{I}_{\mu}({\mathbb{H}})
, recalling (
4.4
),

\mu(I)=\sum_{j=1}^{m}\mu(v_{\varphi(j)})=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}j\in\llbracket 1,m\rrbracket:\\
H_{j}\cap I\neq\emptyset\end{subarray}}|H_{j}\cap I|\mu(v_{\varphi(j)})=\sum_{j=1}^{m}|H_{j}\cap I|\mu(k_{j})=\sum_{H\in{\mathcal{H}}}|H\cap I|\min_{k\in H}\mu(k),

hence (
4.7
).
Now fix
B
, a subset of
{\mathcal{V}}
that is not an independent set of
{\mathbb{H}}
.
Then, we construct by induction the family of sets
B:=B_{0}\supset B_{1}\supset B_{2}\supset...\supset B_{r}
, where
r
is properly defined below, as follows: for any
i\geq 0
, if
B_{i}
is not an independent set of
\mathbb{I}({\mathbb{H}})
,
then we take an arbitrary hyperedge
H_{j_{i}}\in{\mathcal{H}}
such that
H_{j_{i}}\subset B_{i}
, and set
B_{i+1}=B_{i}\setminus\{k_{i}\}
, for an arbitrary
k_{i}\in L_{\mu}(H_{j_{i}})
.
Then, there exists an integer
r\leq|B|-1
such that
B_{r}
is an independent set of
\mathbb{I}({\mathbb{H}})
, and we stop the construction at this point.
Observe that for any
i\in\llbracket 0,r-1\rrbracket
,

\mu(B_{i+1})\;\leq\sum_{H\in{\mathcal{H}}}\left|H\cap B_{i+1}\right|\min_{k\in H}\mu(k)\,\,\Longrightarrow\,\,\mu(B_{i})\;\leq\sum_{H\in{\mathcal{H}}}\left|H\cap B_{i}\right|\min_{k\in H}\mu(k).

(4.8)

To see this, fix
i
and suppose that the left-hand side of the above holds true. Then, we have

\mu(B_{i})=\mu(B_{i+1})+\mu(k_{i}),

(4.9)

and on the other hand,

\sum_{H\in{\mathcal{H}}}\left|H\cap B_{i}\right|\min_{k\in H}\mu(k)\\
\begin{aligned} &=\sum_{H\in\overline{{\mathcal{H}}(k_{i})}}\left|H\cap B_{i}\right|\min_{k\in H}\mu(k)+\sum_{H\in{\mathcal{H}}(k_{i})}\left|H\cap B_{i}\right|\min_{k\in H}\mu(k)\\
&=\sum_{H\in\overline{{\mathcal{H}}(k_{i})}}\left|H\cap B_{i+1}\right|\min_{k\in H}\mu(k)+\sum_{H\in{\mathcal{H}}(k_{i})}\left(\left|H\cap B_{i+1}\right|+1\right)\min_{k\in H}\mu(k)\\
&=\sum_{H\in{\mathcal{H}}}\left|H\cap B_{i+1}\right|\min_{k\in H}\mu(k)+\sum_{H\in{\mathcal{H}}(k_{i})}\mu(k_{H}),\end{aligned}

(4.10)

where for any
H\in{\mathcal{H}}(k_{i})
,
k_{H}
is an arbitrary element of
L_{\mu}(H)
.
But
\mu(k_{i})
is less or equal than the second term of the latter sum because
\mu(k_{i})=\mu(k_{H_{i}})
, and we assumed that

\mu(B_{i+1})
is less than the first term
\left(\mu(B_{i+1})\leq\sum_{H\in{\mathcal{H}}}\left|H\cap B_{i+1}\right|\min_{k\in H}\mu(k)\right)
. This completes the proof of (
4.8
) in view of (
4.10
).
To conclude, as
B_{r}\in\mathbb{I}({\mathbb{H}})
and in view of (
4.7
), we have that

\mu(B_{r})\leq\sum_{H\in{\mathcal{H}}}\left|H\cap B_{r}\right|\min_{k\in H}\mu(k)
, which implies by an immediate induction using (
4.8
), that

\mu(B)\leq\sum_{H\in{\mathcal{H}}}\left|H\cap B\right|\min_{k\in H}\mu(k).

This completes the proof.
∎

Remark 4.1
.

[Graphical case]
Let us consider the special case where
{\mathbb{H}}=({\mathcal{V}},{\mathcal{H}})
is a graph. Then, it is shown in Proposition 2 of
[
33
]
that the stability region of the model is included in the set

\textsc{Ncond}({\mathbb{H}})=\left\{\mu\in\mathscr{M}({\mathcal{V}})\,:\;\forall I\in\mathbb{I}({\mathbb{H}}),\,\mu(I)<\mu({\mathcal{E}}(I))\right\},

where for any set
B\subset{\mathcal{V}}
,
{\mathcal{E}}(B)=\left\{j\in{\mathcal{V}}\,:\,(i,j)\in{\mathcal{H}}\mbox{ for some }i\in B\right\}.

It is then easy to check by hand that

Ncond
({\mathbb{H}})
is included in
\mathscr{N}^{++}_{1}({\mathbb{H}})
. Indeed,
if we let
\mu\in\textsc{Ncond}({\mathbb{H}})
and
I\in\mathbb{I}({\mathbb{H}})
(meaning that
I
is an independent set of the graph

{\mathbb{H}}
, in the usual sense), then,
for any edge
H\in{\mathcal{H}}
,
|H\cap I|=1
if
I
contains a vertex of the edge
H
, and 0 else, so we get that

\sum_{H\in{\mathcal{H}}}\left|H\cap I\right|\min_{j\in H\cap\bar{I}}\mu(j)=\sum_{(i,j)\in{\mathcal{H}}\,:\,i\in I}\mu(j)\geq\sum_{j\in{\mathcal{E}}(I)}\mu(j)=\mu({\mathcal{E}}(I)),

where the inequality above is an equality whenever each element of
{\mathcal{E}}(I)
shares an edge with a single element of
I
, and else a strong inequality.
Thus
\mu\in\mathscr{N}_{1}^{++}({\mathbb{H}})
.
In fact, it is necessarily the case that
\textsc{Ncond}({\mathbb{H}})\subset\mathscr{N}_{1}({\mathbb{H}})
because if it was not true,
there would exist in particular a
\mu\in\overline{\mathscr{N}_{1}({\mathbb{H}})}\cap\textsc{Ncond}({\mathbb{H}})
, making
the system
({\mathbb{H}},\textsc{ml},\mu)
unstable (in view of Proposition
4.1
) despite the fact that

\mu\in\textsc{Ncond}({\mathbb{H}})
, a contradiction to Theorem 2 in
[
33
]
.
Observe however that
\textsc{Ncond}({\mathbb{H}})\neq\mathscr{N}_{1}({\mathbb{H}})
in general.
To see this, consider the case where
{\mathbb{H}}
is the cycle of size
5
,
1-2-3-4-5-1
.
For a small enough
\epsilon>0
, set

\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}\mu(1)&={1\over 2}-{3\epsilon\over 4};\\
\mu(2)=\mu(5)&={1\over 4}-{\epsilon\over 8};\\
\mu(3)&={4\epsilon\over 5};\\
\mu(4)&={\epsilon\over 5}.\end{array}\right.

It is then easily checked that
\mu\in\mathscr{N}_{1}({\mathbb{H}})
. However
\mu\not\in\textsc{Ncond}({\mathbb{H}})
, since the independent set
I=\{1,3\}
is such that

\mu(I)={1\over 2}+{\epsilon\over 20}>{1\over 2}-{\epsilon\over 20}=\mu(\{2,4,5\})=\mu({\mathcal{E}}(I)).

As a conclusion, if
{\mathbb{H}}
is a graph the necessary condition “
\mu\in\textsc{Ncond}({\mathbb{H}})
” is
stronger than
the necessary condition “
\mu\in\mathscr{N}_{1}({\mathbb{H}})
”.

Let us now define the following set of measures,

\mathscr{N}_{2}(\mathbb{H})=\left\{\mu\in\mathscr{M}({\mathcal{V}}):\,\quad\forall T\in{\mathcal{T}}(\mathbb{H})\;,\,\mu(T)\;>{1\over r({\mathbb{H}})}\right\}.

We also have that

Proposition 4.2
.

For any connected hypergraph
\mathbb{H}
and any admissible matching policy
\Phi
,

\textsc{Stab}({\mathbb{H}},\Phi)\subset\mathscr{N}_{2}(\mathbb{H}).

Proof.

Suppose that there exists a transversal
T\in{\mathcal{T}}({\mathbb{H}})
such that
\mu(T)\;\leq{1\over r({\mathbb{H}})}
.
As each match contains at least one element whose class is an element of
T
, at any time
the overall number of completed matches cannot exceed the number of arrivals of elements
whose class belongs to
T
,
in other words
M_{n}({\mathcal{H}})\leq A_{n}(T)
for all
n
. Thus, for all
n
we have that

{X_{n}({\mathcal{V}})\over n}\geq{1\over n}\left(A_{n}({\mathcal{V}})-r({\mathbb{H}})M_{n}({\mathcal{H}})\right)\geq{1\over n}\left(A_{n}({\mathcal{V}})-r({\mathbb{H}})A_{n}(T)\right).

Taking
n
to infinity in the above yields

\limsup_{n}{X_{n}({\mathcal{V}})\over n}\geq 1-r({\mathbb{H}})\mu(T),

and we conclude as in the previous proof.
∎

Remark 4.2
.

As an immediate consequence of Proposition
4.2
, if
{\mathbb{H}}=({\mathcal{V}},{\mathcal{H}})
is of order
q
,
and such that
\tau({\mathbb{H}})\leq{q\over r({\mathbb{H}})}
,
then
\textsc{Stab}({\mathbb{H}},\Phi)
does not contain the uniform measure
\mu_{\textsc{u}}=(1/q,...,1/q)
on
{\mathcal{V}}
, in other words the model
({\mathbb{H}},\Phi,\mu_{\textsc{u}})
is unstable
for any
\Phi
. Indeed, for any minimal transversal
T
of
{\mathbb{H}}
we have that

\mu_{\textsc{u}}(T)={\tau({\mathbb{H}})\over q}\leq{1\over r({\mathbb{H}})}.

We now introduce two necessary conditions of stability based on the anti-rank of the considered hypergraph.
We first introduce the following sets of measures,

\displaystyle\mathscr{N}^{\tiny{+}}_{3}(\mathbb{H})

\displaystyle=\left\{\mu\in\mathscr{M}({\mathcal{V}}):\;\forall i\in{\mathcal{V}},\,\mu(i)\leq{1\over a({\mathbb{H}})}\right\};

(4.11)

\displaystyle\mathscr{N}^{-}_{3}(\mathbb{H})

\displaystyle=\left\{\mu\in\mathscr{M}({\mathcal{V}}):\;\forall i\in{\mathcal{V}},\,\mu(i)<{1\over a({\mathbb{H}})}\right\}.

(4.12)

We have the following,

Proposition 4.3
.

For any connected hypergraph
\mathbb{H}=({\mathcal{V}},{\mathcal{H}})
and any admissible policy
\Phi
,

\textsc{Stab}(\mathbb{H},\Phi)\subset\mathscr{N}^{\tiny{+}}_{3}(\mathbb{H}).

(4.13)

If the hypergraph
\mathbb{H}=({\mathcal{V}},{\mathcal{H}})
is
r
-uniform (i.e.,
a({\mathbb{H}})=r({\mathbb{H}})=r
) we have that

\textsc{Stab}(\mathbb{H},\Phi)\subset\mathscr{N}^{-}_{3}(\mathbb{H}).

(4.14)

in other words the model
({\mathbb{H}},\Phi,\mu)
cannot be stable unless
\mu(i)<1/r
for any
i\in{\mathcal{V}}
.

Proof.

To prove the first statement, we argue again by contradiction. Suppose that

\mu(i_{0})>{1\over a({\mathbb{H}})}
for some node
i_{0}
.
As the function

\begin{cases}{\mathbb{R}}^{+}&\longrightarrow{\mathbb{R}}^{+}\\
x&\longmapsto{r({\mathbb{H}})-a({\mathbb{H}})+x\over xa({\mathbb{H}})}\end{cases}

strictly decreases to
{1\over a}
, there exists
x_{0}>0
such that

\mu(i_{0})>{r({\mathbb{H}})-a({\mathbb{H}})+x_{0}\over x_{0}a({\mathbb{H}})}.

(4.15)

Then, applying the inequality in (
4.1
) to
B\equiv{\mathcal{V}}\setminus\{i_{0}\}
, we readily obtain that a.s. for all
n
,

{r({\mathbb{H}})+x_{0}\over a({\mathbb{H}})}A_{n}\left({\mathcal{V}}\setminus\{i_{0}\}\right)\\
\begin{aligned} &\geq{r({\mathbb{H}})+x_{0}\over a({\mathbb{H}})}\left(\sum\limits_{H\in{\mathcal{H}}(i_{0})}\left|H-1\right|M_{n}\left(H\right)+\sum\limits_{H\in\overline{{\mathcal{H}}(i_{0})}}\left|H\right|M_{n}\left(H\right)\right)\\
&\geq\left(r({\mathbb{H}})+x_{0}-{r({\mathbb{H}})+x_{0}\over a({\mathbb{H}})}\right)M_{n}\left({\mathcal{H}}(i_{0})\right)+(r({\mathbb{H}})+x_{0})M_{n}\left(\overline{{\mathcal{H}}(i_{0})}\right).\end{aligned}

(4.16)

Likewise, applying the equality of (
4.1
) to
\{i_{0}\}
and then
{\mathcal{V}}\setminus\{i_{0}\}
also yields to

X_{n}\left({\mathcal{V}}\setminus\{i_{0}\}\right)+\left(x_{0}+1-{r({\mathbb{H}})+x_{0}\over a({\mathbb{H}})}\right)X_{n}(i_{0})\\
=A_{n}\left({\mathcal{V}}\setminus\{i_{0}\}\right)-\sum\limits_{H\in{\mathcal{H}}(i_{0})}\left|H-1\right|M_{n}\left(H\right)-\sum\limits_{H\in\overline{{\mathcal{H}}(i_{0})}}\left|H\right|M_{n}\left(H\right)\\
+\left(x_{0}+1-{r({\mathbb{H}})+x_{0}\over a({\mathbb{H}})}\right)\left(A_{n}(i_{0})-M_{n}\left({\mathcal{H}}(i_{0})\right)\right)\\
>A_{n}\left({\mathcal{V}}\setminus\{i_{0}\}\right)+\left(x_{0}+1-{r({\mathbb{H}})+x_{0}\over a({\mathbb{H}})}\right)A_{n}(i_{0})\\
-\left(r({\mathbb{H}})+x_{0}-{r({\mathbb{H}})+x_{0}\over a({\mathbb{H}})}\right)M_{n}({\mathcal{H}}(i_{0}))-(r({\mathbb{H}})+x_{0})M_{n}\left(\overline{{\mathcal{H}}(i_{0})}\right).

Combining this with (
4.16
), implies that a.s. for all
n
,

X_{n}\left({\mathcal{V}}\right)+\left(x_{0}-{r({\mathbb{H}})+x_{0}\over a({\mathbb{H}})}\right)X_{n}(i_{0})>\left(1-{r({\mathbb{H}})+x_{0}\over a({\mathbb{H}})}\right)A_{n}\left({\mathcal{V}}\right)+x_{0}A_{n}(i_{0}).

Therefore we have that

\limsup_{n}{1\over n}\left(X_{n}\left({\mathcal{V}}\right)+\left(x_{0}-{r({\mathbb{H}})+x_{0}\over a({\mathbb{H}})}\right)X_{n}(i_{0})\right)\geq 1-{r({\mathbb{H}})+x_{0}\over a({\mathbb{H}})}+x_{0}\mu(i_{0}),

(4.17)

hence the chain
\left\{W_{n};\,n\in{\mathbb{N}}\right\}
is transient since the right-hand side of the above is positive from (
4.15
).

It remains to check that in the case where the hypergraph is
r
-uniform, the model cannot be stable whenever

\mu(i_{0})\geq{1\over a({\mathbb{H}})}={1\over r}

for some
i_{0}\in{\mathcal{V}}
. For this, notice that, as
r({\mathbb{H}})=a({\mathbb{H}})=r
a weak inequality holds true in (
4.15
) for any
x_{0}>0
.
Then, it readily follows from (
4.17
) that for any
x_{0}
,

\limsup_{n}{1\over n}\left(X_{n}\left({\mathcal{V}}\right)+\left(x_{0}-{r({\mathbb{H}})+x_{0}\over a({\mathbb{H}})}\right)X_{n}(i_{0})\right)\geq 0,

and we conclude, as in the proof of Proposition
4.1
, that the chain
\left\{W_{n};\,n\in{\mathbb{N}}\right\}
is at best null recurrent.
∎

4.2
Non-stabilizable hypergraphs

Having Corollary
4.1
, and Propositions
4.1
,
4.2
and
4.3
in hand, one can identify classes of hypergraphs

{\mathbb{H}}
such that
({\mathbb{H}},\Phi,\mu)
has an empty stability region for any admissible
\Phi
.

We start with the following elementary observation,

Proposition 4.4
.

If a hyperedge of
{\mathbb{H}}=({\mathcal{V}},{\mathcal{H}})
contains two isolated nodes, i.e., there exist
H\in{\mathcal{H}}
and
i,j\in H
such that
d(i)=d(j)=1
, then
the model cannot be stable, i.e.,
\textsc{Stab}({\mathbb{H}},\Phi)=\emptyset
for any admissible
\Phi.

Proof.

Let
\mu\in\mathscr{N}^{\tiny{+}}_{1}({\mathbb{H}})
. Then, considering successively the sets
\{i\}
and
\{j\}
,
as
j\in H\cap\bar{\{i\}}
and
i\in H\cap\bar{\{j\}}
we obtain that
\mu(i)<\mu(j)\mbox{ and }\mu(i)>\mu(j),
an absurdity.
∎

\,i
j
H

Figure 4.1
:
Any hypergraph with two isolated nodes is non-stabilizable.

4.2.1
Stars

First recall that, as for any bipartite graph (see Theorem 2 in
[
33
]
), graphical matching models on
trees are always unstable. This is true in particular if the matching graph is a “star”, i.e., a connected graph in which all but one vertices are of degree one.
The following two results can be seen as generalizations of this fact to hypergraphical models,

Proposition 4.5
.

If an
r
-uniform hypergraph
{\mathbb{H}}=({\mathcal{V}},{\mathcal{H}})
has transversal number
\tau({\mathbb{H}})=1
, then it is non-stabilizable.

Proof.

Fix
\Phi
and
\mu
in
\textsc{Stab}({\mathbb{H}},\Phi)
. Let
T
be a transversal of cardinality
1
, i.e.,
T=\{i_{0}\}
, where the vertex
i_{0}
belongs
to all hyperedges in
{\mathcal{H}}
. Then from Proposition
4.3
, we have that
\mu(i_{0})<1/a({\mathbb{H}})=1/r
.
However, Proposition
4.2
implies that
\mu(i_{0})>1/r({\mathbb{H}})=1/r,
an absurdity.
∎

In other words, any uniform hypergraph whose hyperedges all contain the same node
i_{0}
cannot make the corresponding system stable. Moreover,

Proposition 4.6
.

Suppose that there exists a subset
B\subset{\mathcal{V}}
in the hypergraph
{\mathbb{H}}=({\mathcal{V}},{\mathcal{H}})
such that:

•

all hyperedges of
{\mathcal{H}}(B)
contain at least one node of degree 1;

•

at least one of these nodes of degree 1 lies outside of
B
.

Then
{\mathbb{H}}
is non-stabilizable.

Proof.

Let
k=|{\mathcal{H}}(B)|
, i.e., the number of hyperedges intersecting with
B
. Denote by
H_{1},...,H_{k}
these intersecting
hyperedges, and for any
l\in\llbracket 1,k\rrbracket
, by
i_{l}\in{\mathcal{V}}
, a node of degree one belonging to
H_{l}
.
Observe that the nodes
i_{1},...,i_{k}
are not necessarily distinct. On the one hand, for
any
l\in\llbracket 1,k\rrbracket
we have that

X_{n}(i_{l})=A_{n}(i_{l})-M_{n}(H_{l}).

Thus, applying again the inequality in (
4.1
) we get that for all
n
,

A_{n}(B)\geq\sum\limits_{l=1}^{k}|H_{l}\cap B|M_{n}(H_{l})=\sum\limits_{l=1}^{k}|H_{l}\cap B|(A_{n}(i_{l})-X_{n}(i_{l})).

This entails that if
\mu\in\mathscr{N}_{1}^{\tiny{++}}(\mathbb{H})
,

\limsup\limits_{n\to\infty}{1\over n}\sum\limits_{l=1}^{k}|H_{l}\cap B|X_{n}(i_{l})\geq\sum\limits_{l=1}^{k}|H_{l}\cap B|\mu(i_{l})-\mu(B)\geq 0.

If the above inequality is strong, then the chain
\left\{W_{n};\,n\in{\mathbb{N}}\right\}
is transient. If the inequality is weak, then as above we can stochastically lower-bound the chain by a zero-drift chain
\left\{\tilde{Y}_{n};\,n\in{\mathbb{N}}\right\}
, defined by

\tilde{Y}_{n}=\left(A_{n}(B)\;-\;\sum\limits_{l=1}^{k}|H_{l}\cap B|A_{n}(i_{l})\right),\quad n\in{\mathbb{N}},

which is not identically null from the assumption that at least one of the nodes
i_{l}
,
l=1,...,k
is not an element of
B,
which concludes the proof.
∎

Example 4.1
.

Any hypergraph
{\mathbb{H}}=({\mathcal{V}},{\mathcal{H}})
such that there exist two hyperedges
H_{1}
and
H_{2}

with
H_{1}\cap H_{2}\neq\emptyset
and two nodes
i_{1}\in H_{1}\cap\overline{H_{2}}
,
i_{2}\in H_{2}\cap\overline{H_{1}}
and

d\left(i_{1}\right)=d\left(i_{2}\right)=1
is non-stabilizable (see Figure
4.2
).
To see this, take
B=H_{1}\cap H_{2}
in Proposition
4.6
.

\;i_{1}
\,i_{2}
H_{2}
H_{1}

Figure 4.2
:
Two intersecting hyperedges containing each, an isolated node outside of their intersection, make the system unstable.

Remark 4.3

(About the DI condition in
[
21
]
)
.

Most results of
[
21
]
hold under the Assumption 1 therein, stating that the Dedicated Item DI condition is satisfied; namely, each hyperedge contains an isolated
node. The above example shows that any matching model
({\mathbb{H}},\Phi,\mu)
on a hypergraph
{\mathbb{H}}
satisfying the DI condition, is unstable
for any admissible
\Phi
(the case where
{\mathbb{H}}
contains a single hyperedge
H
is trivial).

4.2.2
r
-partite hypergraphs

We now turn to hypergraphical generalizations of bipartite graphs.

Proposition 4.7
.

Any
r
-uniform bipartite hypergraph
\mathbb{H}=({\mathcal{V}},{\mathcal{H}})
is non-stabilizable.

Proof.

Applying (
4.1
) successively to
V_{1}
and
V_{2}
readily implies that for all
n
,

X_{n}(V_{1})=A_{n}(V_{1})-M_{n}({\mathcal{H}})\geq 0\quad\mbox{ and }\quad X_{n}(V_{2})=A_{n}(V_{2})-(r-1)M_{n}({\mathcal{H}})\geq 0,

and thus

X_{n}(V_{1})\geq A_{n}(V_{1})-\displaystyle\frac{1}{r-1}A_{n}(V_{2}).

Then, the usual SLLN-based argument implies that the model cannot be stable unless
\mu(V_{2})\geq(r-1)\mu(V_{1})
.
But as
\mu(V_{1})+\mu(V_{2})=1
we have that
\mu(V_{1})\leq\displaystyle\frac{1}{r}
,
hence
\mu\not\in\mathscr{N}_{2}({\mathbb{H}})
since
V_{1}
is a transversal.
∎

1
2
4
6
5
3
7

Figure 4.3
:
The Fano plane minus the hyperedge
\{4,5,7\}
.

Example 4.2
.

The so-called Fano plane is a well-known object in discrete geometry. It is the smallest
projective plane
, namely, the smallest set of
points
and
lines
such that
any two points share a line, any two lines intersect at a single point, and on every line lies the same number of points. In the settings of hypergraphs (points being nodes and
lines being hyperedges), the Fano plane is thus the smallest uniform hypergraph
{\mathbb{H}}
in which each pair of nodes belongs to a single hyperedge, and each pair of hyperedges intersects at a single node. It can be checked that
{\mathbb{H}}=({\mathcal{V}},{\mathcal{H}})
is of order 7, for
{\mathcal{V}}=\llbracket 1,7\rrbracket
and e.g.

{\mathcal{H}}=\left\{\{1,2,4\},\{1,5,6\},\{1,3,7\},\{2,3,5\},\{4,5,7\},\{4,3,6\},\{6,2,7\}\right\}.

Supported by simulations, we conjecture that Fano planes are stabilizable. However,
if
\mathbb{H}^{\prime}=({\mathcal{V}},{\mathcal{H}}^{\prime})
is the subhypergraph defined by
{\mathcal{H}}^{\prime}={\mathcal{H}}\backslash H
, where
H
is an arbitrary hyperedge of
{\mathcal{H}}
, then
it is easily seen that
\mathbb{H}^{\prime}
is a 3-uniform bipartite hypergraph with
V_{1}=H
and
V_{2}={\mathcal{V}}\backslash H
. So we deduce from
Proposition
4.7
that
\mathbb{H}^{\prime}
is non-stabilizable. A Fano plane minus the hyperedge

\{4,5,7\}
is represented in Figure
4.3
.

We know from Theorem 2 in
[
33
]
that
bipartite graphs are not stabilizable. The next result shows that this can be generalized to
r
-partite hypergraphs (which generalize bipartite graphs - see Remark
1.2
),

Proposition 4.8
.

Any
r
-partite hypergraph
{\mathbb{H}}
is non-stabilizable.

Proof.

As in the above proof we get that for any
i\neq j
and any
n
,

X_{n}(V_{j})=A_{n}(V_{j})-M_{n}({\mathcal{H}})\geq 0\quad\mbox{ and }\quad X_{n}(V_{i})=A_{n}(V_{i})-M_{n}({\mathcal{H}})\geq 0,

implying that
X_{n}(V_{i})\geq A_{n}(V_{i})-A_{n}(V_{j})
, and in turn, that the model cannot be stable unless
\mu(V_{i})\leq\mu(V_{j})
.
By symmetry, this implies that
\mu(V_{i})=\mu(V_{j})
. As the
V_{i}
’s are disjoint, we thus have that
\mu(V_{i})=1/r
for all
i
.
Thus, as any
V_{i}
is a transversal of
{\mathbb{H}}
,
\mu
is not an element of
\mathscr{N}_{2}({\mathbb{H}})
. ∎

It is well known (see
[
25
]
for the particular case of graphs, and the general result in
[
24
]
) that Hall’s condition is necessary and sufficient for the existence of a perfect matching on
{\mathbb{H}}
, i.e., a spanning subhypergraph of
{\mathbb{H}}
in which all nodes have degree 1, in the case where the hypergraph is balanced, i.e., it does not contain any odd
strong cycle. It is intuitively clear that the construction of stable stochastic matching models on hypergraphs is somewhat reminiscent of that of perfect matchings on a growing hypergraph that replicates the matching hypergraph a large number of times in the long run (in the case of graphs, see the discussion in Section 7 of
[
38
]
). This connexion has a simple illustration in the next Proposition, which provides a family of probability measures, naturally including the uniform measure on
{\mathcal{V}}
, that cannot stabilize a matching model on the hypergraph
{\mathbb{H}}
unless the latter satisfies Hall’s condition. In what follows we denote for any
{\mathbb{H}}=({\mathcal{V}},{\mathcal{H}})
and any measure
\mu\in\mathscr{M}({\mathcal{V}})
,

\mu_{\mbox{\scriptsize{min}}}=\min\left\{\mu(i)\,:\,i\in{\mathcal{V}}\right\}\quad\mbox{ and }\quad\mu_{\mbox{\scriptsize{max}}}=\max\left\{\mu(i)\,:\,i\in{\mathcal{V}}\right\}.

(4.18)

Proposition 4.9
.

For any hypergraph
\mathbb{H}=({\mathcal{V}},{\mathcal{H}})
that violates Hall’s condition,
any matching policy
\Phi
and any
\mu\in\mathscr{M}({\mathcal{V}})
such that

{\mu_{\mbox{\emph{\scriptsize{min}}}}\over\mu_{\mbox{\emph{\scriptsize{max}}}}}>{\left\lfloor{q({\mathbb{H}})+1\over 2}\right\rfloor-1\over\left\lfloor{q({\mathbb{H}})+1\over 2}\right\rfloor},

(4.19)

the model
({\mathbb{H}},\Phi,\mu)
is unstable. In particular,
({\mathbb{H}},\Phi,\mu_{\textsc{u}})
is unstable for
\mu_{\textsc{u}}
the uniform distribution on
{\mathcal{V}}
.

Proof.

Fix
{\mathbb{H}}
,
\Phi
, and a measure
\mu
satisfying (
4.19
). We first show that
\mu
is monotonic with respect to the counting measure on
{\mathcal{V}}
, i.e.,

\forall E,F\subset{\mathcal{V}},\quad|E|<|F|\Longrightarrow\mu(E)<\mu(F).

(4.20)

Let
E
and
F
be such that
|E|<|F|
, and let
k=|F|
. Let also
\alpha
be a bijection from
\llbracket 1,q({\mathbb{H}})\rrbracket
to
{\mathcal{V}}
such that

\mu_{\mbox{\scriptsize{min}}}=\mu(\alpha(1))\leq\mu(\alpha(2))\leq...\leq\mu(\alpha(q({\mathbb{H}})))=\mu_{\mbox{\scriptsize{max}}},

(4.21)

in other words

\left(\mu(\alpha(1)),\mu(\alpha(2)),...,\mu(\alpha(q({\mathbb{H}})))\right)
is an ordered (in increasing order) version of the family
\left\{\mu(i);\,i\in{\mathcal{V}}\right\}
.
As
|E|\leq k-1
we clearly have

\mu(F)-\mu(E)\geq\sum_{i=1}^{k}\mu(\alpha(i))-\sum_{i=q-k+2}^{q}\mu(\alpha(i)).

(4.22)

First, if
k\leq\left\lfloor{q({\mathbb{H}})+1\over 2}\right\rfloor
, (
4.19
) entails that
k\mu_{\mbox{\scriptsize{min}}}>(k-1)\mu_{\mbox{\scriptsize{max}}},
whence

\sum_{i=1}^{k}\mu(\alpha(i))-\sum_{i=q-k+2}^{q}\mu(\alpha(i))\geq k\mu_{\mbox{\scriptsize{min}}}-(k-1)\mu_{\mbox{\scriptsize{max}}}>0,

(4.23)

If
k>\left\lfloor{q({\mathbb{H}})+1\over 2}\right\rfloor
, then the index sets
\llbracket 1,k\rrbracket
and
\llbracket q-k+2,q\rrbracket
intersect precisely
on
\llbracket q-k+2,k\rrbracket
. Thus

\displaystyle\sum_{i=1}^{k}\mu(\alpha(i))-\sum_{i=q-k+2}^{q}\mu(\alpha(i))

\displaystyle=\sum_{i=1}^{q-k+1}\mu(\alpha(i))-\sum_{i=k+1}^{q}\mu(\alpha(i))

\displaystyle\geq(q-k+1)\mu_{\mbox{\scriptsize{min}}}-(q-k)\mu_{\mbox{\scriptsize{max}}}>0,

(4.24)

where the last inequality follows, as in (
4.23
), from the fact that
q-k+1\leq\left\lfloor{q({\mathbb{H}})+1\over 2}\right\rfloor
.
Gathering (
4.22
) with (
4.23
-
4.24
) concludes the proof of (
4.20
) in all cases.

Now fix
V_{2}
and
V_{1}
such that
|H\cap V_{2}|\geq|H\cap V_{1}|
for any
H\in{\mathcal{H}}
, and
|V_{2}|<|V_{1}|
which from (
4.20
), implies that

\mu(V_{2})<\mu(V_{1})
. Then,
applying again (
4.1
) to
V_{2}
and
V_{1}
we get that

\displaystyle X_{n}(V_{2})+X_{n}(V_{1})

\displaystyle\geq A_{n}(V_{2})+A_{n}(V_{1})-2\sum\limits_{H\in{\mathcal{H}}}\left|H\cap V_{2}\right|M_{n}(H)

\displaystyle\geq A_{n}(V_{2})+A_{n}(V_{1})-2A_{n}(V_{2}),

thus, from the usual argument, the model cannot be stable unless
\mu(V_{2})\geq\mu(V_{1})
, a contradiction.
∎

4.2.3
Cycles

Proposition 4.10
.

Any
r
-uniform
\ell
-cycle of order
q
such that
r
divides
q
, is non-stabilizable.

Proof.

The partition
V_{1},V_{2},\cdots,V_{r}
of
{\mathcal{V}}
defined by

V_{i}=\left\{v_{i+(j-1)r}\;;\,j\in\llbracket 1,q/r\rrbracket\right\},

satisfies Proposition
4.8
. ∎

Figure
1.6
shows a
3
-uniform
2
-cycle of order
12
and
3
-uniform
2
-cycle of order
6
.

4.3
Stable systems

We show hereafter that stable matching models on hypergraphs exist. With a view to showing how stability can be shown in concrete examples,
we provide hereafter two case studies of simple hypergraphs, on
which a stable stochastic matching model can be defined: complete
3
-uniform hypergraphs, and subhypergraphs of the latter where several hyperedges are erased.

4.3.1
Complete
3
-uniform hypergraphs

We first consider the case of a complete
3
-uniform hypergraph
{\mathbb{H}}
, an example of which for
q({\mathbb{H}})=4
is represented in Figure
1.4
(left).
We show that, in this case, the necessary condition given in Proposition

4.3
is also sufficient,

Theorem 4.1
.

Let
{\mathbb{H}}=({\mathcal{V}},{\mathcal{H}})
be a complete
3
-uniform hypergraph of order
q({\mathbb{H}})\geq 4
.
Then, for any admissible policy
\Phi
we have,

\textsc{Stab}({\mathbb{H}},\Phi)=\mathscr{N}_{3}^{\tiny{-}}({\mathbb{H}}),

that is, the model
({\mathbb{H}},\Phi,\mu)
is stable if and only if
\mu(i)<1/3
for any
i\in{\mathcal{V}}
.

Proof.

Necessity of the condition being shown in Proposition
4.3
, only the sufficiency remains to be proven.
Suppose that
\mu(i)<1/3
for any
i\in{\mathcal{V}}
, and fix
\alpha
such that
\max_{i\in{\mathcal{V}}}\mu(i)<\alpha<1/3
.
Define the planar Markov chain
\left\{U^{\alpha}_{n};\,n\in{\mathbb{N}}\right\}
having the following transitions on
{\mathbb{N}}^{2}
,

\left\{\begin{array}[]{llll}\mbox{First axis:}&P^{\alpha}_{(x,0),(x+1,0)}&=\alpha,&\,x\in{\mathbb{N}}^{+},\\
&P^{\alpha}_{(x,0),(x,1)}&=1-\alpha,&\,x\in{\mathbb{N}}^{+},\\
\mbox{Second axis:}&P^{\alpha}_{(0,y),(0,y+1)}&=\alpha,&\,y\in{\mathbb{N}}^{+},\\
&P^{\alpha}_{(0,y),(1,y)}&=1-\alpha,&\,y\in{\mathbb{N}}^{+},\\
\mbox{Interior:}&P^{\alpha}_{(x,y),(x+1,y)}&=\alpha,&\,x,y\in{\mathbb{N}}^{+},\\
&P^{\alpha}_{(x,y),(x,y+1)}&=\alpha,&\,x,y\in{\mathbb{N}}^{+},\\
&P^{\alpha}_{(x,y),(x-1,y-1)}&=1-2\alpha,&\,x,y\in{\mathbb{N}}^{+},\end{array}\right.

and arbitrary transitions from
(0,0)
to any element of
{\mathbb{N}}^{2}
. (These transitions are represented in Figure
4.4
below).

\alpha
1-\alpha
\alpha
\alpha
1-2\alpha
1-\alpha
\alpha

Figure 4.4
:
Auxiliary Markov chain of the complete 3-uniform hypergraph.

Denote by
\Delta=(\Delta_{x},\Delta_{y})
,
\Delta^{\prime}=(\Delta^{\prime}_{x},\Delta^{\prime}_{y})
and
\Delta^{\prime\prime}=(\Delta^{\prime\prime}_{x},\Delta^{\prime\prime}_{y})
, the mean (horizontal and vertical)
drifts of the chain
\left\{U^{\alpha}_{n};\,n\in{\mathbb{N}}\right\}
, respectively on the interior, on the first and on the second axis, in a way that

\left\{\begin{array}[]{lll}\mbox{First axis:}&\Delta^{\prime}_{x}=\alpha,&\Delta^{\prime}_{y}=1-\alpha;\\
\mbox{Second axis:}&\Delta^{\prime\prime}_{x}=1-\alpha,&\Delta^{\prime\prime}_{y}=\alpha;\\
\mbox{Interior:}&\Delta_{x}=3\alpha-1,&\Delta_{y}=3\alpha-1.\end{array}\right.

Thus,
\Delta_{x}<0
and
\Delta_{y}<0
. Also, we have that

\Delta_{x}\Delta^{\prime}_{y}-\Delta_{y}\Delta^{\prime}_{x}=\Delta_{x}\Delta^{\prime\prime}_{y}-\Delta_{y}\Delta^{\prime\prime}_{x}=(3\alpha-1)(1-2\alpha)<0,

so we can apply Theorem
1.2
, part (a), to claim that the Markov chain
\{U^{\alpha}_{n}\}
is positive recurrent.
Specifically, it can be checked that, setting
u={1-3\alpha\over 2}>0
, for any
w
such that
{{3\alpha-1}}<w<{(3\alpha-1)\alpha\over 1-\alpha}<0
we have that

\begin{cases}2u\Delta_{x}+w\Delta_{y}&<0,\\
2u\Delta_{y}+w\Delta_{x}&<0,\\
2u\Delta^{\prime}_{x}+w\Delta^{\prime}_{y}&<0,\\
2u\Delta^{\prime\prime}_{y}+w\Delta_{x}&<0.\end{cases}

(4.25)

Second, as
4u^{2}>w^{2}
the quadratic form
Q:(x,y)\mapsto ux^{2}+uy^{2}+wxy
is positive definite.
Then, in view of Lemma
1.1
, it follows from (
4.25
) that, defining the mapping

L^{\alpha}:\begin{cases}{\mathbb{N}}^{2}&\longrightarrow{\mathbb{R}}_{+}\\
(x,y)&\longmapsto\sqrt{Q(x,y)}=\sqrt{ux^{2}+uy^{2}+wxy},\end{cases}

we have that for some compact set
\mathcal{K}^{\alpha}\subset{\mathbb{N}}^{2}
, for any
(x,y)\in\overline{\mathcal{K}^{\alpha}}
,

{\mathbb{E}}\left[L^{\alpha}\left(U^{\alpha}_{n+1}\right)-L^{\alpha}(U^{\alpha}_{n})\mid U^{\alpha}_{n}=(x,y)\right]<0.

(4.26)

Now, as
{\mathbb{H}}
is complete
3
-uniform, the states of the Markov chain
\left\{X_{n};\,n\in{\mathbb{N}}\right\}
have at most two non-zero coordinates,
in other words, its state space is

\mathcal{E}=\Bigl\{\textbf{x}=(x_{1},...,x_{q})\in{\mathbb{N}}^{q}\,:\,x_{i}x_{j}x_{k}=0\mbox{ for any distinct }i,j,k\in\llbracket 1,q\rrbracket\Bigl\}.

Define the mapping

L:\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}\mathcal{E}&\longrightarrow{\mathbb{R}}_{+}\\
\textbf{x}&\longmapsto\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}0&\mbox{ if }\textbf{x}=\mathbf{0},\\
L^{\alpha}((x,0))&\mbox{ if $\textbf{x}=x.\textbf{e}_{i}$, for some $x>0$, $i\in{\mathcal{V}}$},\\
L^{\alpha}((x,y))&\mbox{ if $\textbf{x}=x.\textbf{e}_{i}+y.\textbf{e}_{j}$, for some $x,y>0$, $i\neq j$,}\end{array}\right.\end{array}\right.

where the above definition is unambiguous due to the fact that
L^{\alpha}
is a symmetric form on
{\mathbb{N}}^{2}
.
Also define the compact set

\mathcal{K}=\left\{\textbf{x}:=x.\textbf{e}_{i}+y.\textbf{e}_{j}\in\mathcal{E}\,:\,(x,y)\in\mathcal{K}^{\alpha}\right\}.

Then, first, if
\textbf{x}\in\bar{\mathcal{K}}\cap\mathcal{E}
is such that
\textbf{x}=x.\textbf{e}_{i}+y.\textbf{e}_{j}
for some
x,y>0
and
i,j\in{\mathcal{V}}
,
i\neq j
, we get that

{\mathbb{E}}\left[L\left(X_{n+1}\right)-L(X_{n})\mid X_{n}=\textbf{x}\right]\\
=(1-\mu(i)-\mu(j))\left(L\left(\textbf{x}-\textbf{e}_{i}-\textbf{e}_{j}\right)-L(\textbf{x})\right)\\
+\mu(i)\left(L\left(\textbf{x}+\textbf{e}_{i}\right)-L(\textbf{x})\right)+\mu(j)\left(L\left(\textbf{x}+\textbf{e}_{j}\right)-L(\textbf{x})\right)\\
=(1-\mu(i)-\mu(j))\left(L^{\alpha}\left(x-1,y-1\right)-L^{\alpha}(x,y)\right)\\
+\mu(i)\left(L^{\alpha}\left(x+1,y\right)-L^{\alpha}(x,y)\right)+\mu(j)\left(L^{\alpha}\left(x,y+1\right)-L^{\alpha}(x,y)\right)\\
{<(1-2\alpha)}\left(L^{\alpha}\left(x-1,y-1\right)-L^{\alpha}(x,y)\right)\\
+\alpha\left(L^{\alpha}\left(x+1,y\right)-L^{\alpha}(x,y)\right)+\alpha\left(L^{\alpha}\left(x,y+1\right)-L^{\alpha}(x,y)\right)\\
={\mathbb{E}}\left[L^{\alpha}\left(U^{\alpha}_{n+1}\right)-L^{\alpha}(U^{\alpha}_{n})\mid U^{\alpha}_{n}=(x,y)\right],

where, in the inequality above, we used the facts that
L^{\alpha}
is non-decreasing in its first and second variables, and such that

L^{\alpha}\left(x-1,y-1\right){<}\,L^{\alpha}(x,y)
.
Likewise, if
\textbf{x}\in\bar{\mathcal{K}}\cap\mathcal{E}
is such that
\textbf{x}=x.\textbf{e}_{i}
for some
x>0
and
i\in{\mathcal{V}}
, we have that

{\mathbb{E}}\left[L\left(X_{n+1}\right)-L(X_{n})\mid X_{n}=\textbf{x}\right]\\
\begin{aligned} &=\sum_{j\neq i}\mu(j)\left(L\left(\textbf{x}+\textbf{e}_{j}\right)-L(\textbf{x})\right)+\mu(i)\left(L\left(\textbf{x}+\textbf{e}_{i}\right)-L(\textbf{x})\right)\\
&=(1-\mu(i))\left(L^{\alpha}\left(x,1\right)-L^{\alpha}(x,0)\right)+\mu(i)\left(L^{\alpha}\left(x+1,0\right)-L^{\alpha}(x,0)\right)\\
&{<(1-\alpha)}\left(L^{\alpha}\left(x,1\right)-L^{\alpha}(x,0)\right)+\alpha\left(L^{\alpha}\left(x+1,0\right)-L^{\alpha}(x,0)\right)\\
&={\mathbb{E}}\left[L^{\alpha}\left(U^{\alpha}_{n+1}\right)-L^{\alpha}(U^{\alpha}_{n})\mid U^{\alpha}_{n}=(x,0)\right],\end{aligned}

remarking that
L^{\alpha}(x,1){<}\,L^{\alpha}(x,0)
. Recalling that
X_{n}=[W_{n}]
for all
n
,
using (
4.26
) in both cases, we conclude using the Lyapunov-Foster Theorem
1.1
that the chain
\left\{W_{n};\,n\in{\mathbb{N}}\right\}
is positive recurrent.
∎

The complete 3-uniform
k
-partite hypergraphs generalize the complete
k
-partite graphs introduced in p.4 of
[
36
]
, also called
separable
graphs in

[
33
]
and
[
38
]
- or
blow-ups
of the complete graph of order
k
in some other references. Roughly speaking, a complete
3
-uniform
k
-partite hypergraph is a version of the complete
3
-uniform hypergraph of order
k
, in which the
k
nodes are replicated into several replicas, each of the
k
sets of replicas forming an independent set
I_{i}
, such that all replicas of the same set do not share any
hyperedge with each other, but all share hyperedges of size 3 with all other pairs of replicas belonging to two different other sets of replicas.
Observe that in the particular case where all the sets
I_{1},...,I_{k}
are of cardinality 1 (i.e., there are no replica), the complete 3-uniform
k
-partite hypergraph is just the complete 3-uniform hypergraph of order
k
. We can then easily generalize the latter result,

Corollary 4.2
.

For
k\geq 4
, let
\tilde{\mathbb{H}}
be a complete
3
-uniform
k
-partite hypergraph, and let
I_{1},...,I_{k}
be the corresponding partition into independent sets.
Then, for any admissible policy
\Phi
, the model
(\tilde{\mathbb{H}},\tilde{\Phi},\tilde{\mu})
is stable if and only if
\tilde{\mu}(I_{i})<1/3
for any
i\in\llbracket 1,k\rrbracket
.

Proof.

The system has macroscopically (i.e., if we do not distinguish between items of classes that belong to the same independent set of the partition
I_{1},...,I_{k}
) the same
behavior as the complete
3
-uniform hypergraph. Specifically, let
q
be the order of the hypergraph
\tilde{\mathbb{H}}
, and define the mapping

\Psi:\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}{\mathbb{N}}^{q}&\longrightarrow{\mathbb{N}}^{k}\\
\tilde{\textbf{x}}=(\tilde{x}_{1},...,\tilde{x}_{q})&\longmapsto\textbf{x}=(x_{1},...,x_{k}):\,\forall i\in\llbracket 1,k\rrbracket,x_{i}=\sum_{j\in\llbracket 1,q\rrbracket;\,j\in I_{i}}\tilde{x}_{i}.\end{array}\right.

In words,
\Psi
maps the detailed class content of the model, onto a class content where one puts altogether all the elements of classes belonging to the same
independent set of the partition
I_{1},...,I_{k}
. Take
L
as the Lyapunov function introduced in the previous proof. Fix an admissible policy
\tilde{\Phi}
and a probability measure

\tilde{\mu}\in\mathscr{M}(\tilde{\mathcal{V}})
, and let
\left\{\tilde{X}_{n};\,n\in{\mathbb{N}}\right\}
be the class-content process of the model
(\tilde{\mathbb{H}},\tilde{\Phi},\tilde{\mu})
. On another hand, let
\left\{X_{n};\,n\in{\mathbb{N}}\right\}

be the class-content process of the model
({\mathbb{H}},\Phi,\mu)
defined on
{\mathbb{H}}=({\mathcal{V}},{\mathcal{H}})
the complete 3-uniform hypergraph
of order
k
, for an arbitrary matching policy
\Phi
and a probability measure
\mu\in\mathscr{M}({\mathcal{V}})
such that
\mu(i)=\tilde{\mu}(I_{i})
for any
i\in\llbracket 1,k\rrbracket
.
Then, it is easily seen that
\left\{\tilde{X}_{n};\,n\in{\mathbb{N}}\right\}
and
\left\{X_{n};\,n\in{\mathbb{N}}\right\}
are connected by the following relation: for all
n
and all
\tilde{\textbf{x}}\in{\mathbb{N}}^{q}
,

{\mathbb{E}}\left[L\circ\Psi\left(\tilde{X}_{n+1}\right)-L\circ\Psi(\tilde{X}_{n})\mid\tilde{X}_{n}=\tilde{\textbf{x}}\right]={\mathbb{E}}\left[L\left(X_{n+1}\right)-L(X_{n})\mid X_{n}=\Psi(\tilde{\textbf{x}})\right],

and the argument in the proof of Theorem
4.1
shows that the Markov chain
\left\{\tilde{X}_{n};\,n\in{\mathbb{N}}\right\}
is positive recurrent
whenever
\tilde{\mu}(i)<1/3
, that is,
\mu(I_{i})<1/3
, for all

i\in\llbracket 1,k\rrbracket
. This concludes the proof.
∎

4.3.2
Incomplete
3
-uniform hypergraphs

As is shown in Theorem
4.1
and Corollary
4.2
, complete
3
-uniform hypergraphs and complete
3
-uniform
k
-partite hypergraphs are stabilizable for all matching policy
\Phi,
for a large class of measures.
We show hereafter that incomplete hypergraphs (in the sense defined hereafter) can also be stabilizable for a matching policy
ml
,

Theorem 4.2
.

Let
\mathbb{H}=({\mathcal{V}},{\mathcal{H}})
be a complete
3
-uniform hypergraph of order
q\geq 5
, and let

{\mathbb{H}}^{\prime}=({\mathcal{V}},{\mathcal{H}}^{\prime})
be
the
(3\textrm{-uniform})
subhypergraph of
{\mathbb{H}}
obtained by setting
{\mathcal{H}}^{\prime}={\mathcal{H}}\backslash{\mathcal{J}}
, where
{\mathcal{J}}
is a subset of
{\mathcal{H}}
containing disjoint hyperedges. Let
J
be the union of the elements of
{\mathcal{J}}
.
Then the model
({\mathbb{H}}^{\prime},\textsc{ml},\mu)
is stable for any

\mu
in

\mathscr{S}({\mathbb{H}}^{\prime})=\biggl\{\mu\in\mathscr{M}({\mathcal{V}}):\left(\max\limits_{i\in J}\lambda_{i}(\mu)\vee\max\limits_{i\in\bar{J}}\nu_{i}(\mu)\right)<0\biggl\}\,\cap\,\mathscr{N}_{2}(\mathbb{H}^{\prime})\,\cap\mathscr{N}^{\scriptsize{-}}_{3}(\mathbb{H}^{\prime}),

where the
\lambda_{i}(\mu):i\in J
and
\nu_{i}(\mu):i\in\bar{J}
are defined respectively by (
4.28
) and (
4.29
).

Proof.

Fix
\Phi=\textsc{ml}
, and let
\mu\in{\mathscr{S}({\mathbb{H}}^{\prime})}
. For such
{\mathbb{H}}^{\prime}
the study of
\left\{Y_{n};\,n\in{\mathbb{N}}\right\}
does not boil down to that of a planar Markov chain.
Instead, we study the embedded chain
\left\{Y_{n};\,n\in{\mathbb{N}}\right\}=\left\{X_{4n};\,n\in{\mathbb{N}}\right\}
,
and consider the following quadratic Lyapunov function,

Q:\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}{\mathbb{N}}^{q}&\longrightarrow{\mathbb{R}}_{+}\\
\textbf{x}&\longmapsto\sum_{i=1}^{q}(x_{i})^{2}.\end{array}\right.

Fix
n\in{\mathbb{N}}
. We have the following alternatives given the value of the embedded chain
\left\{Y_{n};\,n\in{\mathbb{N}}\right\}
at time
n
,

(i) First, for any
i\in\overline{J}
, and any integer
x_{i}\geq{2}
. It follows that
for any
j\neq k\neq\ell\neq m\neq i\in{\mathcal{V}},
the chain
\left\{Y_{n};\,n\in{\mathbb{N}}\right\}
makes the transitions that we will present in Appendix (
7.10
) from state
Y_{n}=x_{i}.\textbf{e}_{i}
, then we deduce that

\Delta_{i}:={\mathbb{E}}\left[Q\left(Y_{n+1}\right)-Q\left(Y_{n}\right)|Y_{n}=x_{i}.\textbf{e}_{i}\right]\\
\begin{aligned} =&\,(8x_{i}+16)\mu(i)^{4}+4(6x_{i}+10)\mu(i)^{3}\sum_{j}\mu(j)+6(4x_{i}+8)\mu(i)^{2}\sum_{j}\mu(j)^{2}\\
&+4(2x_{i}+10)\mu(i)\sum_{j}\mu(j)^{3}+16\sum_{j}\mu(j)^{4}+12(2x_{i}+1)\mu(i)^{2}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}j,k\end{subarray}}\mu(j)\mu(k)\\
&+12\mu(i)\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}j,k\end{subarray}}\mu(j)^{2}\mu(k)-10(4x_{i}+4)\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}j,k,\ell\end{subarray}}\mu(j)^{2}\mu(k)\mu(\ell)\\
&-2(2x_{i}+5)\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}j,k,\ell\end{subarray}}\mu(j)^{2}\mu(k)\mu(\ell)-6(4x_{i}+4)\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}j,k\end{subarray}}\mu(j)^{2}\mu(k)^{2}\\
&-4(2x_{i}+5)\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}j,k\end{subarray}}\mu(j)^{3}\mu(k)+24\mu(i)\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}j,k\end{subarray}}\mu(j)\mu(k)\mu(\ell)\\
&-24(4x_{i}+4)\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}j,k,\ell,m\end{subarray}}\mu(j)\mu(k)\mu(\ell)\mu(m)=\lambda_{i}(\mu)x_{i}+\beta_{i}(\mu),\end{aligned}

(4.27)

for some bounded
\beta_{i}(\mu)
, and for

\lambda_{i}(\mu)=\;8\mu^{4}(i)+24\mu^{3}(i)\sum_{j\neq i}\mu(j)+24\mu^{2}(i)\sum_{j\neq i}\mu^{2}(j)\\
+8\mu(i)\sum_{j\neq i}\mu^{3}(j)+24\mu^{2}(i)\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}j,k\neq i\end{subarray}}\mu(j)\mu(k)-44\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}j,k,\ell\neq i\end{subarray}}\mu^{2}(j)\mu(k)\mu(\ell)\\
-24\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}j,k\neq i\end{subarray}}\mu^{2}(j)\mu^{2}(k)-8\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}j,k\neq i\end{subarray}}\mu(j)\mu^{3}(k)-96\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}j,k,\ell,m\neq i\end{subarray}}\mu(j)\mu(k)\mu(\ell)\mu(m).

(4.28)

Consequently, as the above is negative, there exists
a_{1}^{*}
such that
\Delta_{i}<0
whenever
x_{i}\geq a_{1}^{*}
.

(ii) For any
i\in J
, and any integer
x_{i}\geq{2}
, the transitions of
\left\{Y_{n};\,n\in{\mathbb{N}}\right\}
from the state
x_{i}.\textbf{e}_{i}
can be retrieved in a similar fashion to (
7.10
). It follows that
for any
j\neq k\neq\ell\neq m\neq p\neq s\neq i\in{\mathcal{V}}
. Set
H=\{i,j,k\}\subset J
, the transitions that will present in the Appendix (
7.11
), then we deduce that
\Delta_{i}^{\prime}=\nu_{i}(\mu)x_{i}+\beta^{\prime}_{i}(\mu)
for some bounded

(see Appendix (
7.12
)), and setting
H=\{i,j,k\}
as the only element of
{\mathcal{J}}
such that
i\in H
, we obtain that

\nu_{i}(\mu)=8\mu^{4}(i)+24\mu^{3}(i)\sum_{\ell\in{\mathcal{V}}\backslash\{i\}}\mu(\ell)+24\mu^{2}(i)\sum_{\ell\in{\mathcal{V}}\backslash\{i\}}\mu^{2}(\ell)+8\mu(i)\sum_{\ell\in{\mathcal{V}}\backslash\{i\}}\mu^{3}(\ell)\\
-8\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\ell\in\overline{H}\end{subarray}}\mu(j)\mu^{3}(\ell)-4\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\ell\in\overline{H}:\\
\textrm{ends with }kk\end{subarray}}\mu(j)\mu^{2}(k)\mu(\ell)-20\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\ell\in\overline{H}:\\
\textrm{otherwise}\end{subarray}}\mu(j)\mu^{2}(k)\mu(\ell)\qquad\qquad\qquad\;\;\\
-48\mu(j)\mu(k)\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\ell\in\overline{H}\end{subarray}}\mu^{2}(\ell)-4\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\ell\in\overline{H}:\\

-8\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\ell\in\overline{H}\end{subarray}}\mu^{3}(j)\mu(\ell)+24\mu(i)\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}j,k\in H\end{subarray}}\mu(j)\mu^{2}(k)-8\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\ell,m\in\overline{H}\end{subarray}}\mu^{3}(\ell)\mu(m)-24\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\ell,m\in\overline{H}\end{subarray}}\mu^{2}(\ell)\mu^{2}(m)\;\;\;\\

(4.29)

Thus, there exists
a_{2}^{*}
such that
\Delta^{\prime}_{i}<0
whenever
x_{i}\geq a_{2}^{*}
.

(iii) For any
i\neq j
such that
\{i,j\}
is not included in a hyperedge of the family
\mathcal{J}
,
for any integers

, we obtain that

\Delta_{ij}:={\mathbb{E}}\left[Q\left(X_{n+1}\right)-Q\left(X_{n}\right)|X_{n}=x_{i}.\textbf{e}_{i}+x_{j}.\textbf{e}_{j}\right]=\lambda_{ij}(\mu)x_{i}+\lambda_{ji}(\mu)x_{j}+\beta_{ij}(\mu),

for a bounded
\beta_{ij}(\mu)
, and for

\lambda_{ij}(\mu)=2\Big(\mu(i)-\sum\limits_{\ell\in{\mathcal{V}}\backslash\{i,j\}}\mu(\ell)\Big)\textrm{ and }\lambda_{ji}(\mu)=2\Big(\mu(j)-\sum\limits_{\ell\in{\mathcal{V}}\backslash\{i,j\}}\mu(\ell)\Big).

(4.30)

Now observe that
{\mathcal{V}}\backslash\{i,j\}\in\mathcal{T}({\mathbb{H}})
, so
\sum\limits_{\ell\in{\mathcal{V}}\backslash\{i,j\}}\mu(\ell)>\displaystyle\frac{1}{3},
then
\lambda_{ij}(\mu)<0
and
\lambda_{ji}(\mu)<0
.
Thus there exists
a_{3}^{*}
such that
\Delta_{ij}<0
whenever
{x_{i}\vee x_{j}}\geq a_{3}^{*}
.

(iv) For any
i,j
such that
i\neq j
and
\{i,j\}\subset H
for some
H\in\mathcal{J}
, for any integers
x_{i},x_{j}>0,
we obtain that

\Delta^{\prime}_{ij}:={\mathbb{E}}\left[Q\left(X_{n+1}\right)-Q\left(X_{n}\right)|X_{n}=x_{i}.\textbf{e}_{i}+x_{j}.\textbf{e}_{j}\right]=\nu_{ij}(\mu)x_{i}+\nu_{ji}(\mu)x_{j}+\beta^{\prime}_{ij}(\mu)

for a bounded
\beta^{\prime}_{ij}(\mu)
and

\nu_{ij}(\mu)=2\Big(\mu(i)-\sum\limits_{\ell\in\overline{H}}\mu(\ell)\Big)\textrm{ and }\nu_{ji}(\mu)=2\Big(\mu(j)-\sum\limits_{\ell\in\overline{H}}\mu(\ell)\Big).

(4.31)

As
\overline{H}\in\mathcal{T}({\mathbb{H}})
, so
\sum\limits_{\ell\in\overline{H}}\mu(\ell)>\displaystyle\frac{1}{3},
then
\nu_{ij}(\mu)<0
and
\nu_{ji}(\mu)<0
.

Again, there exists
a_{4}^{*}
such that
\Delta^{\prime}_{ij}<0
whenever
{x_{i}\vee x_{j}}\geq a_{4}^{*}
.

(v) We finally consider the case where
X_{n}=x_{i}.\textbf{e}_{i}+x_{j}.\textbf{e}_{j}+x_{k}.\textbf{e}_{k}
for
H=\{i,j,k\}
, for some
H\in{\mathcal{J}}
,
and integers
x_{i},x_{j}
and
x_{k}
such that
x_{i},x_{j}\geq x_{k}>0
.

\displaystyle\Delta_{H}

\displaystyle:={\mathbb{E}}\left[Q\left(X_{n+1}\right)-Q\left(X_{n}\right)|X_{n}=x_{i}.\textbf{e}_{i}+x_{j}.\textbf{e}_{j}+x_{k}.\textbf{e}_{k}\right]

\displaystyle=\alpha_{i}(\mu)x_{i}+\alpha_{j}(\mu)x_{j}+\alpha_{k}(\mu)x_{k}+\beta_{H}(\mu),

for a bounded
\beta_{H}(\mu)
, and for

\alpha_{i}(\mu)=2\Big(\mu(i)-\sum\limits_{\ell\in\overline{H}}\mu(\ell)\Big),\;\alpha_{j}(\mu)=2\Big(\mu(j)-\sum\limits_{\ell\in\overline{H}}\mu(\ell)\Big)\textrm{ and }\alpha_{k}(\mu)=2\mu(k).

(4.32)

As
\overline{H}\in\mathcal{T}({\mathbb{H}})
, so
\alpha_{i}(\mu)<0
and
\alpha_{j}(\mu)<0
.
From this, we deduce as above the existence of an integer
a_{5}^{*}
such that
\Delta_{H}<0
whenever

x_{i}\vee x_{j}\geq a_{5}^{*}
.

To conclude, if we let
\mathcal{K}
be the finite set

{\mathcal{K}=\left\{\textbf{x}\in{\mathcal{E}}\;:\;x_{i}\leq\max\Big(a_{1}^{*},...,a_{5}^{*},2\Big);\;i\in{\mathcal{V}}\right\},}

then if follows from the above arguments that for any
\textbf{x}\in{\mathcal{E}}\cap\bar{\mathcal{K}}

and any
n\in{\mathbb{N}}
,

{\mathbb{E}}\left[Q\left(Y_{n+1}\right)-Q\left(Y_{n}\right)|Y_{n}=\textbf{x}\right]<0.

We deduce from Lyapunov-Foster Theorem
1.1
that the chain
\left\{Y_{n};\,n\in{\mathbb{N}}\right\}
is positive recurrent.
This is the case in turn for the chain
\left\{X_{n};\,n\in{\mathbb{N}}\right\}
.
∎

Remark 4.4
.

Observe that the only incomplete (in the sense of Theorem
4.2
)
3
-uniform hypergraph of order
4 would be obtained from the complete one by deleting only one hyperedge. However, as easily seen the transversal number of the resulting hypergraph is 1, so the latter is non-stabilizable from Proposition
4.5
.

In the following examples we show how the stability can be shown for various incomplete
3
-uniform hypergraphs using
Theorem
4.2
,

5
10
15
1
2
3
x
f(x)

Figure 4.5
:
The curve of the function
f(x)=\displaystyle\left(\frac{2x^{4}-9x^{3}+12x^{2}-13x+12}{6x^{2}+10x+24}\right)^{\frac{1}{4}}.

Corollary 4.3
.

Consider an incomplete
3
-uniform hypergraph
\mathbb{H}^{\prime}
satisfying the assumptions of Theorem
4.2
.
Recall (
4.18
), and define the sets

\mathscr{A}({\mathbb{H}}^{\prime}):=\biggl\{\mu\in\mathscr{M}({\mathcal{V}})\,:\,\displaystyle\frac{\mu_{\mbox{\emph{\scriptsize{max}}}}}{\mu_{\mbox{\emph{\scriptsize{min}}}}}<\left(\frac{2q^{4}-9q^{3}+12q^{2}-13q+12}{6q^{2}+10q+24}\right)^{1/4}\biggl\}\,

and

\mathscr{S}_{1}({\mathbb{H}}^{\prime}):=\mathscr{A}({\mathbb{H}}^{\prime})\,\cap\,\mathscr{N}_{2}(\mathbb{H}^{\prime})\,\cap\mathscr{N}^{\scriptsize{-}}_{3}(\mathbb{H}^{\prime}).

Then the model

(\mathbb{H}^{\prime},\textsc{ml},\mu)
is stable for any
\mu\in\mathscr{S}_{1}({\mathbb{H}}^{\prime}).

Proof.

Recalling (
4.28
) and (
4.29
), a simple algebra shows that

\mathscr{A}({\mathbb{H}}^{\prime})\subset\biggl\{\mu\in\mathscr{M}({\mathcal{V}}):\left(\max\limits_{i\in J}\lambda_{i}(\mu)\vee\max\limits_{i\in\bar{J}}\nu_{i}(\mu)\right)<0\biggl\},

thus
\mathscr{S}_{1}({\mathbb{H}}^{\prime})\subset\mathscr{S}({\mathbb{H}}^{\prime})
.
∎

Example 4.3
.

Observe that for any such
{\mathbb{H}}^{\prime}=({\mathcal{V}},{\mathcal{H}}^{\prime})
satisfying the assumptions of Theorem
4.2
,
the model
(\mathbb{H}^{\prime},\textsc{ml},\mu_{\textsc{u}})
is stable for
\mu_{\textsc{u}}
the uniform distribution on
{\mathcal{V}}
.
Indeed, we have
\mu_{\textsc{u}}\in\mathscr{S}_{1}({\mathbb{H}}^{\prime})
.
To see this, first observe that
\forall q\geq 5,\;\frac{2q^{4}-9q^{3}+12q^{2}-13q+12}{6q^{2}+10q+24}>1,
see Figure
4.5
. Moreover, it is immediate that

\mu_{\textsc{u}}\in\mathscr{N}_{3}^{-}({\mathbb{H}}^{\prime})
.
It remains to show that
\mu_{\textsc{u}}\in\mathscr{N}_{2}({\mathbb{H}}^{\prime})
.
We proceed in three steps. First, for
q=5
the only incomplete
3
-uniform hypergraph in the sense of Theorem
4.2
is the complete hypergraph on
\llbracket 1,5\rrbracket
minus one vertex, say
\{1,2,3\}
.
It is then easily seen that
\{4,5\}
is the only minimal transversal of
{\mathbb{H}}^{\prime}
. So
\tau({\mathbb{H}}^{\prime})=2
, in a way that for
all
T^{\prime}\in\mathcal{T}({\mathbb{H}}^{\prime})
,
\mu_{\textsc{u}}(T^{\prime})\geq 2/5>1/3,

showing that
\mu_{\textsc{u}}\in\mathscr{N}_{2}({\mathbb{H}}^{\prime})
.

Now, if
q=6
there are two incomplete
3
-uniform hypergraph in the sense of Theorem
4.2
:
the complete
3
-uniform hypergraph on
\llbracket 1,6\rrbracket
minus one hyperedge, say
\{1,2,3\}
; and the
complete
3
-uniform hypergraph on
\llbracket 1,6\rrbracket
minus two disjoint hyperedges, say
\{1,2,3\}
and
\{4,5,6\}
. In both cases,
\{4,5,6\}
is a minimal transversal of
{\mathbb{H}}^{\prime}
, thus
\tau({\mathbb{H}}^{\prime})=3
, and so

\mu_{\textsc{u}}(T^{\prime})\geq 3/6>1/3,
for all
T^{\prime}\in\mathcal{T}({\mathbb{H}})
,
proving again that
\mu_{\textsc{u}}\in\mathscr{N}_{2}({\mathbb{H}}^{\prime})
.

We now address the case where
q>6
. First observe that

q-2-\left\lfloor{q\over 3}\right\rfloor>{q\over 3}.

(4.33)

Then, let
p=|\mathcal{J}|
(using the notation of Theorem
4.2
),
and denote

\mathcal{J}=\{H_{1},...,H_{p}\}
. It is easily seen that a transversal of
{\mathbb{H}}
can be constructed
from any minimal transversal of
{\mathbb{H}}^{\prime}
, by induction, as follows:

•

Take a minimal transversal
T^{\prime}
of
{\mathbb{H}}^{\prime}
,
and set
{\mathcal{H}}_{0}:={\mathcal{H}}^{\prime}
and
T_{0}:=T^{\prime}
.

•

For any
i=1,...,p
, set
{\mathcal{H}}_{i}={\mathcal{H}}_{i-1}\cup\{H_{i}\}
and set
T_{i}
, a
transversal of
({\mathcal{V}},{\mathcal{H}}_{i})
of minimal size among those including

T_{i-1}
. (
{T_{i}}
necessarily exists since
T_{i-1}\cup\{H_{i}\}
is a transversal of
({\mathcal{V}},{\mathcal{H}}_{i})
, as easily seen by induction.)

•

We obtain
{\mathcal{H}}={\mathcal{H}}_{p}
by construction, and
T:=T_{p}
is a transversal of
{\mathbb{H}}
.

We claim that

|T|\leq|T^{\prime}|+p.

(4.34)

To see this, observe that for any
i=1,...,p
we have the following alternative: either
H_{i}\cap T_{i-1}=\emptyset
, in which case we can take
T_{i}
of the form
T_{i-1}\cup\{k\}
for any
k\in H_{i}
, or

H_{i}\cap T_{i-1}\neq\emptyset
, in which case
T_{i}=T_{i-1}
. In all cases we have that
|T_{i}|\leq|T_{i-1}|+1
, and (
4.34
) follows by induction.
Observing that
|T|\geq\tau(H)=q-2
, that, as the
H_{i}
’s are disjoint,

p\leq\lfloor{q\over 3}\rfloor
, and using (
4.33
) and (
4.34
), we finally obtain that

\mu_{\textsc{u}}(T^{\prime})={|T^{\prime}|\over q}\geq{|T|-p\over q}>{1\over 3},

hence, once again
\mu_{\textsc{u}}\in\mathscr{N}_{2}({\mathbb{H}}^{\prime})
.

To conclude,
\mu_{\textsc{u}}
is in all cases, an element of
\mathscr{S}_{1}({\mathbb{H}}^{\prime})
, implying that the model
(\mathbb{H}^{\prime},\textsc{ml},\mu_{\textsc{u}})
is stable for all such
{\mathbb{H}}^{\prime}
.

4.4
Discussion of results and conclusion

In this chapter, we have studied a generalization of stochastic matching models on graphs, by allowing the matching structure to be a hypergraph.
This class of models appears to have a wide range of applications in operations management, healthcare, and assemble-to-order systems.
After formally introducing the model, we have proposed a simple Markovian representation, under IID assumptions.
We have then addressed the general question of stochastic stability, viewed as the positive recurrence of the underlying Markov chain. For this class of systems, solving this elementary and central question turns out to be an intricate problem.
As the results of Sections
4.1
and
4.2
demonstrate, stochastic matching models on hypergraphs are in general, difficult to stabilize. Unlike the GM on graphs, the non-emptiness of the stability region with matching models on hypergraphs depends on a collection of conditions in the geometry of the compatibility hypergraph: rank, anti-rank, degree, size of the transversals, existence of cycles, and so on.

Nevertheless, we show in Section
4.3
that the “house” of stable systems is not empty, but shelters models on various uniform hypergraphs that are complete, or complete up to a partition of their nodes (which is a reasonable assumption regarding kidney exchange programs with
3
-cycles, in which case, according to the compatibility of blood types and immunological characteristics, most but not all hyperedges of size
3
appear in the compatibility graph). We provide the exact stability region of the system in the first case, and a lower bound in the second. For this, we resort to ad-hoc multi-dimensional Lyapunov techniques.

Chapter 5
Multigraphs

Introduction

In this chapter we provide a further extension of the previous chapter, we introduced a new stochastic matching model on hypergraph, that generalizes the GM model, then we provide the necessary conditions of stability for the present model, and we precise the stability region in particular cases of GM model. Such as among dating websites and peer-to-peer interfaces it is possible to assume that the items of the same class can be matched together. Hence the need to consider matching models on compatibility matching structures that are
multigraph
rather than just a graph or hypergraph, i.e., an architecture of graph admitting self-loops that is, edges with permission to connect to themself.
En route
, by showing results for stochastic matching models on multigraphs, we show various results that have their inner interest for GM models on graphs - see in particular Propositions
5.1
and
5.3
.

This stochastic matching model addressed in this chapter is formally defined as follows: items enter the system by single arrivals, and get matched by groups of 2 or possibly matched to itself in case that there exists
self-loops
, following compatibilities that are represented by a given multigraph.
A matching policy determines the matchings to be executed in the case of a multiple-choice, and the unmatched items are stored in a buffer, waiting for a future match.

This chapter is organized as follows: In Section
5.1
, we present our main results for GM models on multigraphs, among which, the maximality and the explicit product form of the stationary probability for the
fcfm
policy, and the maximality of Max-Weight policies. To illustrate these results, several examples
are presented in Section
5.2
. The proofs of our main results are then presented in
Sections
5.3
,
5.4
and
5.5
.

5.1
Main results

We now state the main results of this chapter.
Similarly to
[
33
]
, we will be led to consider the set

\textsc{Ncond}(G)=\left\{\mu\in\mathscr{M}\left({\mathcal{V}}\right):\forall\,I\in\,{\mathbb{I}}(G),\;\mu\left(I\right)<\mu\left({\mathcal{E}}\left(I\right)\right)\right\}.

(5.1)

Let us immediately observe that

Lemma 5.1
.

For any connected multigraph
G
, we have that

\textsc{Ncond}\left(\check{G}\right)\subseteq\textsc{Ncond}(G).

Where
\check{G}=({\mathcal{V}},\check{{\mathcal{E}}})
is the maximal subgraph of
G
(see Definition
1.13
).

Proof.

The result simply follows from the obvious facts that
\mathbb{I}(G)\subset\mathbb{I}\left(\check{G}\right)
and that, for any
I\in\mathbb{I}(G)
,

{\mathcal{E}}(I)=\check{{\mathcal{E}}}(I)
, since
I\subset{\mathcal{V}}_{2}
.
∎

It is stated in Theorem 1 of
[
33
]
that, if
G
is a graph, the set
Ncond
(G)
is non-empty if and only if
G
is not a bipartite graph.
This result can be generalized to multigraphs:

Proposition 5.1
.

For any connected multigraph
G
, we have that

\textsc{Ncond}(G)=\emptyset\Longleftrightarrow\mbox{ $G$ is a bipartite graph.}

Proposition
5.1
is proven in Section
5.3
.
From Proposition 2 in
[
33
]
, whenever
G
is a graph (i.e.,
{\mathcal{V}}_{1}=\emptyset
),
the set
\textsc{Stab}(G,\Phi)
is included in
\textsc{Ncond}(G)
for any admissible policy
\Phi
. In other words, for any measure
\mu
,
belonging to
\textsc{Ncond}(G)
is necessary for the stability of the system
(G,\Phi,\mu)
, for any
\Phi
.
A similar result holds for any multigraph
G
:

Proposition 5.2
.

For any connected multigraph
G=({\mathcal{V}},{\mathcal{E}})
and any admissible matching policy

\Phi
