, we have that

\textsc{Stab}(G,\Phi)\subset\textsc{Ncond}(G).

Proof.

The proof is analog to that of Proposition 2 in
[
33
]
. ∎

Hence, the notion of maximality of a matching policy is:

Definition 5.1
.

For any connected multigraph
G
that is not a bipartite graph, a matching policy
\Phi
is said maximal if the sets

\textsc{Stab}(G,\Phi)
and
\textsc{Ncond}(G)
coincide.

Whenever
G
is a graph, Theorem 1 of
[
36
]
shows, first, that the policy ‘First Come, First Matched’ (
fcfm
) is maximal, and second, that the stationary probability of the chain
\left\{W_{n};\,n\in{\mathbb{N}}\right\}
can be expressed in a remarkable product form.
We generalize this result to multigraphs:

Theorem 5.1
.

The matching policy ‘First Come, First Matched’ is maximal: for any connected
multigraph
G
that is not a bipartite graph, we have that
Stab
(G,\textsc{fcfm})=\textsc{Ncond}(G)
.
Moreover, for any
\mu\in\textsc{Ncond}(G)
the unique stationary probability
\Pi_{W}
of the chain
\left(W_{n}\right)_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
is defined by

\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}\Pi_{W}(\varepsilon)&=\alpha;\\
\Pi_{W}(w)&=\alpha\displaystyle\prod\limits_{l=1}^{q}\frac{\mu(w_{l})}{\mu(\mathcal{E}(\{w_{1},\dots,w_{l}\}))},\mbox{ for all }w=w_{1}\dots w_{q}\in\mathbb{W}\setminus\{\varepsilon\},\end{array}\right.

where

\alpha^{-1}=\!1\!+\!\sum\limits_{I\in\mathbb{I}\left(\check{G}\right)}\sum\limits_{\sigma\in\mathfrak{S}_{|I|}}\prod\limits_{i=1}^{|I|}\frac{\mu\left(e_{\sigma(i)}\right)}{\mu(\mathcal{E}(\{e_{\sigma(1)},\dots,e_{\sigma(i)}\}))-\mu(\{e_{\sigma(1)},\dots,e_{\sigma(i)}\}\cap{\mathcal{V}}_{2})},

(5.2)

and where we denote
I=\{e_{1},\dots,e_{|I|}\}
for any
I\in\mathbb{I}\left(\check{G}\right)
.

Theorem
5.1
is proven in section
5.4
.

Remark 5.1
.

If the model is finite, i.e., all nodes of
G
have self-loops or in other words,
{\mathcal{V}}_{2}=\emptyset
, then it readily follows from
Theorem
5.1
that the unique stationary probability on the finite state space
\mathbb{W}
, is given by

\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}\Pi_{W}(\varepsilon)&=\alpha;\\
\Pi_{W}\left(e_{\sigma(1)}\cdots e_{\sigma(|I|)}\right)&=\alpha\displaystyle\prod\limits_{i=1}^{|I|}\frac{\mu\left(e_{\sigma(i)}\right)}{\mu(\mathcal{E}(\{e_{\sigma(1)},\dots,e_{\sigma(i)}\}))},\mbox{ for all }I\in\mathbb{I}(\check{G}),\,\sigma\in\mathfrak{S}_{|I|},\end{array}\right.

with the normalizing constant

\alpha=\left[\!1\!+\!\sum\limits_{I\in\mathbb{I}\left(\check{G}\right)}\sum\limits_{\sigma\in\mathfrak{S}_{|I|}}\prod\limits_{i=1}^{|I|}\frac{\mu\left(e_{\sigma(i)}\right)}{\mu(\mathcal{E}(\{e_{\sigma(1)},\dots,e_{\sigma(i)}\}))}\right]^{-1}\cdot

On another hand, as is shown in Theorem 5.3 of
[
29
]
, all Max-Weight matching policies such that
\beta>0
are maximal
whenever
G
is a graph. (In particular, the maximality of ‘Match the Longest’ (
ml
) for GM models was first proven in Theorem 2 of
[
33
]
, as a consequence of the corresponding result for EBM models, (see Theorem 7.1 of
[
12
]
).
This result can also be generalized to multigraphs:

Theorem 5.2
.

Any Max-Weight policy
\Phi
such that
\beta>0
is maximal: for any multigraph
G
that is not a bipartite graph, we have that

Stab
(G,\Phi)=\textsc{Ncond}(G)
.

Aside from
fcfm
and Max-Weight policies, we can determine, or lower-bound, the stability region of the model for particular classes
of multigraphs.

Given a multigraph
G=({\mathcal{V}}_{1}\cup{\mathcal{V}}_{2},{\mathcal{E}}),
let us define an important class of matching policy that is called
{\mathcal{V}}_{2}
-favorable
policies and we will be taken in this chapter.

Definition 5.2
.

We say that an admissible matching policy
\Phi
on
G
is
{\mathcal{V}}_{2}
-favorable if any incoming item always prioritizes a match with a compatible
item of class in
{\mathcal{V}}_{2}
over a compatible item of class in
{\mathcal{V}}_{1}
, whenever it has the choice. Formally, if the class detail is given by
x\in\mathbb{X}
and the arrival is of class
v
, it never occurs that the incoming
v
-item is matched with a
j
-item, for some
j\in{\mathcal{V}}_{1}
, while
{\mathcal{P}}(x,v)\cap{\mathcal{V}}_{2}\neq\emptyset
.

Definition 5.3
.

Let
G
be a connected multigraph.
We say that
G
is complete
p
-partite,
p\geq 2
, if its maximal subgraph
\check{G}
is complete
p
-partite.
Then, the minimal blow-up graph
\hat{G}
(see Definition
1.14
) itself is called an extended complete
p
-partite graph.

Observe that an extended complete
p
-partite graph is
not
complete
p
-partite whenever the construction above is non-trivial, i.e., the multigraph in the above definition is not a graph, see an example in Figure
5.1
.

1
4
5
\;\;\;\;\;2
3
1
4
\;5
\;\;\;\;\;2
3
1
4
\;\;\;\;\;2
5
3
\;\,\underline{5}

Figure 5.1
:
Middle: A multigraph
G.
Left: Its maximal complete
3
-partite subgraph
\check{G}.
Right: Extended complete
3
-partite graph
\hat{G}.

Theorem 5.3
.

Let
G
be a complete
p
-partite multigraph,
p\geq 2
. Then,

(i)

If
p\geq 3
or
{\mathcal{V}}_{1}\neq\emptyset
, then any
{\mathcal{V}}_{2}
-favorable matching policy
\Phi
is maximal, that is,
\textsc{Stab}(G,\Phi)=\textsc{Ncond}(G).

(ii)

If
p\geq 3
, then
\textsc{Ncond}\left(\check{G}\right)\subset\textsc{Stab}(G,\Phi)
, for any admissible matching policy
\Phi
.

With the above results in hands, we have the following panorama regarding the stability region of a matching model on a connected multigraph
G
:

(i)

Any measure
\mu
that does not belong to the set
Ncond
(
G
) makes the system unstable;

(ii)

If
G
is a bipartite graph, then the model cannot be stable;

(iii)

Otherwise, the region
Ncond
(
G
) is necessarily non-empty, and the models
(G,\textsc{fcfm},\mu)
and
(G,\Phi,\mu)
for any
Max-Weight policy
\Phi
, are stable for any
\mu\in\textsc{Ncond}(G)
;

(iv)

For any complete
p
-partite multigraph (
p\geq 2
) that is not a bipartite graph and any
\mu\in\textsc{Ncond}\left({G}\right)
,
any model
(G,\Phi,\mu)
such that
\mu\in\textsc{Ncond}\left(\check{G}\right)
or
\Phi
is
{\mathcal{V}}_{2}
-favorable, is stable.

As a by-product of Theorem
5.3
we can determine, or lower-bound, the stability region of GM models on extended complete
p
-partite graphs.

Definition 5.4
.

For any measures
\mu\in\mathscr{M}({\mathcal{V}})
and
\hat{\mu}\in\mathscr{M}(\hat{\mathcal{V}})
, we say that
\hat{\mu}
extends
\mu
on
\hat{G}
, and that

\mu
reduces
\hat{\mu}
on
G
, if

\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}\displaystyle\hat{\mu}(i)&=\mu(i),\quad\text{for all}\;i\in{\mathcal{V}}_{2}\,;\\
\displaystyle\hat{\mu}(i)+\hat{\mu}(\underline{i})&=\mu(i),\quad\text{for all}\;i\in{\mathcal{V}}_{1}.\end{array}\right.

(5.3)

Definition 5.5
.

Let
\Phi
and
\hat{\Phi}
be two admissible matching policies, respectively on
G
and
\hat{G}
.
We say that
\hat{\Phi}
extends
\Phi
on
\hat{G}
if, for any
\mu\in\mathscr{M}({{\mathcal{V}}})
and
\hat{\mu}\in\mathscr{M}(\hat{\mathcal{V}})
, whenever both systems
(G,\Phi,\mu)
and
(\hat{G},\hat{\Phi},\hat{\mu})
are in the same state
w\in\mathbb{W}
and welcome the same arrival,
\Phi
and
\hat{\Phi}
induce the same choice of match, if any.

Proposition 5.3
.

Let
\hat{G}
be an extended complete
p
-partite graph,
p\geq 2
, and
\check{G}
be its reduced graph.

(i)

If
p\geq 3
or
{\mathcal{V}}_{1}\neq\emptyset
, then for any matching policy
\hat{\Phi}
on
\hat{G}
that extends a
{\mathcal{V}}_{2}
-favorable policy on
G
,

\textsc{Stab}(\hat{G},\hat{\Phi})=\textsc{Ncond}(\hat{G}).

(ii)

If
p\geq 3
, then for any measure
\hat{\mu}
on
\hat{G}
whose reduced measure
\mu
is an element of
\textsc{Ncond}(\check{G})
,
and any matching policy
\hat{\Phi}
on
\hat{G}
, the model
(\hat{G},\hat{\Phi},\hat{\mu})
is stable.

The proofs of Theorem
5.2
, Theorem
5.3
and Proposition
5.3
are given in section
5.5
.

5.2
A few examples

In this section, we illustrate our main results by different examples.

Example 5.1
.

Consider the multigraph
G
of Figure
5.2
, made of four nodes arranged in a square, with a self-loop at each node.

1\;
\;2
\;3
4\;

Figure 5.2
:
Multigraph
G
of Example
5.1
.

Since all nodes have a self-loop, it follows from Remark
2.3
that any matching model on
G
is necessarily stable, that is, for any admissible
\Phi
, we have that
\textsc{Stab}(G,\Phi)=\mathscr{M}({\mathcal{V}}).
Let us focus on the
fcfm
policy.
The set of admissible queue details is given by
\mathbb{W}=\{\varepsilon,1,2,3,4,13,24,31,42\},
and as a consequence of Remark
5.1
, we can compute explicitly
\Pi_{W}
, obtaining the following values:

\left\{\begin{array}[]{llll}&\Pi_{W}(\varepsilon)=\alpha&&\\
&\Pi_{W}(1)=\alpha\frac{\mu(1)}{1-\mu(3)}&&\Pi_{W}(2)=\alpha\frac{\mu(2)}{1-\mu(4)}\\
&\Pi_{W}(3)=\alpha\frac{\mu(3)}{1-\mu(1)}&&\Pi_{W}(4)=\alpha\frac{\mu(4)}{1-\mu(2)}\\
&\Pi_{W}(13)=\alpha\frac{\mu(1)}{1-\mu(3)}\mu(3)&&\Pi_{W}(24)=\alpha\frac{\mu(2)}{1-\mu(4)}\mu(4)\\
&\Pi_{W}(31)=\alpha\frac{\mu(3)}{1-\mu(1)}\mu(1)&&\Pi_{W}(42)=\alpha\frac{\mu(4)}{1-\mu(2)}\mu(2),\end{array}\right.

with

\alpha=\left[1+\mu(1)\frac{1+\mu(3)}{1-\mu(3)}+\mu(2)\frac{1+\mu(4)}{1-\mu(4)}+\mu(3)\frac{1+\mu(1)}{1-\mu(1)}+\mu(4)\frac{1+\mu(2)}{1-\mu(2)}\right]^{-1},

using the fact that
\mathbb{I}\left(\check{G}\right)=\{\{1\},\{2\},\{3\},\{4\},\{1,3\},\{2,4\}\}
.

Example 5.2
.

Consider the multigraph
G
(at the middle) of Figure
1.7
. From Theorems
5.1
and
5.2
, both the stability region
\textsc{Stab}(G,\textsc{fcfm})
under First Come, First Matched, and the stability region
\textsc{Stab}(G,\textsc{mw})

under any Max-Weight policy, coincide with the set

\displaystyle\textsc{Ncond}(G)

\displaystyle=\left\{\mu\in\mathscr{M}({\mathcal{V}}):\mu(1)<\mu(2),\,\mu(\{1,3\})\vee\mu(\{1,4\})<{1\over 2}\right\}.

Second, recall that the maximal subgraph
\check{G}
is the pendant graph
that studied in Lemma 3 of
[
33
]
. For
\eta>0,
there exists a linear Lyapunov function
L_{\eta}
such that for any
w\in\mathbb{W},
the drift
\check{\Delta}^{\check{\Phi}}_{\mu}L_{\eta}(w)<0
then from Proposition
5.8
, we have
\hat{\Delta}^{\hat{\Phi}}_{\hat{\mu}}L_{\eta}(w)<0
and
\Delta^{\Phi}_{\mu}L_{\eta}(w)<0.
Then the model
(G,\Phi,\mu)
and
\left(\hat{G},\hat{\Phi},\hat{\mu}\right)
are stable.

Example 5.3
.

Consider now a multigraph
G
whose maximal subgraph is a complete
2
-partite graph of order 3 (i.e., a string of 3 nodes), as represented in
Figure
5.3
,

1
2
3
1
2
3
1
2
3
\underline{3}

Figure 5.3
:
Left: A multigraph whose maximal subgraph is a complete 2-partite graph of order 3. Right: Its minimal blow-up graph.

We easily obtain that

\displaystyle\textsc{Ncond}(G)

\displaystyle=\left\{\mu\in\mathscr{M}({\mathcal{V}})\,:\,\mu(1)<\mu(2)<{1\over 2}\right\},

\displaystyle\textsc{Ncond}(\hat{G})

\displaystyle=\left\{\hat{\mu}\in\mathscr{M}(\hat{\mathcal{V}}):\hat{\mu}(1)<\hat{\mu}(2),\,\hat{\mu}(2)\vee\hat{\mu}(\{1,3\})\vee\hat{\mu}(\{1,\underline{3}\})<{1\over 2}\right\}.

In view of Theorems
5.1
and
5.2
, the respective stability regions
\textsc{Stab}(G,\textsc{fcfm})
and
\textsc{Stab}(G,\textsc{mw})
under First Come, First Matched,
or any Max-Weight policy, coincide with
\textsc{Ncond}(G)
.

Let us first focus on the
fcfm
policy. The set of admissible queue details is given by:

\mathbb{W}=\{\varepsilon\}\cup\left\{1^{k}:k\geq 1\right\}\cup\left\{2^{k}:k\geq 1\right\}\cup\left\{1^{r}31^{k-r}:k\geq 0,\;0\leq r\leq k\right\}.

By Theorem
5.1
, we have

\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}&\Pi_{W}(\varepsilon)=\alpha\\
&\Pi_{W}(1^{k})=\alpha\left(\frac{\mu(1)}{\mu(2)}\right)^{k}\qquad\Pi_{W}(2^{k})=\alpha\left(\frac{\mu(2)}{1-\mu(2)}\right)^{k}\\
&\Pi_{W}\left(1^{r}31^{k-r}\right)=\alpha\left(\frac{\mu(1)}{\mu(2)}\right)^{r}\times\frac{\mu(3)}{1-\mu(1)}\times\left(\frac{\mu(1)}{1-\mu(1)}\right)^{k-r},\end{array}\right.

and since
\mathbb{I}\left(\check{G}\right)=\{\{1\},\{2\},\{3\},\{1,3\}\}
, we can express
\alpha
as follows,

\alpha=\biggl[1+\frac{\mu(1)}{\mu(2)-\mu(1)}+\frac{\mu(2)}{1-2\mu(2)}+\frac{\mu(3)}{1-\mu(1)}\\
+\frac{\mu(1)}{\mu(2)-\mu(1)}\frac{\mu(3)}{1-2\mu(1)}+\frac{\mu(3)}{1-\mu(1)}\frac{\mu(1)}{1-2\mu(1)}\biggl]^{-1}.

Second, consider a matching policy
\hat{\Phi}
such that a
2
-item always prioritizes a
1
-item over a
3
or a
\underline{3}
-item. Then
\hat{\Phi}
extends a
{\mathcal{V}}_{2}
-favorable policy

\Phi
on
\hat{G}
. Thus, from Proposition
5.3
(i), the stability region of the system is
\textsc{Ncond}(\hat{G})
,
in other words
\hat{\Phi}
is maximal on
\hat{G}
. We thereby generalize with a very simple proof, the result of Lemma 3 of
[
33
]
to the case where
\mu(3)\neq\mu(\underline{3})
.
Last , in view of Theorem
5.3
(i), any
{\mathcal{V}}_{2}
-favorable matching policy on
G
(i.e., such that
2
prioritizes
1
over
3
) is maximal, that is, has the stability region
\textsc{Ncond}(G)
.

Example 5.4
.

Last, consider the multigraph
G
represented in (the middle figure of) Figure

5.1
. The maximal subgraph is complete
3
-partite, and we readily obtain that

\displaystyle\textsc{Ncond}(\check{G})

\displaystyle=\left\{\mu\in\mathscr{M}({\mathcal{V}})\,:\,\mu(1)\vee\mu(\{2,4\})\vee\mu(\{3,5\})<{1\over 2}\right\};

\displaystyle\textsc{Ncond}(G)

\displaystyle=\left\{\mu\in\mathscr{M}({\mathcal{V}})\,:\,\mu(1)\vee\mu(\{2,4\})<{1\over 2},\;\mu(3)<\mu(\{1,2,4\})\right\};

\displaystyle\textsc{Ncond}(\hat{G})

\displaystyle=\left\{\hat{\mu}\in\mathscr{M}(\hat{{\mathcal{V}}})\,:\,\hat{\mu}(1)\vee\hat{\mu}(\{2,4\})\vee\hat{\mu}(\{3,5\})\vee\hat{\mu}(\{3,\underline{5}\})<{1\over 2}\right\}.

Then, from Theorems
5.1
and
5.2
, the respective stability regions
\textsc{Stab}(G,\textsc{fcfm})
and
\textsc{Stab}(G,\textsc{mw})
under First Come, First Matched,
or any Max-Weight policy coincide with the set
\textsc{Ncond}(G)
. From Theorem
5.3
(i), for any policy
\Phi
on
G
according to which all items prioritize
3
-items over
5
items is maximal, i.e.,

\textsc{Stab}(G,\Phi)=\textsc{Ncond}(G)
. From Theorem
5.3
(ii), any policy
\Phi
on
G
is such that
\textsc{Ncond}(\check{G})\subset\textsc{Stab}(G,\Phi)
.
Last, from Proposition
5.3
, any policy
\hat{\Phi}
on
\hat{G}
giving priority to
3
-items over
5
and
\underline{5}
-items is maximal, whereas for any matching policy

\hat{\Phi}
and any measure
\hat{\mu}
on
\hat{\mathcal{V}}
extending a measure of
\textsc{Ncond}(\check{G})
, the model
(\hat{G},\hat{\Phi},\hat{\mu})
is stable.

5.3
Proof of Proposition 5.1

Throughout this section, fix a connected multigraph
G=({\mathcal{V}},{\mathcal{E}})
, where
{\mathcal{V}}={\mathcal{V}}_{1}\cup{\mathcal{V}}_{2}
, and denote its minimal blow-up graph by

\hat{G}=(\hat{\mathcal{V}},\hat{\mathcal{E}})
, where
\hat{\mathcal{V}}={\mathcal{V}}\cup\underline{{\mathcal{V}}_{1}}
.
We first have the following,

Lemma 5.2
.

For any
\mu\in\mathscr{M}({\mathcal{V}})
we have that

\mu\in\textsc{Ncond}(G)\iff\hat{\mu}_{\tiny{1/2}}\in\textsc{Ncond}(\hat{G}),

where
\hat{\mu}_{\tiny{1/2}}
is the extended measure of
\mu
such that

\displaystyle\hat{\mu}_{\tiny{1/2}}(i)=\hat{\mu}_{\scriptsize{1/2}}(\underline{i})={1\over 2}\mu(i)\quad\text{for all}\;i\in{\mathcal{V}}_{1}.

(5.4)

Proof.

For any
I\in{\mathbb{I}}(G)
, set
{\mathcal{E}}_{1}(I)={\mathcal{E}}(I)\cap{\mathcal{V}}_{1}
and
{\mathcal{E}}_{2}(I)={\mathcal{E}}(I)\cap{\mathcal{V}}_{2}
.

Let us prove first the implication
\Longleftarrow
: Let
\hat{\mu}_{\tiny{1/2}}\in\textsc{Ncond}(\hat{G})
and
I\in\mathbb{I}(G)
. As
I\subset{\mathcal{V}}_{2}
, we get that

\displaystyle\mu(I)=\hat{\mu}_{\tiny{1/2}}(I)<\hat{\mu}_{\tiny{1/2}}(\hat{{\mathcal{E}}}(I))

\displaystyle=\hat{\mu}_{\tiny{1/2}}\left({\mathcal{E}}(I)\cup\underline{{\mathcal{E}}_{1}(I)}\right)

\displaystyle=\hat{\mu}_{\tiny{1/2}}({\mathcal{E}}_{1}(I))+\hat{\mu}_{\tiny{1/2}}({\mathcal{E}}_{2}(I))+\hat{\mu}_{\tiny{1/2}}\left(\underline{{\mathcal{E}}_{1}(I)}\right)

\displaystyle=\displaystyle\mu({\mathcal{E}}_{1}(I))+\mu({\mathcal{E}}_{2}(I))

\displaystyle=\mu({\mathcal{E}}(I)),

where the second equality is due to the fact that
\hat{\mathcal{E}}={\mathcal{E}}\cup\underline{{\mathcal{E}}_{1}}.
The third follows from the fact that
{\mathcal{E}}={\mathcal{E}}_{1}\cup{\mathcal{E}}_{2}.
The fouth follows from the fact that the equations (
5.3
) and (
5.4
) holds true.

Let us prove now the opposite implication
\Longrightarrow
: Let us now fix
\mu\in\textsc{Ncond}(G)
and
\hat{I}\in{\mathbb{I}}(\hat{G})
.
Clearly,
\hat{I}
can be written as the union
\hat{I}=J_{2}\cup J_{1}\cup\underline{I_{1}}
, where
J_{1}\cup I_{1}\subset{\mathcal{V}}_{1}
,
J_{2}\subset{\mathcal{V}}_{2}
and
J_{2}\cup J_{1}\cup I_{1}\in{\mathbb{I}}\left(\check{G}\right)
. First observe that
J_{1}
and
I_{1}
are necessarily disjoint, as any element
i\in J_{1}\cap I_{1}
would be such that
i{\--}\underline{i}
in
\hat{G}
,
a contradiction to the fact that
\hat{I}\in{\mathbb{I}}(\hat{G})
. Thus,

\displaystyle\hat{\mu}_{\tiny{1/2}}(\hat{I})=\hat{\mu}_{\tiny{1/2}}(J_{2}\cup J_{1})+\hat{\mu}_{\tiny{1/2}}(\underline{I_{1}})

\displaystyle=\hat{\mu}_{\tiny{1/2}}(J_{2}\cup J_{1})+\hat{\mu}_{\tiny{1/2}}(I_{1})

(5.5)

\displaystyle=\hat{\mu}_{\tiny{1/2}}(J_{2}\cup J_{1}\cup I_{1})\leq\mu(J_{2}\cup J_{1}\cup I_{1}).

Now, observe that

\displaystyle\mu\left({\mathcal{E}}\left(J_{2}\cup J_{1}\cup I_{1}\right)\right)-\mu\left(J_{2}\cup J_{1}\cup I_{1}\right)

\displaystyle=\mu\left({\mathcal{E}}\left(J_{2}\cup J_{1}\cup I_{1}\right)\right)-\mu\left(J_{1}\cup I_{1}\right)-\mu\left(J_{2}\right)

\displaystyle=\mu\left({\mathcal{E}}\left(J_{2}\cup J_{1}\cup I_{1}\right)\cap\left(J_{1}\cup I_{1}\right)^{c}\right)-\mu\left(J_{2}\right)

\displaystyle\geq\mu\left({\mathcal{E}}\left(J_{2}\right)\right)-\mu\left(J_{2}\right)>0,

where the second equality is due to the fact that
J_{1}\cup I_{1}\subset{\mathcal{E}}\left(J_{2}\cup J_{1}\cup I_{1}\right)
, because
J_{1}\cup I_{1}\subset{\mathcal{V}}_{1}
.
The weak inequality follows from the fact that
{\mathcal{E}}\left(J_{2}\right)
is disjoint from
J_{1}\cup I_{1}
(because
\hat{I}
is an independent set of
\hat{G}
), and thereby, is included in

{\mathcal{E}}\left(J_{2}\cup J_{1}\cup I_{1}\right)\cap\left(J_{1}\cup I_{1}\right)^{c}
. The last strict inequality follows from the fact that

I_{2}
is an independent set of
G
(and of
\hat{G}
). This, together with (
5.5
), implies that

\displaystyle\hat{\mu}_{\tiny{1/2}}(\hat{I})

\displaystyle<\mu({\mathcal{E}}(J_{2}\cup J_{1}\cup I_{1}))

\displaystyle=\mu({\mathcal{E}}_{1}(J_{2}\cup J_{1}\cup I_{1}))+\mu({\mathcal{E}}_{2}(J_{2}\cup J_{1}\cup I_{1}))

\displaystyle=\hat{\mu}_{\tiny{1/2}}({\mathcal{E}}_{1}(J_{2}\cup J_{1}\cup I_{1}))+\hat{\mu}_{\tiny{1/2}}\left(\underline{{\mathcal{E}}_{1}(J_{2}\cup J_{1}\cup I_{1})}\right)+\hat{\mu}_{\tiny{1/2}}({\mathcal{E}}_{2}(J_{2}\cup J_{1}\cup I_{1}))

\displaystyle=\hat{\mu}_{\tiny{1/2}}\left({\mathcal{E}}_{1}(J_{2}\cup J_{1}\cup I_{1})\cup\underline{{\mathcal{E}}_{1}(J_{2}\cup J_{1}\cup I_{1})}\cup{\mathcal{E}}_{2}(J_{2}\cup J_{1}\cup I_{1})\right)

\displaystyle=\hat{\mu}_{\tiny{1/2}}\left({\mathcal{E}}(J_{2}\cup J_{1}\cup I_{1})\cup\underline{{\mathcal{E}}_{1}(J_{2}\cup J_{1}\cup I_{1})}\right)

\displaystyle=\hat{\mu}_{\tiny{1/2}}\left(\hat{{\mathcal{E}}}(J_{2}\cup J_{1}\cup I_{1})\right)=\hat{\mu}_{\tiny{1/2}}\left(\hat{{\mathcal{E}}}\left(J_{2}\cup J_{1}\cup\underline{I_{1}}\right)\right)=\hat{\mu}_{\tiny{1/2}}(\hat{{\mathcal{E}}}(\hat{I})),

where the first equality is due to the fact that
{\mathcal{E}}={\mathcal{E}}_{1}\cup{\mathcal{E}}_{2}.
The second equality follows from the fact that the equations (
5.3
) and (
5.4
) holds true. The third follows from the disjoint sets. The fourth equality follows from the fact that
{\mathcal{E}}={\mathcal{E}}_{1}\cup{\mathcal{E}}_{2}.
The fifth equality follows from the fact that
\hat{\mathcal{E}}={\mathcal{E}}\cup\underline{{\mathcal{E}}_{1}}.

Which completes the proof.
∎

We can now turn to the proof of Proposition
5.1
:

Proof of Proposition
5.1
.

If
G
is a bipartite graph, then it follows from Theorem 1 in
[
33
]
that
Ncond
(G)
is empty.
Regarding the converse, suppose that
G
is not a bipartite graph. Then,
\hat{G}
cannot be a bipartite graph neither. Indeed, there are two cases:

•

If
G
is a graph (i.e.,
{\mathcal{V}}_{1}=\emptyset
), then
\hat{G}=G
and so is not a bipartite graph.

•

If
G
is not a graph (i.e.,
{\mathcal{V}}_{1}\neq\emptyset
), then, for any
i\in{\mathcal{V}}_{1}
and any
j\in{\mathcal{E}}(i)\setminus\{i\}
,

\hat{G}
includes the triangle

i{\--}\underline{i}{\--}j{\--}i
. (Observe that
j
necessarily exists since
G
is supposed connected with at least two nodes.)
In particular,
\hat{G}
is not a bipartite graph.

As a consequence, again from Theorem 1 in
[
33
]
,
Ncond
(
\hat{G}
) is non-empty and thus, from Lemma
5.2
,
the set
Ncond
(
G
) is also non-empty.
∎

5.4
Proof of Theorem 5.1

Let us recall that the multigraph
G=(\mathcal{V},\mathcal{E})
is connected but is not a bipartite graph, with
|\mathcal{V}|\geq 2
. Then, in particular,
\textsc{Ncond}(G)\neq\emptyset
(cf. Proposition
5.1
). Our product form result, Theorem
5.1
, follows from a reversibility scheme that generalizes to the case of multigraphs, the one constructed in
[
36
]
. In fact, we propose a proof that is simpler, at some points, than the one in
[
36
]
. We reproduce hereafter the main steps of this construction for easy reference, and only develop exhaustively the points that are specific to the present context, or based on different arguments.

Hereafter, we denote by
{\mathbb{P}}_{W}
, the transition operator of the buffer-content Markov chain, that is, for all
w,w^{\prime}\in\mathbb{W},

we write
P_{W}(w,w^{\prime})={\mathbb{P}}\left[W_{n+1}=w^{\prime}\mid W_{n}=w\right],
for any
n\in{\mathbb{N}}
.

5.4.1
Two auxiliary chains

As in section 3.2 of
[
36
]
, we first need to define two auxiliary Markov chains. For this, let us denote by
\overline{\mathcal{V}}
an independent copy of
\mathcal{V}
, i.e., a set with the same cardinal formed with copies of elements of
\mathcal{V}
. We set
\mathbf{V}=\mathcal{V}\cup\overline{\mathcal{V}},
and we define, for
\mathbf{w}\in\mathbf{V}^{*}
,

\displaystyle\mathcal{V}(\mathbf{w})=\{a\in\mathcal{V}:|\mathbf{w}|_{a}>0\},

\displaystyle\overline{\mathcal{V}}(\mathbf{w})=\{\overline{a}\in\overline{\mathcal{V}}:|\mathbf{w}|_{\overline{a}}>0\}.

For
a\in\mathbf{V},
we will use the notation
\overline{\overline{a}}=a.

Definition 5.6
.

We define the
backward detailed chain
as the process
(B_{n})_{n\in{\mathbb{N}}}
with values in
\mathbf{V}^{*}
given by

B_{0}=\varepsilon
and, for any
n\geq 1
,

•

if
W_{n}=\varepsilon
(i.e., all the items arrived up to time
n
are matched at time
n
), then
B_{n}=\varepsilon
,

•

otherwise, let
i(n)\in[\![1,n]\!]
be the arrival time of the oldest item still in the buffer, then, the word
B_{n}
is the word of length
n-i(n)+1
, defined, for any
\ell\in[\![1,n-i(n)+1]\!]
, by

(B_{n})_{\ell}=\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}V_{i(n)+\ell-1}&\mbox{if $V_{i(n)+\ell-1}$ has not been matched up to time $n$};\\
\overline{V_{k}}&\mbox{if $V_{i(n)+\ell-1}$ is matched at or before time $n$, with item $V_{k}$}\\
&\mbox{(where $1\leq k\leq n$)}.\\
\end{array}\right.

In other words, the word
B_{n}
gathers the class indexes of all unmatched items entered up to
n
, at the places corresponding to their arrival times,
and the copies of the class
indexes of the items matched before
n
, but after the arrival of the oldest unmatched item at
n
, at the place corresponding to the arrival time of
their respective match.

Observe that by the construction of
(B_{n})_{n\in{\mathbb{N}}}
, for all
n\in{\mathbb{N}}
, the word
B_{n}
necessarily contains all the letters of
W_{n}
. More precisely, for any
n\in\mathbb{N}
,
W_{n}
is the restriction of the word
B_{n}
to its letters in
\mathcal{V}
.
Furthermore,
(B_{n})_{n\in{\mathbb{N}}}
is also a Markov chain since for any
n\geq 0
, the value of
B_{n+1}
can be deduced from that of
B_{n}
and from the class
V_{n+1}
of the item entered at time
n+1
.

A state
\mathbf{w}\in\mathbf{V}^{*}
is said to be
admissible for
(B_{n})_{n\in{\mathbb{N}}}
if it can be reached by the chain
(B_{n})_{n\in{\mathbb{N}}}
, under the
fcfm
policy. We set

\mathbb{B}=\{\mathbf{w}\in\mathbf{V}^{*}:\mathbf{w}\;\text{is admissible for}\;(B_{n})_{n\in{\mathbb{N}}}\}.

The following result can be proven exactly as Lemma 1 in
[
36
]
.

Lemma 5.3
.

Let
\mathbf{w}=\mathbf{w}_{1}\dots\mathbf{w}_{q}\in\mathbf{V}^{*}.
Then,
\mathbf{w}\in\mathbb{B}
if and only if
\mathbf{w}_{1}\in\mathcal{V}
and for
1\leq i<j\leq q
,

•

if
(\mathbf{w}_{i},\mathbf{w}_{j})\in\mathcal{V}^{2}
, then
\mathbf{w}_{i}{\not\!\!\--}\mathbf{w}_{j}
,

•

if
(\mathbf{w}_{i},\mathbf{w}_{j})\in\mathcal{V}\times\overline{\mathcal{V}}
, then
\mathbf{w}_{i}{\not\!\!\--}{\overline{\mathbf{w}}_{j}}
.

As a consequence of Lemma
5.3
, any word
\mathbf{w}\in\mathbb{B}
can be written as

\mathbf{w}=b_{1}\overline{a_{11}}\overline{a_{12}}\dots\overline{a_{1k_{1}}}b_{2}\overline{a_{21}}\overline{a_{22}}\dots\overline{a_{2k_{2}}}b_{3}\dots b_{q}\overline{a_{q1}}\dots\overline{a_{qk_{q}}},

where
q,k_{1},\ldots,k_{q}\in{\mathbb{N}},

b_{1},\ldots,b_{q}\in\mathcal{V}
,
a_{ij}\in\mathcal{V}
for
1\leq i\leq q,1\leq j\leq k_{i}
, and

\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}\{b_{1},\dots,b_{q}\}=\mathcal{V}(\mathbf{w})\in\mathbb{I}\left(\check{G}\right),\\
\forall i\in[\![1,q]\!],\;b_{i}\in{\mathcal{V}}_{1}\;\Rightarrow\left[\forall j\neq i,\;b_{i}\neq b_{j}\right],\\
\forall i\in[\![1,q]\!],\;\forall j\in[\![1,k_{i}]\!],\;a_{ij}\in\mathcal{E}(\{b_{1},\dots,b_{i}\})^{c}.\end{array}\right.

The transition operator of the chain
\left\{B_{n};\,n\in{\mathbb{N}}\right\}
is denoted by
{\mathbb{P}}_{B}
, that is, for all
\mathbf{w},\mathbf{w}^{\prime}\in\mathbb{B}
, we write

{\mathbb{P}}_{B}(\mathbf{w},\mathbf{w}^{\prime})={\mathbb{P}}\left[B_{n+1}=\mathbf{w}^{\prime}\mid B_{n}=\mathbf{w}\right]
, for all
n\in{\mathbb{N}}
.

Definition 5.7
.

We define the
forward detailed chain
as the process
(F_{n})_{n\in{\mathbb{N}}}
with values in
\mathbf{V}^{*}
given by
F_{0}=\varepsilon
(the empty word) and, for any
n\geq 1
,

•

if
W_{n}=\varepsilon
(i.e., all the items arrived up to time
n
are matched at time
n
), then
F_{n}=\varepsilon
,

•

otherwise, let
\mathscr{U}_{n}
be the set of items arrived before time
n
that are not matched at time
n
(note that
\mathscr{U}_{n}
is non-empty since
W_{n}\neq\varepsilon
). Also, set

j(n)=\sup\left\{m\geq n+1:V_{m}\mbox{ is matched with an element of }\mathscr{U}_{n}\right\}.

Observe that
j(n)
is possibly infinite. Then,
if
j(n)
is finite,
F_{n}
is the word of
\mathbf{V}^{*}
of length
j(n)-n
(respectively of
A^{{\mathbb{N}}}
of length
+\infty
, if
j(n)=+\infty)
,
such that for any
\ell\in[\![1,j(n)-n]\!]
(respectively
\ell\in{\mathbb{N}}_{+})
,

(F_{n})_{\ell}=\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}V_{n+\ell}&\mbox{if $V_{n+\ell}$ is not matched with an item arrived up to $n$};\\
\overline{V_{k}}&\mbox{if $V_{n+\ell}$ is matched with item $V_{k}$, where $1\leq k\leq n$}.\end{array}\right.

In other words, the word
F_{n}
contains the copies of all the class indexes of the items entered up to time
n
and matched after
n
, at the place corresponding to the arrival time of their respective match,
together with the class indexes of all items entered after
n
and before the last item matched with an item entered up to
n
, and not matched with an element entered before
n
, if any, at the place corresponding to their arrival time.
Similarly to
[
36
]
, we make the three following simple observations:

•

If
F_{n}\in\mathbf{V}^{*}
is finite, then
(F_{n})_{j(n)-n}\in\overline{{\mathcal{V}}}
;

•

\left\{F_{n};\,n\in{\mathbb{N}}\right\}
is a Markov chain;

•

If
F_{n}
is a.s. an element of
\mathbf{V}^{*}
for all
n\in{\mathbb{N}}
.

As for the backward chain, we say that a state
\mathbf{w}\in\mathbf{V}^{*}
is
admissible for
(F_{n})_{n\in{\mathbb{N}}}
if it can be reached by the chain
(F_{n})_{n\in{\mathbb{N}}}
, under the
fcfm
policy. Then, we set

\mathbb{F}=\{\mathbf{w}\in\mathbf{V}^{*}:\mathbf{w}\;\text{is admissible for}\;(F_{n})_{n\in{\mathbb{N}}}\}

and we denote by
{\mathbb{P}}_{F}
the transition operator of the chain
\left\{F_{n};\,n\in{\mathbb{N}}\right\}
on
\mathbb{F}
.
For any word
\mathbf{w}=\mathbf{w}_{1}\dots\mathbf{w}_{n}\in\mathbf{V}^{*}
, let us define its reversed-copy by
\overleftarrow{\overline{\mathbf{w}}}=\overline{\mathbf{w}_{n}}\dots\overline{\mathbf{w}_{1}}\in\mathbf{V}^{*}.
Note that the map

\Psi:\mathbf{V}^{*}\to\mathbf{V}^{*},\mathbf{w}\mapsto\overleftarrow{\overline{\mathbf{w}}}
satisfies
\Psi\circ\Psi=Id_{\mathbf{V}^{*}}
. Thus,
\Psi
is a bijection and its inverse function is
\Psi^{-1}=\Psi
.

Lemma 5.4
.

The map

\Phi:\begin{cases}{\mathbb{B}}\longrightarrow{\mathbb{F}}\\
\mathbf{w}\longmapsto\Psi(\mathbf{w})=\overleftarrow{\overline{\mathbf{w}}}\end{cases}

is well-defined and bijective.

Proof.

Exactly as in Lemma 2 in
[
36
]
, it can be proven that
\mathbf{w}\in\mathbb{F}
if and only if
\Psi(\mathbf{w})\in\mathbb{B}
.
This guarantees that the mapping
\Phi
is well-defined and surjective. It is injective because
\Psi
clearly is so.
∎

Let us define a measure
\nu
on
\mathbf{V}^{*}
by
\nu(\varepsilon)=1
and

\forall\mathbf{w}\in\mathbf{V}^{*}\setminus\{\varepsilon\},\;\nu(\mathbf{w})=\prod\limits_{i=1}^{|\mathcal{V}|}\mu(i)^{|\mathbf{w}|_{i}+|\overline{\mathbf{w}}|_{i}}.

(5.6)

We can use the measure
\nu
defined above to establish the following link between the dynamics of the chains
(B_{n})_{n\in{\mathbb{N}}}
and
(F_{n})_{n\in{\mathbb{N}}}
.
The following result can be established exactly as Lemma 3 in
[
36
]
,

Proposition 5.4
.

For any
(\mathbf{w},\mathbf{w^{\prime}})\in\mathbb{B}^{2}
, we have that

\nu(\mathbf{w}){\mathbb{P}}_{B}(\mathbf{w},\mathbf{w}^{\prime})=\nu\left(\overleftarrow{\overline{\mathbf{w^{\prime}}}}\right){\mathbb{P}}_{F}\left(\overleftarrow{\overline{\mathbf{w}^{\prime}}},\overleftarrow{\overline{\mathbf{w}}}\right).

5.4.2
Positive recurrence of
(B_{n})_{n\in{\mathbb{N}}}
and
(F_{n})_{n\in{\mathbb{N}}}
.

We will exploit the local balance equations of Proposition
5.4
to derive stationary distributions of these two Markov chains.
To this end, the following technical lemma will simplify the proofs.

Lemma 5.5
.

The measure
\nu
defined by (
5.6
) satisfies the following properties:

1.

For any
\mathcal{A}\subset\mathbf{V}=\mathcal{V}\cup\overline{\mathcal{V}}
, we have
\nu(\mathcal{A})=\mu(\mathcal{V}(\mathcal{A}))+\mu\left(\overline{\overline{\mathcal{V}}(\mathcal{A})}\right)
.

2.

For any
\mathcal{A}_{1},\ldots,\mathcal{A}_{n}\subset\mathbf{V},\;\nu(\mathcal{A}_{1}\dots\mathcal{A}_{n})=\nu(\mathcal{A}_{1})\dots\nu(\mathcal{A}_{n})
.
In particular,
\nu(\mathcal{A}^{k})=\nu(\mathcal{A})^{k}
.

3.

If
\mathcal{A}\subset\mathbf{V}
is such that
\nu(\mathcal{A})<1
, then
\nu(\mathcal{A}^{*})={1\over 1-\nu(\mathcal{A})}
.

Proof.

The first point follows from the definition of
\nu
and the second point is a direct consequence of its multiplicative structure.
Regarding the third point, observe that
\mathcal{A}^{*}=\cup_{k\in{\mathbb{N}}}\mathcal{A}^{k}
, so that

\nu(\mathcal{A}^{*})=\sum_{k\in{\mathbb{N}}}\nu\left(\mathcal{A}^{k}\right)=\sum_{k\in{\mathbb{N}}}\nu(\mathcal{A})^{k}.

∎

We can now state the following result,

Proposition 5.5
.

Suppose that
\mu\in\textsc{Ncond}(G)
. Then,
the chains
(B_{n})_{n\in{\mathbb{N}}}
and
(F_{n})_{n\in{\mathbb{N}}}
are positively recurrent and admit respectively the restrictions on
\mathbb{B}
and on
\mathbb{F}

of
\nu
(that is,
\nu_{B}(\mathbf{w})=\nu_{F}(\Phi(\mathbf{w}))=\nu(\mathbf{w})
, for any
\mathbf{w}\in\mathbb{B})
as unique stationary measure (up to a multiplicative constant), respectively on
\mathbb{B}
and
\mathbb{F}
.

Proof.

Let
\mu\in\textsc{Ncond}(G)
.

Step 1
: we first prove that
\nu_{B}
is a stationary measure for the chain
(B_{n})_{n\in{\mathbb{N}}}.

For this, let us fix
\mathbf{w^{\prime}}\in\mathbb{B}
. Then we have that

\displaystyle\displaystyle\sum\limits_{\mathbf{w}\in\mathbb{B}}\frac{{\mathbb{P}}_{B}(\mathbf{w},\mathbf{w}^{\prime})\nu_{B}(\mathbf{w})}{\nu_{B}(\mathbf{w^{\prime}})}

\displaystyle=\sum\limits_{\mathbf{w}\in\mathbb{B}}\frac{{\mathbb{P}}_{F}\left(\overleftarrow{\overline{\mathbf{w}^{\prime}}},\overleftarrow{\overline{\mathbf{w}}}\right)\nu_{B}\left(\overleftarrow{\overline{\mathbf{w^{\prime}}}}\right)}{\nu_{B}(\mathbf{w^{\prime}})}

\displaystyle=\sum\limits_{\mathbf{w}\in\mathbb{B}}{\mathbb{P}}_{F}\left(\overleftarrow{\overline{\mathbf{w}^{\prime}}},\overleftarrow{\overline{\mathbf{w}}}\right)=1,

where the first equality follows from Proposition
5.4
,
the second from the fact that

\nu_{B}\left(\overleftarrow{\overline{\mathbf{w^{\prime}}}}\right)=\nu_{B}(\mathbf{w^{\prime}})
and the last, from Lemma
5.4
.
Thus, for all
\mathbf{w}^{\prime}\in\mathbb{B}
, we have that

\nu_{B}(\mathbf{w^{\prime}})=\sum_{\mathbf{w}\in\mathbb{B}}{\mathbb{P}}_{B}(\mathbf{w},\mathbf{w}^{\prime})\nu_{B}(\mathbf{w}),

which means exactly that
\nu_{B}
is a stationary measure for the chain
(B_{n})_{n\in{\mathbb{N}}}
.

Step 2
: we now prove that
\nu_{B}(\mathbb{B})<\infty
.

By Lemma
5.3
, we know that

\mathbf{w}\in\mathbb{B}\setminus\{\varepsilon\}
if and only if
\mathbf{w}
belongs to a set

b_{1}\,\mathcal{A}^{*}_{1}\,b_{2}\,\mathcal{A}^{*}_{2}\dots b_{q}\,\mathcal{A}^{*}_{q}\\
=\left\{w\in\mathbf{V}^{*}:w=b_{1}w^{1}b_{2}w^{2}\dots b_{q}w^{q};\,w^{i}\in\mathcal{A}^{*}_{i}\mbox{, for all }i\in\llbracket 1,q\rrbracket\right\},\quad q\geq 1,

(5.7)

where
b_{1},\ldots,b_{q}
are elements of

\mathcal{V}
such that
\{b_{1},\dots,b_{q}\}\in\mathbb{I}\left(\check{G}\right)
and such that for all distinct
i,j
in
\llbracket 1,q\rrbracket
,

b_{i}\in{\mathcal{V}}_{1}
implies that
b_{i}\neq b_{j}
, and where we denote

\mathcal{A}_{i}=\overline{\mathcal{E}(\{b_{1},\dots,b_{i}\})^{c}},\quad i\in\llbracket 1,q\rrbracket.

Equivalently, by highlighting only the first occurrence of each letter of
\mathcal{V}
appearing in
\mathbf{w}
and employing a similar notation to (
5.7
)
we obtain that
\mathbf{w}\in\mathbb{B}\setminus\{\varepsilon\}
if and only if
\mathbf{w}
belongs to some set of the form

{\mathbb{C}}_{I,\sigma}=e_{\sigma(1)}\,\mathcal{B}^{*}_{\sigma(1)}\,e_{\sigma(2)}\,\mathcal{B}^{*}_{\sigma(2)}\dots e_{\sigma(|I|)}\,\mathcal{B}^{*}_{\sigma(|I|)},

where
I=\left\{e_{1},...,e_{|I|}\right\}\in\mathbb{I}\left(\check{G}\right)
,

\sigma\in\mathfrak{S}_{|I|}
,
and where we denote

\mathcal{B}_{\sigma(i)}=\overline{\mathcal{E}(\{e_{\sigma(1)},\dots,e_{\sigma(i)}\})^{c}}\,\cup\,(\{e_{\sigma(1)},\ldots,e_{\sigma(i)}\}\cap\mathcal{V}_{2}),\quad i\in\llbracket 1,|I|\rrbracket.

In view of assertion (1) of Lemma
5.5
, we have that for all
i\in\llbracket 1,k\rrbracket
,

\displaystyle\nu_{B}(\mathcal{B}_{\sigma(i)})

\displaystyle=\mu(\mathcal{E}(\{e_{\sigma(1)},\dots,e_{\sigma(i)}\}^{c})+\mu(\{e_{\sigma(1)},\dots,e_{\sigma(i)}\}\cap\mathcal{V}_{2})

\displaystyle=1-\mu(\mathcal{E}(\{e_{\sigma(1)},\dots,e_{\sigma(i)}\}))+\mu(\{e_{\sigma(1)},\dots,e_{\sigma(i)}\}\cap\mathcal{V}_{2}).

Since
\{e_{\sigma(1)},\dots,e_{\sigma(i)}\}\in\mathbb{I}(\check{G})
, we have, by definition, that

\{e_{\sigma(1)},\dots,e_{\sigma(i)}\}\cap\mathcal{V}_{2}\in\mathbb{I}(G)
and since the measure
\mu
satisfies
\textsc{Ncond}(G)
, it follows that

\displaystyle\mu\left(\{e_{\sigma(1)},\dots,e_{\sigma(i)}\}\cap\mathcal{V}_{2}\right)

\displaystyle<\mu\left({\mathcal{E}}\left(\{e_{\sigma(1)},\dots,e_{\sigma(i)}\}\cap\mathcal{V}_{2}\right)\right)

\displaystyle\leq\mu\left({\mathcal{E}}\left(\{e_{\sigma(1)},\dots,e_{\sigma(i)}\}\right)\right)

and thereby, that

\nu_{B}(\mathcal{B}_{i})<1
. As a conclusion, applying successively all assertions of Lemma
5.5
, we obtain that for all such
I
and
\sigma
,

\nu_{B}({\mathbb{C}}_{I,\sigma})=\prod\limits_{i=1}^{|I|}\frac{\mu(e_{\sigma(i)})}{\mu(\mathcal{E}(\{e_{\sigma(1)},\dots,e_{\sigma(i)}\}))-\mu(\{e_{\sigma(1)},\dots,e_{\sigma(i)}\}\cap{\mathcal{V}}_{2})}.

The set
\mathbb{B}
is the disjoint union of the sets
{\mathbb{C}}_{I,\sigma}
, for
I
in the finite set
\mathbb{I}\left(\check{G}\right)
, and
\sigma

in the finite set
\mathfrak{S}_{|I|}
. It follows that
\nu_{B}(\mathbb{B})
is finite, and given by

\displaystyle\nu_{B}(\mathbb{B})

\displaystyle=\nu_{B}(\varepsilon)+\sum\limits_{I\in\mathbb{I}\left(\check{G}\right)}\sum\limits_{\sigma\in\mathfrak{S}_{|I|}}\nu_{B}({\mathbb{C}}_{I,\sigma})

\displaystyle=1+\sum\limits_{I\in\mathbb{I}\left(\check{G}\right)}\sum\limits_{\sigma\in\mathfrak{S}_{|I|}}\prod\limits_{i=1}^{|I|}\frac{\mu(e_{\sigma(i)})}{\mu(\mathcal{E}(\{e_{\sigma(1)},\dots,e_{\sigma(i)}\}))-\mu(\{e_{\sigma(1)},\dots,e_{\sigma(i)}\}\cap{\mathcal{V}}_{2})}.

(5.8)

Step 3
: we conclude with the positive recurrence of the two chains.

By the results above, the chain
(B_{n})_{n\in{\mathbb{N}}}
has a stationary probability distribution on
\mathbb{B}
, which is given by the measure
\nu_{B}
normalized by
\nu_{B}(\mathbb{B})
.

Observe that the chain is irreducible on
\mathbb{B}
. To see this, let
\mathbf{w}\in\mathbb{B}
and first observe that the empty word
\varepsilon
leads to
\mathbf{w}
with positive probability for the transitions of
\left\{B_{n};\,n\in{\mathbb{N}}\right\}

(this is the constructive argument proving Lemma
5.3
- see the proof of Lemma 1 in
[
36
]
). Conversely, denoting by
b_{1},\dots,b_{q}
the elements of
{\mathcal{V}}(\mathbf{w})
, it is easy to see that the word
\mathbf{w}
leads to the empty word with positive probability for the transitions of
\left\{B_{n};\,n\in{\mathbb{N}}\right\}
:
indeed, by the definition of the policy
fcfm
, if the chain is in the state
\mathbf{w}
, then it will reach the empty state after exactly
q
steps,
by seeing the successive arrivals of
q
elements of respective classes in
{\mathcal{E}}(b_{1})
,
{\mathcal{E}}(b_{2}),\dots,{\mathcal{E}}(b_{q})
, which concludes the proof
of irreducibility.

It then follows that the chain
\left\{B_{n};\,n\in{\mathbb{N}}\right\}
is positively recurrent on
\mathbb{B}
and that its stationary probability distribution is unique. Consequently,
\nu_{B}
is the unique stationary measure (up to a multiplicative constant) of the chain
(B_{n})_{n\in{\mathbb{N}}}
.

Now, as in step 1, we obtain that for all
\mathbf{w}^{\prime}\in\mathbb{B}
,

\nu_{F}(\Phi(\mathbf{w^{\prime}}))=\sum\limits_{\mathbf{w}\in\mathbb{B}}{\mathbb{P}}_{F}(\Phi(\mathbf{w}),\Phi(\mathbf{w}^{\prime}))\nu_{F}(\Phi(\mathbf{w})).

Using Lemma
5.4
, we deduce that
\nu_{F}
is a stationary measure for the chain
(F_{n})_{n\in{\mathbb{N}}}
.
Then, step 2 shows equivalently that
\nu_{F}(\mathbb{F})<\infty
.
So, the chain
(F_{n})_{n\in{\mathbb{N}}}
has a stationary probability distribution on
\mathbb{F}
, which is given by the measure
\nu_{F}
normalized by
\nu_{F}(\mathbb{F})
.

Similarly as above, we can check that the chain
\left\{F_{n};\,n\in{\mathbb{N}}\right\}
is irreducible on
\mathbb{F}
. First, the empty word leads with positive probability
to any element
\mathbf{w}\in\mathbb{F}
, as can be checked using the same constructive argument as in the proof of Lemma
5.3
.
Conversely, suppose that the chain
\left\{F_{n};\,n\in{\mathbb{N}}\right\}
is at time
n
in a state

\mathbf{w}=a_{qk_{q}}\dots a_{q1}\overline{a_{q}}\dots\overline{a_{3}}a_{2k_{2}}\dots a_{21}\overline{a_{2}}a_{1k_{1}}\dots a_{11}\overline{a_{1}}\in\mathbb{F}

and let
r=q+\sum_{i=1}^{q}k_{i}
be the length of
\mathbf{w}
. Then, going forward in time, perform the
fcfm
matching of
the ‘unmatched’ elements of respective classes in
{\mathcal{V}}(\mathbf{w})
. Say there remains in the system, at time
n+r
,
\ell
unmatched elements denoted
c_{1},c_{2},\dots,c_{\ell}
in their order of arrivals. Then, the chain can return to the empty state in particular if the first
\ell
arrivals
after time
n+r
(excluded) are of respective classes in
{\mathcal{E}}(c_{1})
,
{\mathcal{E}}(c_{2}),\dots,{\mathcal{E}}(c_{\ell})
. This concludes the proof of irreducibility.

As a consequence, the chain
\left\{F_{n};\,n\in{\mathbb{N}}\right\}
is positively recurrent on
\mathbb{F}
and its stationary probability distribution is unique.
Consequently,
\nu_{F}
is the unique stationary measure (up to a multiplicative constant) of the chain
(F_{n})_{n\in{\mathbb{N}}}
, which concludes the proof.
∎

5.4.3
Positive recurrence of
(W_{n})_{n\in{\mathbb{N}}}

The Markov chain
(W_{n})_{n\in{\mathbb{N}}}
can be seen as the projection of the chain
(B_{n})_{n\in{\mathbb{N}}}
on
\mathcal{V}^{*}
. In order to obtain the stationary probability distribution of
(W_{n})_{n\in{\mathbb{N}}}
from the one of
(B_{n})_{n\in{\mathbb{N}}}
, we will use the following lemma:

Lemma 5.6
.

Let
{\mathbb{P}}_{Y}
and
{\mathbb{P}}_{Y^{\prime}}
be the transition matrices of two homogeneous
Markov chains
\left\{Y_{n};\,n\in{\mathbb{N}}\right\}
and
\left\{Y^{\prime}_{n};\,n\in{\mathbb{N}}\right\}
with values in some countable sets
S
and
S^{\prime}
respectively, and consider a map

p:S\to S^{\prime}
satisfying

\forall a^{\prime},b^{\prime}\in S^{\prime},\;\forall a\in p^{-1}(\{a^{\prime}\}),{\mathbb{P}}_{Y}(a,p^{-1}(\{b^{\prime}\}))={\mathbb{P}}_{Y^{\prime}}(a^{\prime},b^{\prime}).

Then, if a measure
\mu
is invariant for
{\mathbb{P}}_{Y}
, the measure
\mu^{\prime}
defined by
\mu^{\prime}(a^{\prime})=\mu(p^{-1}(\{a^{\prime}\}))
for all
a^{\prime}\in S^{\prime}
,
is an invariant measure for
{\mathbb{P}}_{Y^{\prime}}
on
S^{\prime}
.

Proof.

Let
\mu
be an invariant measure for
{\mathbb{P}}_{Y}
, and let
b^{\prime}\in S^{\prime}
. We have

\displaystyle\sum_{a^{\prime}\in S^{\prime}}\mu^{\prime}(a^{\prime}){\mathbb{P}}_{Y^{\prime}}(a^{\prime},b^{\prime})

\displaystyle=\sum_{a^{\prime}\in S^{\prime}}\left(\sum_{a\in p^{-1}(\{a^{\prime}\})}\mu(a)\right){\mathbb{P}}_{Y^{\prime}}(a^{\prime},b^{\prime})

\displaystyle=\sum_{a^{\prime}\in S^{\prime}}\sum_{a\in p^{-1}(\{a^{\prime}\})}\mu(a){\mathbb{P}}_{Y}(a,p^{-1}(\{b^{\prime}\}))

\displaystyle=\sum_{s\in S}\mu(s){\mathbb{P}}_{Y}(s,p^{-1}(\{b^{\prime}\}))=\mu(p^{-1}(\{b^{\prime}\}))=\mu^{\prime}(b^{\prime}),

meaning that
\mu^{\prime}
is invariant for
{\mathbb{P}}_{Y^{\prime}}
.
∎

For
\mu\in\textsc{Ncond}(G)
, let us denote by
\Pi_{B}
the unique stationary probability law associed to the chain
(B_{n})_{n\in{\mathbb{N}}}
(cf. Prop.
5.5
). It is defined by

\forall\mathbf{w}\in\mathbb{B},\;\Pi_{B}(\mathbf{w})=\alpha\nu_{B}(\mathbf{w}),

where
\alpha=(\nu_{B}(\mathbb{B}))^{-1}
is given by (
5.2
) in view of (
5.8
).
Let us now introduce the projection

p:\begin{cases}\mathbb{B}\longrightarrow\mathbb{W}\\
\mathbf{w}\longmapsto\mathbf{w}|_{\mathcal{V}},\end{cases}

which is well-defined from Lemma
5.3
. We have the following result:

Proposition 5.6
.

Let
\mu\in\textsc{Ncond}(G)
. Then, the Markov chain
(W_{n})_{n\in{\mathbb{N}}}
is positively recurrent, and its unique stationary probability distribution is the measure
\Pi_{W}
defined on
\mathbb{W}
by:

\forall w\in\mathbb{W},\;\Pi_{W}(w)=\Pi_{B}(p^{-1}(w))=\sum_{\mathbf{w}\in\mathbb{B}\;:\;\mathbf{w}|_{\mathcal{V}}=w}\Pi_{B}(\mathbf{w}).

Proof.

Let
\mu\in\textsc{Ncond}(G)
. We can apply Lemma
5.6
to
{\mathbb{P}}_{B}
and
{\mathbb{P}}_{W}
to prove that
\Pi_{W}
is a stationary distribution for
(W_{n})_{n\in{\mathbb{N}}}
.
Indeed, using the fact that for any
n\in\mathbb{N}
,
W_{n}
is the restriction of the word
B_{n}
to its letters in
\mathcal{V}
, we have that

\forall w,w^{\prime}\in\mathbb{W},\;\forall\mathbf{w}\in p^{-1}(\{w\}),\;{\mathbb{P}}_{B}(\mathbf{w},p^{-1}(\{w^{\prime}\}))={\mathbb{P}}_{W}(w,w^{\prime}).

The measure
\Pi_{W}
is a probability distribution on
\mathbb{W}
, since
\Pi_{W}(\mathbb{W})=\Pi_{B}(p^{-1}(\mathbb{W}))=\Pi_{B}(\mathbb{B})=1
.
The chain
(W_{n})_{n\in{\mathbb{N}}}
being irreducible on
\mathbb{W}
, it follows that
\Pi_{W}
is its unique stationary probability distribution.
∎

5.4.4
Concluding the proof

We first show that
\textsc{Stab}(G,\textsc{fcfm})=\textsc{Ncond}(G)
.
First, we know from Proposition
5.2
that
\textsc{Stab}(G,\textsc{fcfm})\subset\textsc{Ncond}(G).
Also,
from Proposition
5.1
,
\textsc{Ncond}(G)\neq\emptyset
, since
G
is not a bipartite graph.
Then, for all
\mu\in\textsc{Ncond}(G)
, by Proposition
5.6
, the chain
(W_{n})_{n\in{\mathbb{N}}}
is positively recurrent on
\mathbb{W}
.
So,
\textsc{Ncond}(G)\subset\textsc{Stab}(G,\textsc{fcfm})
, and therefore
\textsc{Stab}(G,\textsc{fcfm})=\textsc{Ncond}(G)
.

We now fix
\mu\in\textsc{Ncond}(G)
, and compute explicitly the unique stationary probability distribution
\Pi_{W}
of the chain

(W_{n})_{n\in{\mathbb{N}}}
.
First, if
w=\varepsilon
, then
p^{-1}(\{w\})=\varepsilon
and
\Pi_{W}(\varepsilon)=\alpha
, given by (
5.2
).
Now, fix
w\neq\varepsilon
in
\mathbb{W}
.
By (
5.7
), we know that if
w=w_{1}\dots w_{q}\in\mathbb{W}
,
q\geq 1
, then

p^{-1}(\{w\})=w_{1}\,\mathcal{A}^{*}_{1}\,w_{2}\,\mathcal{A}^{*}_{2}\dots w_{q}\,\mathcal{A}^{*}_{q},
with
\mathcal{A}_{i}=\overline{\mathcal{E}(\{w_{1},\dots,w_{i}\})^{c}}
, for all
i\in\llbracket 1,q\rrbracket
.
Applying Lemma
5.5
and observing that
for all
i
,
\mu\left(\overline{\mathcal{A}_{i}}\right)<1
since
\mathcal{A}_{i}\varsubsetneq\overline{\mathcal{V}}
, it follows that

\displaystyle\Pi_{W}(w)

\displaystyle=\Pi_{B}(w_{1}\,\mathcal{A}^{*}_{1}\,w_{2}\,\mathcal{A}^{*}_{2}\dots w_{q}\,\mathcal{A}^{*}_{q})

\displaystyle=\alpha\nu_{B}(w_{1}\,\mathcal{A}^{*}_{1}\,w_{2}\,\mathcal{A}^{*}_{2}\dots w_{q}\,\mathcal{A}^{*}_{q})

\displaystyle=\alpha\prod_{i=1}^{q}{\mu(w_{i})\over 1-\mu\left(\overline{\mathcal{A}_{i}}\right)}=\alpha\prod_{i=1}^{q}{\mu(w_{i})\over\mu(\mathcal{E}(\{w_{1},\dots,w_{i}\}))}\cdot

The proof is complete.

5.5
Remaining proofs

Throughout the section
G
is a connected multigraph,
\check{G}
is its maximal subgraph and
\hat{G}
denotes its minimal blow-up graph.
To simply compare a
(G,\Phi,\mu)
system with the two corresponding matching models on graphs

(\hat{G},\hat{\Phi},\hat{\mu})
and
(\check{G},\check{\Phi},\mu)
, let us add a “hat” (resp. a “check”)
to all characteristics of the second (resp. the third) system: in particular, we denote, for all
n
, by
\hat{V}_{n}
(resp.
\check{V}_{n}
), the class of the item entering in the

(\hat{G},\hat{\Phi},\hat{\mu})

(
resp.
(\check{G},\check{\Phi},\mu)
)
system at time
n
.
The natural Markov chain of the system is then denoted by
(\hat{W}_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}

(
resp.
(\check{W}_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
)

and its state space, by
\hat{{\mathbb{W}}}

(
resp.
\check{{\mathbb{W}}}
)
. Specifically,

\displaystyle\hat{\mathbb{W}}

\displaystyle=\Bigl\{w\in\left({\mathcal{V}}\cup\underline{{\mathcal{V}}_{1}}\right)^{*}\;:\;\forall i\neq j\;\text{s.t.}\;(i,j)\in\hat{\mathcal{E}},\;|w|_{i}|w|_{j}=0\Bigr\};

\displaystyle\check{\mathbb{W}}

\displaystyle=\Bigl\{w\in{\mathcal{V}}^{*}\;:\;\forall i\neq j\;\text{s.t.}\;(i,j)\in\check{\mathcal{E}},\;|w|_{i}|w|_{j}=0\Bigr\}.

Observe that we have
\mathbb{W}\subset\check{\mathbb{W}}\subset\hat{\mathbb{W}}
.

For any measurable mapping
F:{\mathbb{W}}\to{\mathbb{R}}
(resp.
\check{{\mathbb{W}}}\to{\mathbb{R}}
,
\hat{{\mathbb{W}}}\to{\mathbb{R}}
) and any given
w\in{\mathbb{W}}
(resp.
\hat{w}\in\hat{{\mathbb{W}}}
,
\check{w}\in\check{{\mathbb{W}}}
), we denote by

\Delta F^{\Phi}_{\mu}(w)
(resp.
\hat{\Delta}F^{\hat{\Phi}}_{\hat{\mu}}(\hat{w})
,
\check{\Delta}F^{\check{\Phi}}_{\check{\mu}}(\check{w})
) the drift of the chain
\left\{W_{n};\,n\in{\mathbb{N}}\right\}
(resp.
\left\{\hat{W}_{n};\,n\in{\mathbb{N}}\right\}
,

\left\{\check{W}_{n};\,n\in{\mathbb{N}}\right\}
) starting from
w
(resp.
\hat{w}
,
\check{w}
) for a
(G,\Phi,\mu)
(resp.
(\hat{G},\hat{\Phi},\hat{\mu})
,
(\check{G},\check{\Phi},\check{\mu})
) system.
In other words, for any
n\in{\mathbb{N}}
we denote

\displaystyle\Delta^{\Phi}_{\mu}F(w)

\displaystyle={\mathbb{E}}\left[F(W_{n+1})-F(W_{n})\,\big|\,W_{n}=w\right];

\displaystyle\hat{\Delta}^{\hat{\Phi}}_{\hat{\mu}}F(\hat{w})

\displaystyle={\mathbb{E}}\left[F(\hat{W}_{n+1})-F(\hat{W}_{n})\,\big|\,\hat{W}_{n}=\hat{w}\right];

\displaystyle\check{\Delta}^{\check{\Phi}}_{\check{\mu}}F(\check{w})

\displaystyle={\mathbb{E}}\left[F(\check{W}_{n+1})-F(\check{W}_{n})\,\big|\,\check{W}_{n}=\check{w}\right].

5.5.1
Drift inequalities

Consider the following mappings,

Q:\begin{cases}\hat{\mathbb{W}}&\longrightarrow{\mathbb{R}}^{+}\\
\hat{w}&\longmapsto\sum\limits_{i=1}^{|{\mathcal{V}}|}\left(|\hat{w}|_{i}\right)^{2}+\sum\limits_{i=1}^{|{\mathcal{V}}_{1}|}\left(|\hat{w}|_{\underline{i}}\right)^{2};\end{cases}

(5.9)

L:\begin{cases}\hat{\mathbb{W}}&\longrightarrow{\mathbb{R}}^{+}\\
\hat{w}&\longmapsto\sum\limits_{i=1}^{|{\mathcal{V}}|}\alpha_{i}|\hat{w}|_{i}+\sum\limits_{i=1}^{|{\mathcal{V}}_{1}|}\alpha_{\underline{i}}|\hat{w}|_{\underline{i}},\end{cases}

(5.10)

with
\alpha_{i},\alpha_{\underline{i}}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{+}
and
\alpha_{i}=\alpha_{\underline{i}}
for all
i\in{\mathcal{V}}_{1}.

Where it follows from the observation above that
Q
and
L
are well defined also on
\mathbb{W}
and
\check{\mathbb{W}}
.

Definition 5.8
.

Let
\Phi
and
\hat{\Phi}
be two admissible matching policies, respectively on
G
and
\hat{G}
.
We say that
\hat{\Phi}
extends
\Phi
on
\hat{G}
if, for any
\mu\in\mathscr{M}({{\mathcal{V}}})
and
\hat{\mu}\in\mathscr{M}(\hat{\mathcal{V}})
, whenever both systems
(G,\Phi,\mu)
and
(\hat{G},\hat{\Phi},\hat{\mu})
are in the same state
w\in\mathbb{W}
and welcome the same arrival,
\Phi
and
\hat{\Phi}
induce the same choice of match, if any.

We have the following result,

Proposition 5.7
.

Let
\Phi
be an admissible policy on
G
and
\mu\in\mathscr{M}({\mathcal{V}})
.
Let
\hat{\Phi}
be a matching policy extending
\Phi
on
\hat{G}
and
\hat{\mu}
be a measure extending
\mu
on
\hat{\mathcal{V}}
.
Then, for all
w\in{\mathbb{W}}
we have that

\Delta^{\Phi}_{\mu}Q(w)\leq\hat{\Delta}^{\hat{\Phi}}_{\hat{\mu}}Q(w).

Proof.

Fix
w\in{\mathbb{W}}
throughout the proof. Recall that for all
i\in{\mathcal{V}}_{1}
(if any), we have that
|w|_{i}\in\{0,1\}
, and let us set

\displaystyle\mathcal{O}_{w}

\displaystyle=\{i\in{\mathcal{V}}_{1}:|w|_{i}=1\},

\displaystyle\mathcal{Z}_{w}

\displaystyle=\{i\in{\mathcal{V}}_{1}:|w|_{i}=0\mbox{ and }|w|_{j}=0\mbox{, for any }j\in{\mathcal{E}}(i)\}.

First, for any
i\in{\mathcal{V}}_{2}
, an incoming item of class
i
finding the system
(G,\Phi,\mu)
in a state
w
finds the same possible matches (if any) as an incoming item of class
i
finding the system

(\hat{G},\hat{\Phi},\hat{\mu})
in a state
w
.
As
\hat{\Phi}
extends
\Phi
, the choice of the match (if any) of the incoming item of class
i
is then the same, or follows the same distribution in case of a draw, in both systems.
Thus, for all
n\in\mathbb{N}
, as
|\hat{W}_{n+1}|_{\underline{{\mathcal{V}}_{1}}}=0
the conditional distribution of
W_{n+1}{\mathchoice{\rm 1\mskip-4.0mul}{\rm 1\mskip-4.0mul}{\rm 1\mskip-4.5mul}{\rm 1\mskip-5.0mul}}_{\{V_{n+1}=i\}}
given
\{W_{n}=w\}
equals the conditional distribution of

\hat{W}_{n+1}{\mathchoice{\rm 1\mskip-4.0mul}{\rm 1\mskip-4.0mul}{\rm 1\mskip-4.5mul}{\rm 1\mskip-5.0mul}}_{\left\{\hat{V}_{n+1}=i\right\}}
given
\left\{\hat{W}_{n}=w\right\}
. Therefore, we obtain that for all
n\in\mathbb{N}
,

{\mathbb{E}}\left[\left(Q(W_{n+1})-Q(W_{n})\right){\mathchoice{\rm 1\mskip-4.0mul}{\rm 1\mskip-4.0mul}{\rm 1\mskip-4.5mul}{\rm 1\mskip-5.0mul}}_{\{V_{n+1}\in{\mathcal{V}}_{2}\}}\,\big|\,W_{n}=w\right]\\
\begin{aligned} =&\sum_{i\in{\mathcal{V}}_{2}}{\mathbb{E}}\left[Q(W_{n+1}){\mathchoice{\rm 1\mskip-4.0mul}{\rm 1\mskip-4.0mul}{\rm 1\mskip-4.5mul}{\rm 1\mskip-5.0mul}}_{\{V_{n+1}=i\}}\,\big|\,W_{n}=w\right]-\sum_{i\in{\mathcal{V}}_{2}}{\mathbb{E}}\left[Q(W_{n}){\mathchoice{\rm 1\mskip-4.0mul}{\rm 1\mskip-4.0mul}{\rm 1\mskip-4.5mul}{\rm 1\mskip-5.0mul}}_{\{V_{n+1}=i\}}\,\big|\,W_{n}=w\right]\\
=&\sum_{i\in{\mathcal{V}}_{2}}{\mathbb{E}}\left[Q(W_{n+1}){\mathchoice{\rm 1\mskip-4.0mul}{\rm 1\mskip-4.0mul}{\rm 1\mskip-4.5mul}{\rm 1\mskip-5.0mul}}_{\{V_{n+1}=i\}}\,\big|\,W_{n}=w\right]-\mu({\mathcal{V}}_{2})Q(w)\\
=&\sum_{i\in{\mathcal{V}}_{2}}{\mathbb{E}}\left[Q\left(\hat{W}_{n+1}\right){\mathchoice{\rm 1\mskip-4.0mul}{\rm 1\mskip-4.0mul}{\rm 1\mskip-4.5mul}{\rm 1\mskip-5.0mul}}_{\left\{\hat{V}_{n+1}=i\right\}}\,\big|\,\hat{W}_{n}=w\right]-\hat{\mu}({\mathcal{V}}_{2})Q(w)\\
=&\;{\mathbb{E}}\left[\left(Q\left(\hat{W}_{n+1}\right)-Q\left(\hat{W}_{n}\right)\right){\mathchoice{\rm 1\mskip-4.0mul}{\rm 1\mskip-4.0mul}{\rm 1\mskip-4.5mul}{\rm 1\mskip-5.0mul}}_{\left\{\hat{V}_{n+1}\in{\mathcal{V}}_{2}\right\}}\,\big|\,\hat{W}_{n}=w\right],\end{aligned}

(5.11)

where the third equality due to the equality of the conditional distribution of
W_{n+1}{\mathchoice{\rm 1\mskip-4.0mul}{\rm 1\mskip-4.0mul}{\rm 1\mskip-4.5mul}{\rm 1\mskip-5.0mul}}_{\{V_{n+1}=i\}}
given
\{W_{n}=w\}
and
\hat{W}_{n+1}{\mathchoice{\rm 1\mskip-4.0mul}{\rm 1\mskip-4.0mul}{\rm 1\mskip-4.5mul}{\rm 1\mskip-5.0mul}}_{\left\{\hat{V}_{n+1}=i\right\}}
given
\left\{\hat{W}_{n}=w\right\}.

Likewise, if a system
(G,\Phi,\mu)
is in state
w
, then, for any
i\in\mathcal{V}_{1}\cap(\mathcal{O}_{w})^{c}\cap(\mathcal{Z}_{w})^{c}
, an incoming of class
i

finds the same possible matches (if any) as an incoming item of class
i
or of class
\underline{i}
finding the system
(\hat{G},\hat{\Phi},\hat{\mu})
in the state
w
.
Again, the choice of the match of the latter is the same in both systems, or follows the same distribution in case of a draw. Like in (
5.11
), we obtain that, for all
n\in\mathbb{N}
,

{\mathbb{E}}\left[\left(Q(W_{n+1})-Q(W_{n})\right){\mathchoice{\rm 1\mskip-4.0mul}{\rm 1\mskip-4.0mul}{\rm 1\mskip-4.5mul}{\rm 1\mskip-5.0mul}}_{\{V_{n+1}\in\mathcal{V}_{1}\cap(\mathcal{O}_{w})^{c}\cap(\mathcal{Z}_{w})^{c}\}}\,\big|\,W_{n}=w\right]\\
=\mathbb{E}\Biggl[\!\left(\!Q\left(\hat{W}_{n+1}\right)-Q\left(\hat{W}_{n}\right)\!\right)\!{\mathchoice{\rm 1\mskip-4.0mul}{\rm 1\mskip-4.0mul}{\rm 1\mskip-4.5mul}{\rm 1\mskip-5.0mul}}_{\left\{\hat{V}_{n+1}\in\left(\mathcal{V}_{1}\cap(\mathcal{O}_{w})^{c}\cap(\mathcal{Z}_{w})^{c}\right)\cup\left(\underline{\mathcal{V}_{1}\cap(\mathcal{O}_{w})^{c}\cap(\mathcal{Z}_{w})^{c}}\right)\right\}}\big|\,\hat{W}_{n}=w\!\Biggl],

(5.12)

where we also use the fact that

\hat{\mu}\left(\left(\mathcal{V}_{1}\cap(\mathcal{O}_{w})^{c}\cap(\mathcal{Z}_{w})^{c}\right)\cup\left(\underline{\mathcal{V}_{1}\cap(\mathcal{O}_{w})^{c}\cap(\mathcal{Z}_{w})^{c}}\right)\right)=\mu(\mathcal{V}_{1}\cap(\mathcal{O}_{w})^{c}\cap(\mathcal{Z}_{w})^{c}).

Now, if a system
(G,\Phi,\mu)
is in state
w
, then, for any
i\in\mathcal{Z}_{w}
, and incoming item of class
i
finds no possible match.
So, it is stored in line and the coordinate
i
of the chain increases from 0 to 1. Consequently, for any
n\in\mathbb{N}
, conditional on
\{W_{n}=w\}
and for any such
i
, we get that

\left(Q(W_{n+1})-Q(W_{n})\right){\mathchoice{\rm 1\mskip-4.0mul}{\rm 1\mskip-4.0mul}{\rm 1\mskip-4.5mul}{\rm 1\mskip-5.0mul}}_{\{V_{n+1}=i\}}=\left(Q(wi)-Q(w)\right){\mathchoice{\rm 1\mskip-4.0mul}{\rm 1\mskip-4.0mul}{\rm 1\mskip-4.5mul}{\rm 1\mskip-5.0mul}}_{\{V_{n+1}=i\}}={\mathchoice{\rm 1\mskip-4.0mul}{\rm 1\mskip-4.0mul}{\rm 1\mskip-4.5mul}{\rm 1\mskip-5.0mul}}_{\{V_{n+1}=i\}}.

(5.13)

Similarly, if the system
(\hat{G},\hat{\Phi},\hat{\mu})
is in the state
w
and the entering item is of class
i\in\mathcal{Z}_{w}
or of class
\underline{i}\in\underline{\mathcal{Z}_{w}}
, then, in both cases, the entering item does not find any possible match in
(\hat{G},\hat{\Phi},\hat{\mu})
and so the coordinate
i
or
\underline{i}
of the Markov chain increases from 0 to 1. Thus, given that
\left\{\hat{W}_{n}=w\right\}
, we get that

\left(Q\left(\hat{W}_{n+1}\right)-Q\left(\hat{W}_{n}\right)\right){\mathchoice{\rm 1\mskip-4.0mul}{\rm 1\mskip-4.0mul}{\rm 1\mskip-4.5mul}{\rm 1\mskip-5.0mul}}_{\left\{\hat{V}_{n+1}\in\{i,\underline{i}\}\right\}}\\
=\left(Q(wi)-Q(w)\right){\mathchoice{\rm 1\mskip-4.0mul}{\rm 1\mskip-4.0mul}{\rm 1\mskip-4.5mul}{\rm 1\mskip-5.0mul}}_{\left\{\hat{V}_{n+1}=i\right\}}+\left(Q\left(w\underline{i}\right)-Q(w)\right){\mathchoice{\rm 1\mskip-4.0mul}{\rm 1\mskip-4.0mul}{\rm 1\mskip-4.5mul}{\rm 1\mskip-5.0mul}}_{\left\{\hat{V}_{n+1}=\underline{i}\right\}}={\mathchoice{\rm 1\mskip-4.0mul}{\rm 1\mskip-4.0mul}{\rm 1\mskip-4.5mul}{\rm 1\mskip-5.0mul}}_{\left\{\hat{V}_{n+1}=i\right\}}+{\mathchoice{\rm 1\mskip-4.0mul}{\rm 1\mskip-4.0mul}{\rm 1\mskip-4.5mul}{\rm 1\mskip-5.0mul}}_{\left\{\hat{V}_{n+1}=\underline{i}\right\}}.

(5.14)

This, together with (
5.13
), entails that

{\mathbb{E}}\left[\left(Q(W_{n+1})-Q(W_{n})\right){\mathchoice{\rm 1\mskip-4.0mul}{\rm 1\mskip-4.0mul}{\rm 1\mskip-4.5mul}{\rm 1\mskip-5.0mul}}_{\{V_{n+1}\in\mathcal{Z}_{w}\}}\,|\,W_{n}=w\right]\\
\begin{aligned} =&\sum_{i\in\mathcal{Z}_{w}}{\mathbb{E}}\left[{\mathchoice{\rm 1\mskip-4.0mul}{\rm 1\mskip-4.0mul}{\rm 1\mskip-4.5mul}{\rm 1\mskip-5.0mul}}_{\{V_{n+1}=i\}}\,|\,W_{n}=w\right]\\
=&\;\mu({\mathcal{Z}}_{w})\\
=&\;\hat{\mu}\left({\mathcal{Z}}_{w}\right)+\hat{\mu}\left(\underline{{\mathcal{Z}}_{w}}\right)\\
=&\sum_{i\in\mathcal{Z}_{w}}{\mathbb{E}}\left[{\mathchoice{\rm 1\mskip-4.0mul}{\rm 1\mskip-4.0mul}{\rm 1\mskip-4.5mul}{\rm 1\mskip-5.0mul}}_{\{\hat{V}_{n+1}=i\}}+{\mathchoice{\rm 1\mskip-4.0mul}{\rm 1\mskip-4.0mul}{\rm 1\mskip-4.5mul}{\rm 1\mskip-5.0mul}}_{\{\hat{V}_{n+1}=\underline{i}\}}\,|\,\hat{W}_{n}=w\right]\\
=&\;{\mathbb{E}}\left[\left(Q(\hat{W}_{n+1})-Q(\hat{W}_{n})\right){\mathchoice{\rm 1\mskip-4.0mul}{\rm 1\mskip-4.0mul}{\rm 1\mskip-4.5mul}{\rm 1\mskip-5.0mul}}_{\{\hat{V}_{n+1}\in\mathcal{Z}_{w}\cup\underline{{\mathcal{Z}}_{w}}\}}\,|\,\hat{W}_{n}=w\right],\end{aligned}

(5.15)

where the first equality due to the equation (
5.13
). The third equality follows from the equation (
5.3
) as
{\mathcal{Z}}_{w}\subset{\mathcal{V}}_{1}.
The fourth equality follows from the equation (
5.14
).

At last, if the system
\left(G,\Phi,\mu\right)
is in the state
w
, then, the arrival of a class
i
-item, for
i\in\mathcal{O}_{w}
, leads to the matching of two items
of class
i
. Therefore, as
|w|_{i}=1
we obtain

\left(Q(W_{n+1})-Q(W_{n})\right){\mathchoice{\rm 1\mskip-4.0mul}{\rm 1\mskip-4.0mul}{\rm 1\mskip-4.5mul}{\rm 1\mskip-5.0mul}}_{\{V_{n+1}\in\mathcal{O}_{w}\}}=-{\mathchoice{\rm 1\mskip-4.0mul}{\rm 1\mskip-4.0mul}{\rm 1\mskip-4.5mul}{\rm 1\mskip-5.0mul}}_{\{V_{n+1}\in\mathcal{O}_{w}\}}.

(5.16)

Now, suppose that the system
\left(\hat{G},\hat{\Phi},\hat{\mu}\right)
is in the state
\hat{W}_{n}=w
.
Then, if an item of class
i\in\mathcal{O}_{w}
enters in the system, the corresponding item is not matched and the number of
i
-items in the system increases from 1 to 2.
Therefore we get that

\left(Q\left(\hat{W}_{n+1}\right)-Q\left(\hat{W}_{n}\right)\right){\mathchoice{\rm 1\mskip-4.0mul}{\rm 1\mskip-4.0mul}{\rm 1\mskip-4.5mul}{\rm 1\mskip-5.0mul}}_{\left\{\hat{V}_{n+1}\in\mathcal{O}_{w}\right\}}=3{\mathchoice{\rm 1\mskip-4.0mul}{\rm 1\mskip-4.0mul}{\rm 1\mskip-4.5mul}{\rm 1\mskip-5.0mul}}_{\left\{\hat{V}_{n+1}\in\mathcal{O}_{w}\right\}}.

(5.17)

If on the other hand, an item of class
\underline{i}\in\underline{\mathcal{O}_{w}}
enters in the same system
(\hat{G},\hat{\Phi},\hat{\mu})
,
then, the corresponding item match with the stored class
i
-item and so the coordinate
i
of the chain decreases to 0. Thus,

\left(Q\left(\hat{W}_{n+1}\right)-Q\left(\hat{W}_{n}\right)\right){\mathchoice{\rm 1\mskip-4.0mul}{\rm 1\mskip-4.0mul}{\rm 1\mskip-4.5mul}{\rm 1\mskip-5.0mul}}_{\left\{\hat{V}_{n+1}\in\underline{\mathcal{O}_{w}}\right\}}=-{\mathchoice{\rm 1\mskip-4.0mul}{\rm 1\mskip-4.0mul}{\rm 1\mskip-4.5mul}{\rm 1\mskip-5.0mul}}_{\left\{\hat{V}_{n+1}\in\underline{\mathcal{O}_{w}}\right\}}.

Gathering this with (
5.16
) and (
5.17
) and then taking expectations, we obtain that

{\mathbb{E}}\left[\left(Q(W_{n+1})-Q(W_{n})\right){\mathchoice{\rm 1\mskip-4.0mul}{\rm 1\mskip-4.0mul}{\rm 1\mskip-4.5mul}{\rm 1\mskip-5.0mul}}_{\{V_{n+1}\in\mathcal{O}_{w}\}}\,\big|\,W_{n}=w\right]\\
={\mathbb{E}}\left[\left(Q\left(\hat{W}_{n+1}\right)-Q\left(\hat{W}_{n}\right)\right){\mathchoice{\rm 1\mskip-4.0mul}{\rm 1\mskip-4.0mul}{\rm 1\mskip-4.5mul}{\rm 1\mskip-5.0mul}}_{\left\{\hat{V}_{n+1}\in\mathcal{O}_{w}\cup\underline{\mathcal{O}_{w}}\right\}}\,\big|\,\hat{W}_{n}=w\right]-4\hat{\mu}\left(\mathcal{O}_{w}\right).

(5.18)

Finally, (
5.11
) together with (
5.12
), (
5.15
) and (
5.18
) give that

\Delta^{\Phi}_{\mu}Q(w)=\hat{\Delta}^{\hat{\Phi}}_{\hat{\mu}}Q(w)-4\hat{\mu}\left(\mathcal{O}_{w}\right),

(5.19)

which concludes the proof.
∎

Definition 5.9
.

Let
G
be a connected multigraph and
\Phi
be an admissible matching policy on
G
. We say that
\check{\Phi}
reduces
\Phi
if,
for any
{\mu}\in\mathscr{M}({{{\mathcal{V}}}})
, whenever the two systems
(\check{G},\check{\Phi},\mu)
and
(G,\Phi,\mu)
are in the
same state
{w}\in{{\mathbb{W}}}
and welcome the same arrival, then
\check{\Phi}
and
\Phi
induce the same choice of match, if any.

Proposition 5.8
.

Let
G=({\mathcal{V}},{\mathcal{E}})
be a connected multigraph and
\Phi
be a class admissible policy on
G
and
\mu\in\mathscr{M}({\mathcal{V}})
.
Let
\hat{\Phi}
be a matching policy extending
\Phi
on
\hat{G}
,
\hat{\mu}
a measure extending
\mu
on
\hat{\mathcal{V}}

and
\check{\Phi}
be a policy that reduces
\Phi
on
\check{G}
.
Then the drift of the respective Markov chains are such that for all
w\in{\mathbb{W}}
,

{\Delta}^{\Phi}_{\mu}L(w)\leq\hat{\Delta}^{\hat{\Phi}}_{\hat{\mu}}L(w)\leq\check{\Delta}^{\check{\Phi}}_{\mu}L(w).

(5.20)

Proof.

Fix
w\in{\mathbb{W}}
.
The only case in which the proof of the left inequality of (
5.20
) differs from that of Proposition
5.7
is when
an item of class
i\in\mathcal{O}_{w}
enters the
(\hat{G},\hat{\Phi},\hat{\mu})
system in a state
w
and we multiply each transition by it’s convenient
\alpha_{i}
.
Then, we now get that for all
n
,

\left(L(\hat{W}_{n+1})-L(\hat{W}_{n})\right){\mathchoice{\rm 1\mskip-4.0mul}{\rm 1\mskip-4.0mul}{\rm 1\mskip-4.5mul}{\rm 1\mskip-5.0mul}}_{\{\hat{V}_{n+1}\in\mathcal{O}_{w}\}}=\sum_{i\in\mathcal{O}_{w}}\alpha_{i}{\mathchoice{\rm 1\mskip-4.0mul}{\rm 1\mskip-4.0mul}{\rm 1\mskip-4.5mul}{\rm 1\mskip-5.0mul}}_{\{\hat{V}_{n+1}=i\}},

which, taking expectations and reasoning as in (
5.19
), leads to

{\Delta}^{\Phi}_{\mu}L(w)=\hat{\Delta}^{\hat{\Phi}}_{\hat{\mu}}L(w)-\sum_{i\in\mathcal{O}_{w}}\alpha_{i}\hat{\mu}\left(i\right).

We now turn to the proof of the right inequality of (
5.20
). Denote

\mathcal{P}_{w}=\{i\in{\mathcal{V}}_{1}\,:\,|w|_{i}>0\}.

Fix also
n\in{\mathbb{N}}
, and denote by
\check{V}_{n}
, the class of the incoming item at time
n

in the
(\check{G},\check{\Phi},\mu)
system. First, similarly to (
5.11
), (
5.12
) and (
5.15
) we clearly get that

{\mathbb{E}}\left[\left(L(\check{W}_{n+1})-L(\check{W}_{n})\right){\mathchoice{\rm 1\mskip-4.0mul}{\rm 1\mskip-4.0mul}{\rm 1\mskip-4.5mul}{\rm 1\mskip-5.0mul}}_{\{\check{V}_{n+1}\in{\mathcal{V}}_{2}\cup\left(\mathcal{V}_{1}\cap(\mathcal{P}_{w})^{c}\right)\}}\,|\,\check{W}_{n}=w\right]\\
={\mathbb{E}}\left[\left(L(\hat{W}_{n+1})-L(\hat{W}_{n})\right){\mathchoice{\rm 1\mskip-4.0mul}{\rm 1\mskip-4.0mul}{\rm 1\mskip-4.5mul}{\rm 1\mskip-5.0mul}}_{\left\{\hat{V}_{n+1}\in{\mathcal{V}}_{2}\cup\left(\mathcal{V}_{1}\cap(\mathcal{P}_{w})^{c}\cup\left(\underline{\mathcal{V}_{1}\cap(\mathcal{P}_{w})^{c}}\right)\right)\right\}}|\hat{W}_{n}=w\right].

(5.21)

Now, we also clearly have that

\displaystyle{\mathbb{E}}\left[\left(L(\check{W}_{n+1})-L(\check{W}_{n})\right){\mathchoice{\rm 1\mskip-4.0mul}{\rm 1\mskip-4.0mul}{\rm 1\mskip-4.5mul}{\rm 1\mskip-5.0mul}}_{\{\check{V}_{n+1}\in\mathcal{P}_{w}\}}\,|\,\check{W}_{n}=w\right]

\displaystyle=\sum_{i\in\mathcal{P}_{w}}\alpha_{i}\mu\left(i\right);

\displaystyle{\mathbb{E}}\left[\left(L(\hat{W}_{n+1})-L(\hat{W}_{n})\right){\mathchoice{\rm 1\mskip-4.0mul}{\rm 1\mskip-4.0mul}{\rm 1\mskip-4.5mul}{\rm 1\mskip-5.0mul}}_{\{\hat{V}_{n+1}\in\mathcal{P}_{w}\}}\,|\,\hat{W}_{n}=w\right]

\displaystyle=\sum_{i\in\mathcal{P}_{w}}\alpha_{i}\hat{\mu}\left(i\right);

\displaystyle{\mathbb{E}}\left[\left(L(\hat{W}_{n+1})-L(\hat{W}_{n})\right){\mathchoice{\rm 1\mskip-4.0mul}{\rm 1\mskip-4.0mul}{\rm 1\mskip-4.5mul}{\rm 1\mskip-5.0mul}}_{\{\hat{V}_{n+1}\in\underline{\mathcal{P}_{w}}\}}\,|\,\hat{W}_{n}=w\right]

\displaystyle=-\sum_{i\in\underline{\mathcal{P}_{w}}}\alpha_{i}\hat{\mu}\left(i\right),

which, together with (
5.21
), implies that

\hat{\Delta}^{\hat{\Phi}}_{\hat{\mu}}L(w)=\check{\Delta}^{\check{\Phi}}_{\mu}L(w)-2\sum_{i\in\underline{\mathcal{P}_{w}}}\alpha_{i}\hat{\mu}\left(i\right).

∎

5.5.2
Proofs of the remaining main results

We are now in position to prove Theorem
5.2
, Theorem
5.3
and Proposition
5.3
.

Proof of Theorem
5.2
.

Let
\mu\in\textsc{Ncond}(G)
. From Lemma
5.2
, the measure
\hat{\mu}
belongs to
Ncond
(\hat{G})
.
Let
\Phi
be a matching policy of the Max-Weight class on
G
, with
\beta>0
. Clearly, its extension
\hat{\Phi}
is also
of the Max-Weight class on
\hat{G}
. Then, we know from Theorem 5.3 in
[
29
]
that the model
(\hat{G},\hat{\Phi},\hat{\mu})
is stable.
In particular,
we see in the proof of Theorem 5.3 in
[
29
]
that
the Lyapunov-Foster Theorem
1.1
can be applied to the chain
\left(\hat{W}_{n}\right)_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
for the quadratic function
Q
.
Specifically, there exist
\eta>0
and a finite set
\hat{\mathcal{K}}\subset\hat{{\mathbb{W}}}
such that

\hat{\Delta}^{\hat{\Phi}}_{\hat{\mu}}Q(\hat{w})<-\eta
for all
\hat{w}\not\in\hat{\mathcal{K}}
.
Thus, in view of Proposition
5.7
, we have that
\Delta^{\Phi}_{\mu}Q({w})<-\eta

for any
w
that lies outside the finite subset
\mathcal{K}=\hat{\mathcal{K}}\cap{\mathbb{W}}
.
We conclude by applying the Lyapunov-Foster Theorem to the mapping
Q
and the compact set
\mathcal{K}
.
∎

Proof of Theorem
5.3
.

(i) Fix
\mu\in\textsc{Ncond}(G)
.
First, if
G
is a graph (
{\mathcal{V}}_{1}=\emptyset
) and
G=\check{G}
is complete
p
-partite for
p\geq 3
, then
the result follows from Theorem 2, Assertion (16) in
[
33
]
: specifically, we have that for some
\eta>0
,
for any
w\in{\mathbb{W}}\setminus\{\varepsilon\},

{\Delta}^{\Phi}_{\mu}L(w)<-\eta,

(5.22)

and the Lyapunov-Foster criterion applies.
Now, if
G
is not a graph, i.e.
{\mathcal{V}}_{1}\neq\emptyset
, then let

\delta=\min\left\{\mu({\mathcal{E}}(I))-\mu(I)\,:\,I\in{\mathbb{I}}(G)\right\},

which is strictly positive since
\mu\in\textsc{Ncond}(G)
, and the mapping

L_{\delta}:\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}{\mathbb{W}}&\longrightarrow{\mathbb{R}}^{+}\\
w&\longmapsto\displaystyle\sum_{i\in{\mathcal{V}}_{1}}{\delta\over 2\mu({\mathcal{V}}_{1})}|w|_{i}+\sum_{i\in{\mathcal{V}}_{2}}|w|_{i}.\end{array}\right.

Then, for any
w\in{\mathbb{W}}
the set
I^{w}=\{i\in{\mathcal{V}}\,:\,|w|_{i}>0\}
is an independent set of
\check{G}
, so by the very definition of a complete
p
-partite graph, there exists a unique maximal independent set

\check{I}
of
\check{G}
such that
I^{w}\subset\check{I}
.
Then, for all
w\in{\mathbb{W}}
such that
I^{w}\cap{\mathcal{V}}_{2}\neq\emptyset
, for any
n
, if
\{W_{n}=w\}
the Markov chain can make two types of moves upon the arrival of
V_{n+1}
:

•

either one coordinate of
W_{n}
decreases from 1 if
V_{n+1}
is of a class in
\check{I}^{c}=\check{{\mathcal{E}}}\left(\check{I}\right)
, or of a class in
I^{w}\cap{\mathcal{V}}_{1}
;

•

or one coordinate of
W_{n}
increases from 1, if
V_{n+1}
is of a class in
\check{I}\cap\left((I^{w})^{c}\cup{\mathcal{V}}_{2}\right)
.

Therefore, for any
{\mathcal{V}}_{2}
-favorable matching policy
\Phi
we have that

{\Delta}^{\Phi}_{\mu}L_{\delta}(w)\\
=-{\delta\over 2\mu({\mathcal{V}}_{1})}\mu\left({\mathcal{V}}_{1}\cap I^{w}\right){\mathchoice{\rm 1\mskip-4.0mul}{\rm 1\mskip-4.0mul}{\rm 1\mskip-4.5mul}{\rm 1\mskip-5.0mul}}_{\{{\mathcal{V}}_{1}\cap I^{w}\neq\emptyset\}}+{\delta\over 2\mu({\mathcal{V}}_{1})}\mu\left({\mathcal{V}}_{1}\cap\check{I}\cap(I^{w})^{c}\right)+\mu\left(\check{I}\cap{\mathcal{V}}_{2}\right)-\mu\left(\check{I}^{c}\right).

(5.23)

Observe that
\check{I}\cap{\mathcal{V}}_{2}
is an independent set of
G
, and that
\check{I}^{c}={\mathcal{E}}\left(\check{I}\cap{\mathcal{V}}_{2}\right)
. Hence (
5.23
) implies that

{\Delta}^{\Phi}_{\mu}L_{\delta}(w)\leq{\delta\over 2\mu({\mathcal{V}}_{1})}\mu\left({\mathcal{V}}_{1}\cap\check{I}\cap(I^{w})^{c}\right)+\mu\left(\check{I}\cap{\mathcal{V}}_{2}\right)-\mu\left({\mathcal{E}}\left(\check{I}\cap{\mathcal{V}}_{2}\right)\right)\leq{\delta\over 2}-\delta=-{\delta\over 2}.

As this is true for any
w
outside the finite set
\{w\in{\mathbb{W}}\,:\,I^{w}\cap{\mathcal{V}}_{2}=\emptyset\}
, we conclude again using the Lyapunov-Foster Theorem that
\textsc{Stab}(G,\Phi)=\textsc{Ncond}(G).

(ii) Fix
\mu\in\textsc{Ncond}(\check{G}),
and an admissible matching policy
\Phi
. Applying (
5.22
) to
\check{G}
, we obtain that for any
\check{\Phi}
that reduces
\Phi
,
for some
\eta>0
we have

{\check{\Delta}}^{\check{\Phi}}_{\mu}L(\check{w})<-\eta

for all
\check{w}\in\check{{\mathbb{W}}}\setminus\{\varepsilon\}
.
Combining this with (
5.20
), and recalling that
{\mathbb{W}}\subset\check{{\mathbb{W}}}
we obtain that
{\Delta}^{\Phi}_{\mu}L({w})<-\eta
for all
w\in{\mathbb{W}}\setminus\{\varepsilon\}
,
which concludes the proof.
∎

Proof of Proposition
5.3
.

(i) Remark that for any
\hat{w}\in\hat{\mathbb{W}}
, the set
\{i\in{\mathcal{V}}\,:\,|\hat{w}|_{i}>0\}
is again an independent set of
\check{G}
. So we can apply, for any

\hat{\mu}\in\textsc{Ncond}(\hat{G})
, the exact same argument as for assertion (i) in Theorem
5.3
, by replacing
{\mathcal{V}}_{1}

by
{\mathcal{V}}_{1}\cup\underline{{\mathcal{V}}_{1}}
.

(ii)
Let
\hat{\mu}
be an element of
\mathscr{M}(\hat{{\mathcal{V}}})
whose reduced measure
\mu
belongs to
\textsc{Ncond}(\check{G})
. Let
\hat{\Phi}
be an admissible policy on
\hat{{\mathcal{V}}}
,

\Phi
be a policy on
{\mathcal{V}}
such that
\hat{\Phi}
extends
\Phi
, and
\check{\Phi}
be a policy reducing
\Phi
on
\check{G}
.

First, as in (
5.22
) there exists
\eta>0
such that
\check{\Delta}^{\check{\Phi}}_{\mu}L(w)<-\eta
for any
w\in{\mathbb{W}}\setminus\{\varepsilon\}
.

Fix
\hat{w}
in
\hat{{\mathbb{W}}}\setminus\{\varepsilon\}
.
Then define the permutation
\gamma
of
\hat{{\mathcal{V}}}
by

\begin{cases}\gamma(i)&=\underline{i}\mbox{ and }\gamma(\underline{i})=i\mbox{ if }|\hat{w}|_{\underline{i}}>0\mbox{ and }|\hat{w}|_{i}=0,\,i\in{\mathcal{V}}_{1},\\
\gamma(j)&=j\,\mbox{ else, for any }j\in\hat{{\mathcal{V}}}.\end{cases}

Let us also denote by
\gamma(\hat{w})
, the word obtained from
w
by replacing the letters of
\hat{w}
by their image through
\gamma
, in other words for all
i\in\llbracket 1,|\hat{w}|\rrbracket
,

\gamma(\hat{w})_{i}=\gamma(\hat{w}_{i})
. Observe that
\gamma(\hat{w})
is clearly an element of
{\mathbb{W}}\setminus\{\varepsilon\}
, so in view of the above observation we have that

\check{\Delta}^{\check{\Phi}}_{\mu}L(\gamma(\hat{w}))<-\eta.

(5.24)

Now, as
i
and
\underline{i}
have the same connectivity in
\hat{G}
for any
i\in{\mathcal{V}}_{1}
, for all
n
the conditional distribution of
\hat{W}_{n+1}
given
\{\hat{W}_{n}=\hat{w}\}
in the
(\hat{G},\hat{\Phi},\hat{\mu})

system equals that of
\hat{W}_{n+1}
given
\{\hat{W}_{n}=\gamma(\hat{w})\}
in the
(\hat{G},\hat{\Phi},\hat{\mu}\circ\gamma)
system. In particular, we have that

\hat{\Delta}^{\hat{\Phi}}_{\hat{\mu}}(\hat{w})=\hat{\Delta}^{\hat{\Phi}}_{\hat{\mu}\circ\gamma}(\gamma(\hat{w})).

(5.25)

On the other hand, as
\gamma(\hat{w})
is an element of
{\mathbb{W}}
and
the measure
\hat{\mu}\circ\gamma\in\mathscr{M}(\hat{{\mathcal{V}}})
clearly extends the measure
\mu
, the right inequality of (
5.20
) implies that

\hat{\Delta}^{\hat{\Phi}}_{\hat{\mu}\circ\gamma}L(\gamma(\hat{w}))\leq\check{\Delta}^{\check{\Phi}}_{\mu}L(\gamma(\hat{w})),

and it follows from (
5.24
-
5.25
) that
\hat{\Delta}^{\hat{\Phi}}_{\hat{\mu}}(\hat{w})<-\eta
. As this is true for any
\hat{w}
in
\hat{{\mathbb{W}}}\setminus\{\varepsilon\}
, the proof is complete.
∎

5.6
Discussion of results and conclusion

In this chapter, we have studied a generalization of stochastic matching models on graphs by allowing the self-loops matching. Different applications appear to this class of models such as dating sites or collaborative sites, that is, individuals of the same class can be married.

For a given multigraph
G
, we build its maximal subgraph
\check{G}
that is obtained by deleting all self-loops in
G,
and its minimal blow-up graph
\hat{G}
that is obtained by duplicating each node having a self-loop by two nodes having the same neighborhood and replacing each self-loop by an edge between the node and its copy. Taken into consideration the graphs
\check{G}
and
\hat{G}
which are built above, we can transmit and generalize different results to
G.

The multigraph
G
under the matching policies
fcfm
and Max-Weight such that
\beta>0
are maximal. Also, if
G
is a complete
p
-partite multigraph,
(p\geq 2)
, then for
p\geq 3
or
{\mathcal{V}}_{1}\neq 0,
any
{\mathcal{V}}_{2}
-favorable matching policy is maximal.

Chapter 6
Fluid limits techniques for stability

Introduction

In the previous chapters, we studied the stability of stochastic matching models using Lyapunov techniques. In this chapter, we present a new approach that allows us to retrieve, and complete these results in continuous-time settings. This technique consists of speeding up time and scaling the process appropriately to obtain a deterministic and continuous approximation of the original process, which allows, among other features, to study the ergodicity properties of the process at hand. In the limit, one gets a sort of caricature of the initial stochastic process which is defined as its
fluid limit
.

This chapter is organized as follows: In Section
6.1
we start by providing necessary conditions of stability for graphical matching models in continuous time. In Section
6.2
we derive fluid approximations of matching models on multigraphs. In Section
6.3
we present different case studies. Last, in Section
6.4
we elaborate the fluid limit technique to study matching models on complete
3
-uniform hypergraphs and complete
3
-uniform
k
-partite hypergraphs. We conclude and discuss this chapter in Section
6.5
.

6.1
Necessary conditions of stability

The necessary condition of stability for discrete-time stochastic matching models on graphs was recalled in Section
3.4
. For all
\mathbb{G}=({\mathcal{V}},{\mathcal{E}})

it reads as follows,

\textsc{Ncond}(\mathbb{G}):=\big\{\mu\textrm{ with support }{\mathcal{V}}\;:\mu(I)<\mu({\mathcal{E}}(I))\;\textrm{ for all }I\in\mathbb{I}(\mathbb{G})\big\}.

(6.1)

The first question that arises is to find an analog of (
6.1
) for continuous-time models, that is, in the context of matching queues, as defined in section
2.5
(recall the notation therein).
The following condition was introduced in
[
38
]
: for any matching graph
\mathbb{G},

\textsc{Ncond}_{C}(\mathbb{G}):=\left\{\lambda\in({\mathbb{R}}^{++})^{|{\mathcal{V}}|}\;:\;\bar{\lambda}_{I}<\bar{\lambda}_{{\mathcal{E}}(I)}\textrm{ for all }I\in\mathbb{I}(G)\right\}.

(6.2)

Fix a graph
{\mathbb{G}}=({\mathcal{V}},{\mathcal{E}})
, a class-admissible matching policy
\Phi,
and an arrival vector
\lambda:=(\lambda_{1},\cdots,\lambda_{|{\mathcal{V}}|})
. Let
for all
i\in{\mathcal{V}}
,

\mu_{\lambda}(i)={\lambda_{i}\over\bar{\lambda}}={\lambda_{i}\over\sum\limits_{i\in{\mathcal{V}}}\lambda_{i}}\cdot

Then, it is easily seen that
\mu_{\lambda}
defines a probability measure on

{\mathcal{V}}
. Further, if we denote for all
n\geq 1
, by
A(n):=\{A_{n}(i),\;i\in{\mathcal{V}}\}

the vector tracking the number of items in the buffers of all nodes up to time

n
in the discrete-time system associated to
\mathbb{G},

\Phi
and
\mu_{\lambda}
.
On another hand, let
N
be the superposition of the arrival Poisson processes in the continuous-time matching system associated with
\mathbb{G}
,
\Phi
and
\lambda
. Then, it is immediate that we have the identity in distribution
Q_{t}=A(N(t))
. As there are finitely many Poisson processes in the continuous-time model, the sojourn time of the corresponding CTMC is of a rate that is bounded away from zero. This implies that
Q
is positive recurrent if and only if
A
is so, see
[
31
, Theorem 6.18]
. Consequently, relating (
6.1
) to (
6.2
) we get that the stability region of the continuous-time model is included in
\textsc{Ncond}_{C}(\mathbb{G})
.

We now use fluid-limit technique to derive precisely the stability region of continuous-time models. A throughout presentation of the following section can be found e.g. in
[
38
]
.

6.2
Fluid Stability

Fix a multigraph
G=({\mathcal{V}}_{1}\cup{\mathcal{V}}_{2},{\mathcal{E}})
, a matching policy

\Phi
of the priority type and an arrival vector
\lambda
.
Observe that for all
t\geq 0
, for all
i\in{\mathcal{V}},\;Q_{i}(t)
increases by 1 for each class-
i
arrivals such that
Q_{j}(t)=0
for any
j\in{\mathcal{E}}(i)
. On another hand,
Q_{i}(t)
decreases by 1 (when it is positive) for any class-
j
arrival with
j\in{\mathcal{E}}(i)
, such that
Q_{k}(t)
are empty for any
k\in{\mathcal{E}}(j)
such that
j
gives a higher priority to
k
over
i.

The state space in CTMC is then as follows:

\mathbb{E}=\biggl\{z\in({\mathbb{Z}}^{+})^{|{\mathcal{V}}|}\;:\;z_{i}z_{j}=0,\textrm{ for any }i\in{\mathcal{V}},\\
\textrm{ and }j\in{\mathcal{E}}(i)\textrm{ with }|z_{k}|\leq 1\textrm{ for any }k\in{\mathcal{V}}_{1}\biggl\}.

(6.3)

For each
i\in{\mathcal{V}},
we introduce the following sets,

\begin{array}[]{rrll}&\mathcal{N}_{i}&:=&\{z\in\mathbb{E}\;:\;z_{i}>0\};\\
&\mathcal{O}_{i}&:=&\{z\in\mathbb{E}\;:\;z_{j}=0\;\;\textrm{for all }j\in\mathcal{E}(i)\};\\
&\Phi_{j}(i)&:=&\left\{k\in\mathcal{E}(j);\;j\textrm{ gives priority to }k\textrm{ over }i\textrm{ according to }\Phi\right\};\\
&\mathcal{P}_{j}(i)&:=&\{z\in\mathbb{E}\;:\;z_{k}=0\;\;\textrm{for all }k\in\Phi_{j}(i)\},\;j\in\mathcal{E}(i).\end{array}

(6.4)

Marginal process corresponding to a particular node
.

Fix a matching node
i_{0}
of
G
. Let

\mathcal{R}:=\mathcal{R}^{i_{0}}={\mathcal{V}}\backslash\big({i_{0}}\cup{\mathcal{E}}(i_{0})\big)=\left\{i_{1},\cdots,i_{|\mathcal{R}|}\right\}.

For
x,y\in\mathbb{E},
denote by
{\mathcal{A}}(x,y)
the infinitesimal generator of the queue process
Q.

For any
z\in\mathbb{E}
such that
z_{i_{0}}>0,
the only positive terms
\mathcal{A}(z,y),\;y\in\mathbb{E},
are given by

\left\{\begin{array}[]{llccc}\mathcal{A}(z,z+\textbf{e}_{i_{0}})=\lambda_{i_{0}};\\
\\
\mathcal{A}(z,z-\textbf{e}_{i_{0}})=\sum_{j\in{\mathcal{E}}(i_{0})}\left(\lambda_{j}{{\mathchoice{\rm 1\mskip-4.0mul}{\rm 1\mskip-4.0mul}{\rm 1\mskip-4.5mul}{\rm 1\mskip-5.0mul}}_{\mathcal{P}}}_{j}(i_{0})(z)\right);\\
\\
\mathcal{A}(z,z+\textbf{e}_{i_{\ell}})=\lambda_{i_{\ell}}{{\mathchoice{\rm 1\mskip-4.0mul}{\rm 1\mskip-4.0mul}{\rm 1\mskip-4.5mul}{\rm 1\mskip-5.0mul}}_{\mathcal{O}}}_{i_{\ell}}(z),\;\ell\in\llbracket 1,|\mathcal{R}|\rrbracket;\\
\\
\mathcal{A}(z,z-\textbf{e}_{i_{\ell}})={{\mathchoice{\rm 1\mskip-4.0mul}{\rm 1\mskip-4.0mul}{\rm 1\mskip-4.5mul}{\rm 1\mskip-5.0mul}}_{\mathcal{N}}}_{i_{\ell}}(z)\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}j\in{\mathcal{E}}(i_{0})\\
i_{0}\notin\Phi_{j}(i_{\ell})\end{subarray}}\left(\lambda_{j}{{\mathchoice{\rm 1\mskip-4.0mul}{\rm 1\mskip-4.0mul}{\rm 1\mskip-4.5mul}{\rm 1\mskip-5.0mul}}_{\mathcal{P}}}_{j}(i_{0})(z)\right),\;\ell\in\llbracket 1,|\mathcal{R}|\rrbracket.\end{array}\right.

(6.5)

Let
R:=R^{i_{0}}=\{R(t)\;:\;t\geq 0\}
denote the restriction of the process
Q
to the nodes of
\mathcal{R}
, i.e.,

R=(R_{1},R_{2},\cdots,R_{|\mathcal{R}|}):=\left(Q_{i_{1}},\cdots,Q_{i_{|\mathcal{R}|}}\right).

(6.6)

Define the set

\mathbb{E}^{\mathcal{R}}=\biggl\{z\in({\mathbb{Z}}^{+})^{|\mathcal{R}|}\;:\;z_{i_{k}}z_{i_{l}}=0,\textrm{ for any }i_{k}\in\mathcal{R},\\
\textrm{ and }i_{l}\in{\mathcal{E}}(i_{k})\textrm{ with }|z_{i_{m}}|\leq 1\textrm{ for any }i_{m}\in{\mathcal{V}}_{1}\biggl\}.

(6.7)

Conditionally on the
{i_{0}}
-th coordinate of
Q
being positive,
the process
R
clearly coincides in distribution with a Markov process

\mathcal{X}
on
\mathbb{E}^{\mathcal{R}}
. The latter will be termed
marginal Markov process
associated to node
i_{0}
.
The idea is as follows: using a stochastic averaging principle, as in

[
30
]
, showing that the marginal process reaches its stationary state
immediately at fluid scale. The drifts of the fluid limit of
Q
will then be a function of this stationary measure.

The FWLLN
. We consider the sequence of fluid-scaled processes
\{\bar{Q}_{n}\;:\;n\geq 1\}
, defined via
