\bar{Q}^{n}(t)=\frac{Q^{n}(t)}{n}:=\frac{Q(nt)}{n},\;t\geq 0,\;\;n\geq 1.

We also denote by
\mathcal{X}^{n}
the
n
-th marginal process corresponding to
i_{0},
defined by

\mathcal{X}^{n}(t)=\mathcal{X}(nt),\;t\geq 0,

and define

\bar{\mathcal{X}}^{n}(t)=\frac{\mathcal{X}(nt)}{n},\;t\geq 0,\;\;n\geq 1.

For the fluid analysis, we make two assumptions below,

ASSUMPTION 1
.

Q^{n}(0)\in\mathbb{E},
for any
n\geq 1,
and
\bar{Q}^{n}(0)\Rightarrow\bar{Q}(0)
as
n\longrightarrow\infty,
where
\bar{Q}(0)
is a deterministic element of
{\mathbb{R}}^{|{\mathcal{V}}|},
with
\bar{Q}_{i_{0}}(0)>0
and
\bar{Q}_{i}(0)=0,\;i\in{\mathcal{V}}\backslash\{i_{0}\}.

ASSUMPTION 2
. For all
n\geq 1,
the
\mathbb{E}^{\mathcal{R}}-
valued process
\mathcal{X}^{n}
is ergodic with stationary probalility
\pi^{n}.

For
n\geq 1,
let

\rho^{n}:=\rho^{n}(Q^{n}(0)):=\inf\{t\geq 0\;:\;Q_{i_{0}}^{n}(t)=0\},\;\textrm{ with }\inf\emptyset:=\infty.

(6.8)

In the case where
\mathbb{G}
is a graph, the following result was given as Theorem 4 of
[
38
]
. Hereafter, for a sequence of random variables

\{\rho^{n}\}
, we write
\rho^{n}\Rightarrow\rho
whenever
{\mathbb{P}}\left[\rho^{n}>M\right]\underset{n\to\infty}{\longrightarrow}1
for any
M\in{\mathbb{R}}
.

Theorem 6.1
.

(FWLLN) Let
\mathbb{G}
be a graph, and
(\mathbb{G},\Phi,\lambda)_{C}
be a matching queue such that
\Phi

is class-admissible. If, for some node
i_{0}

\lambda_{i_{0}}-\sum\limits_{j\in{\mathcal{E}}(i_{0})}\lambda_{j}\pi\left(\mathcal{P}_{j}^{\mathcal{R}}(i_{0})\right)<0,

(6.9)

for
\pi
stationary probability and
\mathcal{P}_{j}^{\mathcal{R}}(i_{0}),\;j\in{\mathcal{E}}(i_{0})
, then
\rho^{n}\Rightarrow\rho
in
{\mathbb{R}}
as
n\longrightarrow\infty,
for
\rho^{n}
in (
6.8
), where

\rho=\displaystyle\frac{\bar{Q}_{i_{0}}(0)}{\sum_{j\in{\mathcal{E}}(i_{0})}\lambda_{j}\pi\left(\mathcal{P}_{j}^{\mathcal{R}}(i_{0})\right)-\lambda_{i_{0}}}.

(6.10)

Otherwise,
\rho^{n}\Rightarrow\infty.
In either case,
\bar{Q}^{n}\Rightarrow\bar{Q}
in
\mathbb{D}^{|{\mathcal{V}}|}[0,\rho)
as
n\longrightarrow\infty,
where

\left\{\begin{aligned} \bar{Q}_{i_{0}}(t)&=\bar{Q}_{i_{0}}(0)+\left(\lambda_{i_{0}}-\sum\limits_{j\in{\mathcal{E}}(i_{0})}\lambda_{j}\pi\left(\mathcal{P}_{j}^{\mathcal{R}}(i_{0})\right)\right)t,\\
\bar{Q}_{i}(t)&=0,\qquad i\in{\mathcal{V}}\backslash\{i_{0}\}.\end{aligned}\right.

(6.11)

Notice that
\rho^{n}
going to infinity in the sense specified above readily
entails that the process
Q
cannot be positive recurrent, as is shown
in Lemma 1 of
[
38
]
, following Proposition 9.9 in
[
45
]
.
In other words,

Corollary 6.1
.

If
\rho^{n}\Rightarrow\infty
, for
\rho^{n}
in (
6.8
), then
({\mathbb{G}},\Phi,\lambda)_{C}
is unstable.

Now, if
G=({\mathcal{V}}_{1}\cup{\mathcal{V}}_{2},{\mathcal{E}})
is a multigraph,
for any
i\in{\mathcal{V}}_{1}
,
Q_{i}(0)
is always zero or one, so
the
i
-th coordinate of the fluid limit
\bar{Q}
is necessarily null at all times.
Moreover, it is immediate to observe the following,

Corollary 6.2
.

For any class-admissible policy
\Phi
, the conclusions of Theorem
6.1
and Corollary
6.1
remain valid
for a continuous-time model
(\mathbb{G},\Phi,\lambda)_{C}
on a multigraph

\mathbb{G}
if we assume that (
6.9
) holds
for some
i_{0}\in{\mathcal{V}}_{2}
.

In the Section
6.3
below, we will study examples of multigraphs for which the stationary probability of the marginal process can be explicitly computed, and so an explicit fluid limit and an explicit necessary condition of stability can be derived, respectively using Theorem
6.1
and Corollary
6.2
.
Moreover, using fluid stability arguments as in
[
18
]
will also prove that the latter conditions are also sufficient, thereby providing the exact stability region of the models under consideration.

6.3
Multigraphical cases study

In this section we present different examples of multigraph
G=({\mathcal{V}}={\mathcal{V}}_{1}\cup{\mathcal{V}}_{2},\mathcal{E})
and the corresponding matching queues
(G,\Phi,\lambda)_{C},
for the arrival-rate vector
\lambda:=(\lambda_{1},\cdots,\lambda_{|{\mathcal{V}}|})
and a priority policy
\Phi
that is
depicted by the arrows
on each dedicated figure. We deduce the precise stability regions of the corresponding stochastic matching models using the above fluid limit results.

6.3.1
Pendant graphs with a self-loop

In the subsection below, we present various multigraphs that consist of a pendant graph with a self-loop on a given vertex.

Pendant graph with a self-loop on the vertex 2

Consider the multigraph
G
depicted on Figure
6.1
such that
{\mathcal{V}}_{1}=\{2\}
,
{\mathcal{V}}_{2}=\{1,3,4\}
and
\mathcal{E}=\{\{1,2\},\{2\},\{2,3\},\{2,4\},\{3,4\}\}
.

1
2
3
4

Figure 6.1
:
Multigraph
G
with a self-loop on the vertex 2.

Proposition 6.1
.

Let
G
be the pendant graph with a self-loop on the vertex 2 and
\Phi
the matching policy depicted on Figure
6.1
. Consider an arrival-rate vector
\lambda\in\textsc{Ncond}_{C}(G),
i.e.,

\lambda_{1}<\lambda_{2},\qquad\lambda_{1}+\lambda_{3}<\lambda_{2}+\lambda_{4}\;\;\textrm{ and }\;\;\lambda_{1}+\lambda_{4}<\lambda_{2}+\lambda_{3}.

If
\bar{Q}^{n}(0)\Rightarrow x\textbf{e}_{1}
in
{\mathbb{R}}^{4}
for some
x\in{\mathbb{R}}^{++}
, then
\bar{Q}^{n}\Rightarrow\bar{Q}
in
{\mathbb{D}}^{4}[0,\rho)
as
n\to+\infty
, where

\bar{Q}(t)=(x+(\lambda_{1}-\lambda_{2}\alpha)t,0,0,0),\qquad 0\leq t<\rho,

for
\rho:=x/(\lambda_{2}\alpha-\lambda_{1})
if
\alpha>\lambda_{1}/\lambda_{2}
and
\rho:=\infty
otherwise, and for

\alpha:=\left[1+\frac{\lambda_{3}}{\lambda_{2}+\lambda_{4}-\lambda_{3}}+\frac{\lambda_{4}}{\lambda_{2}+\lambda_{3}-\lambda_{4}}\right]^{-1}=\frac{(\lambda_{2})^{2}-(\lambda_{3}-\lambda_{4})^{2}}{\lambda_{2}(\lambda_{2}+\lambda_{3}+\lambda_{4})}.

(6.12)

Proof.

The result follows from Corollary
6.2
by considering
i_{0}=1
. In that case the marginal process
\mathcal{X}
has the following infinitesimal generator
A^{\mathcal{R}}
, with
\mathcal{R}=\{3,4\},

\left\{\begin{array}[]{llc}\mathcal{A}^{\mathcal{R}}((i,0),(i+1,0))&=\lambda_{3};\\
\mathcal{A}^{\mathcal{R}}((i,0),(i-1,0))&=(\lambda_{2}+\lambda_{4}){\mathchoice{\rm 1\mskip-4.0mul}{\rm 1\mskip-4.0mul}{\rm 1\mskip-4.5mul}{\rm 1\mskip-5.0mul}}_{[1,+\infty)}(i);\\
\mathcal{A}^{\mathcal{R}}((i,0),(i,0))&=-\left(\lambda_{3}+(\lambda_{2}+\lambda_{4}){\mathchoice{\rm 1\mskip-4.0mul}{\rm 1\mskip-4.0mul}{\rm 1\mskip-4.5mul}{\rm 1\mskip-5.0mul}}_{[1,+\infty)}(i)\right);\\
\mathcal{A}^{\mathcal{R}}((0,j),(0,j+1))&=\lambda_{4};\\
\mathcal{A}^{\mathcal{R}}((0,j),(0,j-1))&=(\lambda_{2}+\lambda_{3}){\mathchoice{\rm 1\mskip-4.0mul}{\rm 1\mskip-4.0mul}{\rm 1\mskip-4.5mul}{\rm 1\mskip-5.0mul}}_{[1,+\infty)}(j);\\
\mathcal{A}^{\mathcal{R}}((0,j),(0,j))&=-\left(\lambda_{4}+(\lambda_{2}+\lambda_{3}){\mathchoice{\rm 1\mskip-4.0mul}{\rm 1\mskip-4.0mul}{\rm 1\mskip-4.5mul}{\rm 1\mskip-5.0mul}}_{[1,+\infty)}(j)\right);\\
\end{array}\right.

then we get,

\left\{\begin{array}[]{lll}\displaystyle-\lambda_{3}\pi(0,0)+(\lambda_{2}+\lambda_{4})\pi(1,0)=0\\
\;\;\;\displaystyle\lambda_{3}\pi(0,0)-(\lambda_{2}+\lambda_{3}+\lambda_{4})\pi(1,0)+(\lambda_{2}+\lambda_{4})\pi(2,0)=0\\
\;\;\;\displaystyle\lambda_{3}\pi(1,0)-(\lambda_{2}+\lambda_{3}+\lambda_{4})\pi(2,0)+(\lambda_{2}+\lambda_{4})\pi(3,0)=0\\
\;\;\;\qquad\ldots\quad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\ldots\quad\qquad\qquad\qquad\ldots=0\end{array}\right.

\Longleftrightarrow\left\{\begin{array}[]{lll}\displaystyle\pi(1,0)=\left(\frac{\lambda_{3}}{\lambda_{2}+\lambda_{4}}\right)\pi(0,0)\\
\\
\displaystyle\pi(2,0)=\left(\frac{\lambda_{3}}{\lambda_{2}+\lambda_{4}}\right)\pi(1,0)\\
\\
\displaystyle\pi(3,0)=\left(\frac{\lambda_{3}}{\lambda_{2}+\lambda_{4}}\right)\pi(2,0)\\
\qquad\quad\;\vdots\end{array}\right.\qquad\Longleftrightarrow\begin{array}[]{lll}\displaystyle\pi(i,0)=\left(\frac{\lambda_{3}}{\lambda_{2}+\lambda_{4}}\right)\pi(i-1,0)&;&i\geq 1.\end{array}

Set
\pi(0,0)=\alpha,
so, for any state
x\in\mathbb{E}^{\mathcal{R}}
we have,

\pi(x)=\left\{\begin{array}[]{cccc}\alpha\left(\displaystyle\frac{\lambda_{3}}{\lambda_{2}+\lambda_{4}}\right)^{i}&x=(i,0),\;i\geq 1\\
&\\
\alpha\left(\displaystyle\frac{\lambda_{4}}{\lambda_{2}+\lambda_{3}}\right)^{j}&x=(0,j),\;j\geq 1.\\
\end{array}\right.

Then we have,

\displaystyle 1

\displaystyle=\sum\limits_{i\geq 1}\pi(i,0)+\sum\limits_{j\geq 1}\pi(0,j)+\pi(0,0)

\displaystyle=\pi(0,0)\left[\sum\limits_{i\geq 1}\pi(i,0)+\sum\limits_{j\geq 1}\pi(0,j)+1\right]

\displaystyle=\pi(0,0)\left[\frac{\lambda_{3}}{\lambda_{2}+\lambda_{4}-\lambda_{3}}+\frac{\lambda_{4}}{\lambda_{2}+\lambda_{3}-\lambda_{4}}+1\right].

The stationary distribution
\pi
of this reversible CTMC is unique, so Assumption 2 holds. The stated convergence of
Q^{n}
to the fluid limits follows from Corollary
6.2
.
∎

Proposition 6.2
.

The matching queue
(G,\Phi,\lambda)_{C}
corresponding to the pendant graph
G
with a self-loop on the vertex 2 and the priority type
\Phi
depicted on Figure
6.1
, is stable if and only if
\textsc{Ncond}_{C}(G)
holds together with

\lambda_{1}<\alpha\lambda_{2}\;\;\;\textrm{ for }\alpha\textrm{ in }(\ref{eq:alphaPendantVertex2}).

Proof.

It follows from Proposition
6.1
that, for any initial condition of the form
(x,0,0,0),\;x>0
, the fluid limit
\bar{Q}
will hit the origin if and only if
\lambda_{1}<\alpha\lambda_{2}
.

Assume that
\bar{Q}_{1}(0)>0
. Then at most one of
\bar{Q}_{3}(0)
or
\bar{Q}_{4}(0)>0
. Say
\bar{Q}_{3}(0)>0.
According to the priority of 3 over 1, then

\bar{Q}_{3}(t)=\bar{Q}_{3}(0)+(\lambda_{3}-\lambda_{2}-\lambda_{4})t,\;\;0\leq t\leq\frac{\bar{Q}_{3}(0)}{\lambda_{2}+\lambda_{4}-\lambda_{3}}.

In particular, the fluid process
\bar{Q}_{3}
will hit
0
in finite time, so that
\bar{Q}_{3}
will hit
the origin in finite time by Proposition
6.1
. A similar argument applies to the case where
\bar{Q}_{4}(0)>0.

Assume now that
\bar{Q}_{2}(0)>0,
there exists
t_{0}
such that
\forall t>t_{0},\;\bar{Q}_{2}(t)=0
(self-loop on vertex 2.)
Now, since the prelimit processes
Q_{i},\;i=2,3,4
have drifts towards 0 whenever any of them is strictly positive, the fluid limit must remain in state 0 after hitting this state, and Proposition
6.1
shows that
\bar{Q}_{1}
will also remain fixed at 0 after hitting that state.
Thus, the ergodicity of the system follows from

[
18
, Theorem 4.2]
.
∎

Proposition 6.3
.

We have the strict inclusion

\left\{\lambda_{1}<\alpha\lambda_{2}\right\}\cap\textsc{Ncond}_{C}(G)\subsetneq\textsc{Ncond}_{C}(G).

Proof.

Fix
\varepsilon\in(0,2/5]
and set

\left\{\begin{array}[]{cl}\lambda_{1}=&\varepsilon/2;\\
\lambda_{2}=&\varepsilon;\\
\lambda_{3}=&\lambda_{4}=1/2-3\varepsilon/4.\\
\end{array}\right.

Clearly,
\lambda
belong to the set
\textsc{Ncond}_{C}(G),
but not to
\{\lambda_{1}<\alpha\lambda_{2}\}
, since

\lambda_{1}-\alpha\lambda_{2}=\varepsilon/2-\frac{\varepsilon^{2}}{\varepsilon+1-3\varepsilon/2}=\varepsilon/2-\frac{2\varepsilon^{2}}{2-\varepsilon}\geq 0.

∎

Pendant graph with a self-loop on the vertex
3

Consider the multigraph
G
depicted on Figure
6.2
such that
{\mathcal{V}}_{1}=\{3\}
,
{\mathcal{V}}_{2}=\{1,2,4\}
and
\mathcal{E}=\{\{1,2\},\{2,3\},\{2,4\},\{3\},\{3,4\}\}
.

1
2
3
4

Figure 6.2
:
Multigraph
G
with a self-loop on the vertex 3.

Proposition 6.4
.

Let
G
be the pendant graph with a self-loop on the vertex 3 and
\Phi
the matching policy depicted on Figure
6.2
above. Consider an arrival-rate vector
\lambda\in\textsc{Ncond}_{C}(G),
i.e.,

\lambda_{1}<\lambda_{2}<\lambda_{1}+\lambda_{3}+\lambda_{4},\quad\textrm{ and }\quad\lambda_{1}+\lambda_{4}<\lambda_{2}+\lambda_{3}.

If
\bar{Q}^{n}(0)\Rightarrow x\textbf{e}_{1}
in
{\mathbb{R}}^{4}
for some
x\in{\mathbb{R}}^{++}
, then
\bar{Q}^{n}\Rightarrow\bar{Q}
in
{\mathbb{D}}^{4}[0,\rho)
as
n\to+\infty
, where

\bar{Q}(t)=(x+(\lambda_{1}-\lambda_{2}\alpha)t,0,0,0),\qquad 0\leq t<\rho,

for
\rho:=x/(\lambda_{2}\alpha-\lambda_{1})
if
\alpha>\lambda_{1}/\lambda_{2}
and
\rho:=\infty
otherwise, and for

\alpha:=\left[1+\frac{\lambda_{3}}{\lambda_{2}+\lambda_{3}+\lambda_{4}}+\frac{\lambda_{4}}{\lambda_{2}+\lambda_{3}-\lambda_{4}}\right]^{-1}=\displaystyle\frac{(\lambda_{2}+\lambda_{3})^{2}-\lambda_{4}^{2}}{\lambda_{2}^{2}+2\lambda_{3}^{2}+3\lambda_{2}\lambda_{3}+\lambda_{2}\lambda_{4}}.

(6.13)

Proof.

We prove with the same argue for Proposition
6.1
.
Set
\pi(0,0)=\alpha,
we get that

\displaystyle\pi(x)=\left\{\begin{array}[]{lll}\displaystyle\frac{\alpha\lambda_{3}}{\lambda_{2}+\lambda_{3}+\lambda_{4}}&x=(1,0);\\
&\\
\alpha\left(\displaystyle\frac{\lambda_{4}}{\lambda_{2}+\lambda_{3}}\right)^{j}&x=(0,j),\;j\geq 1.\\
\end{array}\right.

Again, the stationary distribution
\pi
of this reversible CTMC is unique, so Assumption 2 holds. The stated convergence of
Q^{n}
to the fluid limits follows from Corollary
6.2
.
∎

Proposition 6.5
.

The matching queue
(G,\Phi,\lambda)_{C}
corresponding to the pendant graph
G
with a self-loop on the vertex 3 and the priority type
\Phi
depicted on Figure
6.2
, is stable if and only if
\textsc{Ncond}_{C}(G)
holds together with

\lambda_{1}<\alpha\lambda_{2}\;\;\;\textrm{ for }\alpha\textrm{ in }(\ref{eq:alphaPendantVertex3}).

Proof.

Assume that
\bar{Q}_{1}(0)>0.
It follows from the Proposition
6.4
that, for any initial condition of the form
(x,0,0,0),\;x>0
, the fluid limit
\bar{Q}
will hit the origin if and only if
\lambda_{1}<\alpha\lambda_{2}
.

Assume now that
\bar{Q}_{2}(0)>0,
then we have

\bar{Q}_{2}(t)=\bar{Q}_{2}(0)+(\lambda_{2}-\lambda_{1}-\lambda_{3}-\lambda_{4})t,\;\;0\leq t\leq\frac{\bar{Q}_{2}(0)}{\lambda_{1}+\lambda_{3}+\lambda_{4}-\lambda_{2}}.

According to
\lambda\in\textsc{Ncond}_{C}(G)

the fluid queue hits the origin in finite time.

Assume that
\bar{Q}_{1}(0)>0,
with at most one of
\bar{Q}_{3}(0)
or
\bar{Q}_{4}(0)>0
. Set
\bar{Q}_{3}(0)>0
then it is equal to 1. So there exists
t_{0}>0
such that

\bar{Q}_{3}(t)=0,\;\;\forall t>t_{0}.

Now assume that
\bar{Q}_{1}(0)>0,
and
\bar{Q}_{4}(0)>0,
then according to the priority of 4 over 1, then

\bar{Q}_{4}(t)=\bar{Q}_{4}(0)+(\lambda_{4}-\lambda_{2}-\lambda_{3})t,\;\;0\leq t\leq\frac{\bar{Q}_{4}(0)}{\lambda_{2}+\lambda_{3}-\lambda_{4}}.

According to
\lambda\in\textsc{Ncond}_{C}(G),
in particular, the fluid process
\bar{Q}_{4}
will hit
0
in finite time, so that
\bar{Q}_{4}
will hit
the origin in finite time by Proposition
6.4
.

Now, since the prelimit processes
Q_{i},\;i=2,3,4
have drifts towards 0 whenever any of them is strictly positive, and Proposition
6.4
shows that
\bar{Q}_{1}
will also remain fixed at 0 after hitting that state. Thus, the ergodicity of the system follows again from
[
18
, Theorem 4.2]
.
∎

Proposition 6.6
.

We have the strict inclusion

\left\{\lambda_{1}<\alpha\lambda_{2}\right\}\cap\textsc{Ncond}_{C}(G)\subsetneq\textsc{Ncond}_{C}(G).

Proof.

Fix
\varepsilon\in(0,1/3]
and set

\left\{\begin{array}[]{cl}\lambda_{1}=&\varepsilon/2;\\
\lambda_{2}=&\varepsilon.\\
\lambda_{3}=&\lambda_{4}=1/2-3\varepsilon/4;\\
\end{array}\right.

Clearly,
\lambda
belongs to
\textsc{Ncond}_{C}(G),
but
not to
\{\lambda_{1}<\alpha\lambda_{2}\}
, since

\lambda_{1}-\alpha\lambda_{2}=\varepsilon/2-\frac{\left(\varepsilon+1/2-3\varepsilon/4\right)^{2}-\left(1/2-3\varepsilon/4\right)^{2}}{\varepsilon^{2}+2\left(1/2-3\varepsilon/4\right)^{2}+4\varepsilon\left(1/2-3\varepsilon/4\right)}\left(\varepsilon\right)=\varepsilon/2-\frac{8\varepsilon^{2}-4\varepsilon^{3}}{-7\varepsilon^{2}+4\varepsilon+4}\geq 0.

∎

Complete bipartite graphs with a self-loop

Complete bipartite graph of order 3 with a self-loop on the vertex 2

Consider the multigraph

depicted on Figure

such that

,

and

.

:

Proposition 6.7
.

Let
G
be the bipartite graph with a self-loop on the vertex 2 and
\Phi
the matching policy depicted on Figure
6.3
above. Consider an arrival-rate vector

\lambda\in\textsc{Ncond}_{C}(G)=\{\lambda_{1}+\lambda_{3}<\lambda_{2}\}.

If
\bar{Q}^{n}(0)\Rightarrow x\textbf{e}_{1}
in
{\mathbb{R}}^{3}
for some
x\in{\mathbb{R}}^{++}
, then
\bar{Q}^{n}\Rightarrow\bar{Q}
in
{\mathbb{D}}^{3}[0,\rho)
as
n\to+\infty
, where

\bar{Q}(t)=(x+(\lambda_{1}-\alpha\lambda_{2})t,0,0),\qquad 0\leq t<\rho,

for
\rho:=x/(\lambda_{2}\alpha-\lambda_{1})
if
\alpha>\lambda_{1}/\lambda_{2}
and
\rho:=\infty
otherwise, and for

\alpha:=\left[1+\frac{\lambda_{3}}{\lambda_{2}-\lambda_{3}}\right]^{-1}=\displaystyle\frac{\lambda_{2}-\lambda_{3}}{\lambda_{2}}.

(6.14)

Proof.

We argue as above. Setting
\pi(0)=\alpha,
for any
i\geq 1,
we get that

\pi(i)=\alpha\left(\displaystyle\frac{\lambda_{3}}{\lambda_{2}}\right)^{i},

and the result follows again from Corollary
6.2
.
∎

Proposition 6.8
.

The matching queue
(G,\Phi,\lambda)_{C}
corresponding to the complete bipartite graph with a self-loop on the vertex 2 depicted on Figure
6.3
and for all matching policy
\Phi
,
(G,\textsc{fcfm},\mu)_{C}
is stable if and only if
\mu
belongs to the set
\textsc{Ncond}_{C}(G)
.

Proof.

First, consider that the priority type policy that depicted on Figure
6.3
, we have:

Assume that that
\bar{Q}_{1}(0)>0,
and
\bar{Q}_{3}(0)>0,
then according to the priority of 3 over 1, then

\bar{Q}_{3}(t)=\bar{Q}_{3}(0)+(\lambda_{3}-\lambda_{2})t,\;\;0\leq t\leq\frac{\bar{Q}_{3}(0)}{\lambda_{2}-\lambda_{3}}.

According to
\lambda\in\textsc{Ncond}_{C}(G),
in particular, the fluid process
\bar{Q}_{3}
will hit
0
in finite time, so that
\bar{Q}_{3}
will hit
the origin in finite time by Proposition
6.7
.

Now assume that
\bar{Q}_{1}(0)>0,
it follows from the proposition
6.7
that, for any initial condition of the form
(x,0,0),\;x>0
, the fluid limit
\bar{Q}
will hit the origin if and only if
\lambda_{1}<\alpha\lambda_{2}.

Indeed, we have

\displaystyle\lambda_{1}

\displaystyle<\alpha\lambda_{2}

\displaystyle\iff\lambda_{1}

\displaystyle<\left(\frac{\lambda_{2}-\lambda_{3}}{\lambda_{2}}\right)\lambda_{2}

\displaystyle\iff\lambda_{1}

\displaystyle<\lambda_{2}-\lambda_{3}.

For this, it suffices that
\lambda\in\textsc{Ncond}_{C}(G).

However, by symmetry between the vertices 1 and 3, we conclude that for all class-admissible matching policy
\Phi,
we have

\textsc{Stab}_{C}(G,\Phi)=\textsc{Ncond}_{C}(G).
See Theorem
5.3
(i).
∎

q=4

Proposition 6.9
.

Let
G
be the bipartite graph with a self-loop on the vertex 1 and
\Phi
the matching policy depicted on Figure
6.4
(a). Consider an arrival-rate vector

\lambda\in\textsc{Ncond}_{C}(G)=\{\lambda_{2}+\lambda_{4}<\lambda_{1}+\lambda_{3},\;\textrm{ and }\;\lambda_{3}<\lambda_{2}+\lambda_{4}\}.

If
\bar{Q}^{n}(0)\Rightarrow x\textbf{e}_{3}
in
{\mathbb{R}}^{4}
for some
x\in{\mathbb{R}}^{++}
, then
\bar{Q}^{n}\Rightarrow\bar{Q}
in
{\mathbb{D}}^{4}[0,\rho)
as
n\to+\infty
, where

\bar{Q}(t)=(0,0,x+(\lambda_{3}-\alpha_{1}(\lambda_{2}+\lambda_{4}))t,0),\qquad 0\leq t<\rho,

for
\rho:=x/((\lambda_{2}+\lambda_{4})\alpha_{1}-\lambda_{3})
if
\alpha_{1}>\lambda_{3}/(\lambda_{2}+\lambda_{4})
and
\rho:=\infty
otherwise, and for

\alpha_{1}=\left[1+\displaystyle\frac{\lambda_{1}}{\lambda_{1}+\lambda_{2}+\lambda_{4}}\right]^{-1}=\displaystyle\frac{\lambda_{1}+\lambda_{2}+\lambda_{4}}{2\lambda_{1}+\lambda_{2}+\lambda_{4}}.

(6.15)

Proof.

We argue as above, by computing explicitly the unique stationary distribution of the reversible marginal process.
∎

Proposition 6.10
.

The matching queue
(G,\Phi,\lambda)_{C}
corresponding to the complete bipartite graph
G
with a self-loop on the vertex 1 and the priority type
\Phi
depicted on Figure
6.4
(a), is stable if and only if
\textsc{Ncond}_{C}(G)
holds together with

\lambda_{3}<(\lambda_{2}+\lambda_{4})\alpha_{1}

for
\alpha_{1}
in (
6.15
).

Proof.

Assume that
\bar{Q}_{2}(0)>0,
then according to the priority of 2 over 4, then

\bar{Q}_{2}(t)=\bar{Q}_{2}(0)+(\lambda_{2}-(\lambda_{1}+\lambda_{3}))t\qquad 0\leq t<\frac{\bar{Q}_{2}(0)}{\lambda_{1}+\lambda_{3}-\lambda_{2}}.

Now assume that
\bar{Q}_{4}(0)>0,
so we have

\bar{Q}_{4}(t)=\bar{Q}_{4}(0)+(\lambda_{4}-(\lambda_{1}+\lambda_{3})\alpha_{2})t\qquad 0\leq t<\frac{\bar{Q}_{4}(0)}{(\lambda_{1}+\lambda_{3})\alpha_{2}-\lambda_{4}},

for a simple calculs we get,
\alpha_{2}=\displaystyle\frac{\lambda_{1}+\lambda_{3}-\lambda_{2}}{\lambda_{1}+\lambda_{3}}.

Observe that
\lambda_{4}-(\lambda_{1}+\lambda_{3})\alpha_{2}=\lambda_{2}+\lambda_{4}-(\lambda_{1}+\lambda_{3})
, which is negative according to
\textsc{Ncond}_{C}(G).

Now assume that
\bar{Q}_{3}(0)>0,
so we have

\bar{Q}_{3}(t)=\bar{Q}_{3}(0)+(\lambda_{3}-(\lambda_{2}+\lambda_{4})\alpha_{1})t\qquad 0\leq t<\frac{\bar{Q}_{3}(0)}{(\lambda_{2}+\lambda_{4})\alpha_{1}-\lambda_{3}},

it follows from the Proposition
6.9
that, for any initial condition of the form
(x,0,0,0),\;x>0
, the fluid limit
\bar{Q}
will hit the origin if and only if
\lambda_{3}<\alpha_{1}(\lambda_{2}+\lambda_{4}).

∎

Proposition 6.11
.

We have the strict inclusion

\left\{\lambda_{3}<(\lambda_{2}+\lambda_{4})\alpha_{1}\right\}\cap\textsc{Ncond}_{C}(G)\subsetneq\textsc{Ncond}_{C}(G).

Proof.

Fix
\varepsilon\in(0,4/7]
and set

\left\{\begin{array}[]{cl}\lambda_{1}=&1-5\varepsilon/4;\\
\lambda_{2}=&\lambda_{4}=\varepsilon/2;\\
\lambda_{3}=&3\varepsilon/4.\\
\end{array}\right.

Clearly,
\lambda
belong to
\textsc{Ncond}_{C}(G),
but not to the set
\{\lambda_{3}<(\lambda_{2}+\lambda_{4})\alpha_{1}\}
, since

\lambda_{3}-(\lambda_{2}+\lambda_{4})\alpha_{1}=\frac{3\varepsilon}{4}-(\varepsilon)\frac{1-5\varepsilon/4+\varepsilon}{2-5\varepsilon/2+\varepsilon}=\frac{3\varepsilon}{4}-(\varepsilon)\frac{4-\varepsilon}{8-6\varepsilon}\geq 0.

∎

Remark 6.1
.

Obseve that the multigraph

and

-favorable depicted on Figure

\bar{Q}_{3}(t)=\bar{Q}_{3}(0)+(\lambda_{3}-\lambda_{2}-\lambda_{4})t\qquad 0\leq t<\frac{\bar{Q}_{3}(0)}{\lambda_{2}+\lambda_{4}-\lambda_{3}},

which satisfies the Theorem
5.3
. Thus for all
\Phi={\mathcal{V}}_{2}
-favorable
(G,{\mathcal{V}}_{2}-\textrm{favorable},\mu)_{C}
is stable if and only if
\mu
belongs to the set
\textsc{Ncond}_{C}(G)
.

6.4
Hypergraphical cases study

In this section, we develop an example of a hypergraph in which we apply the fluid limits technique presented in Section

with some adaptation for hypregraphs and we find the results proved in Theorem

for complete

6.4.1
Complete
3
-uniform hypergraph of order 4

First of all, given a complete
3
-unifom hypergraph
{\mathbb{H}}=({\mathcal{V}},{\mathcal{H}})
of order
q\geq 4,
then observe that
\mathcal{N}^{-}_{3}({\mathbb{H}})=\left\{\mu(i)<\frac{1}{3},\;\forall i\in{\mathcal{V}}\right\}=\left\{\mu(i)<\frac{1}{2}\sum_{j\in{\mathcal{V}}\backslash\{i\}}\mu(j),\;i\in{\mathcal{V}}\right\}.

Proposition 6.12
.

Let
{\mathbb{H}}=({\mathcal{V}},{\mathcal{H}})
be a complete
3
-uniform hypergraph of order 4 and for all matching policy
\Phi
. Consider an arrival-rate vector
\lambda\in\left(\mathscr{N}^{-}_{3}\right)_{C}({\mathbb{H}})
i.e.

\left\{\lambda_{i}<\frac{1}{2}\sum_{j\in{\mathcal{V}}\backslash\{i\}}\lambda(j),\;i\in{\mathcal{V}}\right\}.

1.

If
\bar{Q}^{n}(0)\Rightarrow x\textbf{e}_{1}
in
{\mathbb{R}}^{4}
for some
x\in{\mathbb{R}}^{++}
, then
\bar{Q}^{n}\Rightarrow\bar{Q}
in
{\mathbb{D}}^{4}[0,\rho)
as
n\to+\infty
, where

\bar{Q}(t)=(x+(\lambda_{1}-\alpha_{2}(\lambda_{3}+\lambda_{4})-\alpha_{3}(\lambda_{2}+\lambda_{4})-\alpha_{4}(\lambda_{2}+\lambda_{3}))t,0,0,0),\qquad 0\leq t<\rho,

for
\rho:=x/(\alpha_{2}(\lambda_{3}+\lambda_{4})+\alpha_{3}(\lambda_{2}+\lambda_{4})+\alpha_{4}(\lambda_{2}+\lambda_{3})-\lambda_{1}),
with

\left\{\begin{array}[]{cccc}\alpha_{2}&=\displaystyle\frac{(\lambda_{3}-\lambda_{4})^{2}+\lambda_{2}(\lambda_{3}+\lambda_{4})}{\lambda_{2}^{2}-(\lambda_{3}-\lambda_{4})^{2}};\\
&\\
\alpha_{3}&=\displaystyle\frac{(\lambda_{2}-\lambda_{4})^{2}+\lambda_{3}(\lambda_{2}+\lambda_{4})}{\lambda_{3}^{2}-(\lambda_{2}-\lambda_{4})^{2}};\\
&\\
\alpha_{4}&=\displaystyle\frac{(\lambda_{2}-\lambda_{3})^{2}+\lambda_{4}(\lambda_{2}+\lambda_{3})}{\lambda_{4}^{2}-(\lambda_{2}-\lambda_{3})^{2}}.\end{array}\right.

(6.16)

2.

If
\bar{Q}^{n}(0)\Rightarrow x\textbf{e}_{1}+y\textbf{e}_{2}
in
{\mathbb{R}}^{4}
for some
x,y\in{\mathbb{R}}^{++}
, then
\bar{Q}^{n}\Rightarrow\bar{Q}^{\prime}
in
{\mathbb{D}}^{4}[0,\rho)
as
n\to+\infty
, where

\bar{Q}^{\prime}(t)=(x+(\lambda_{1}-\lambda_{3}-\lambda_{4}))t,y+(\lambda_{2}-\lambda_{3}-\lambda_{4}))t,0,0),\qquad 0\leq t<\rho,

for
\rho:=\min\left(x/(\lambda_{3}+\lambda_{4}-\lambda_{1}),y/(\lambda_{3}+\lambda_{4}-\lambda_{2})\right).

Proof.

1.

The marginal process is
\mathcal{R}=\{2,3,4\}.
Then, similarly to the above resolution, we deduce that

\displaystyle\pi(x)=\left\{\begin{array}[]{ccccc}\pi(0,0,0)\left(\displaystyle\frac{\lambda_{2}}{\lambda_{3}+\lambda_{4}}\right)^{i},&x=(i,0,0)&i\geq 1;\\
&\\
\pi(0,0,0)\left(\displaystyle\frac{\lambda_{3}}{\lambda_{2}+\lambda_{4}}\right)^{j},&x=(0,j,0)&j\geq 1;\\
&\\
\pi(0,0,0)\left(\displaystyle\frac{\lambda_{4}}{\lambda_{2}+\lambda_{3}}\right)^{k},&x=(0,0,k)&k\geq 1.\end{array}\right.

Set
\pi(0,0,0)=\pi(0),
and for any
i,j,k\geq 1,
we set the following,

\pi(i,0,0)=\pi_{2}(i),\quad\pi(0,j,0)=\pi_{3}(j)\textrm{ and }\pi(0,0,k)=\pi_{3}(k).

\displaystyle 1

\displaystyle=\sum\limits_{i\geq 1}\pi_{1}(i)+\sum\limits_{j\geq 1}\pi_{2}(j)+\sum\limits_{k\geq 1}\pi_{3}(k)+\pi(0)

\displaystyle=\pi(0)\left[\sum\limits_{i\geq 1}\left(\frac{\lambda_{2}}{\lambda_{3}+\lambda_{4}}\right)^{i}+\sum\limits_{j\geq 1}\left(\frac{\lambda_{3}}{\lambda_{2}+\lambda_{4}}\right)^{j}+\sum\limits_{k\geq 1}\left(\frac{\lambda_{4}}{\lambda_{2}+\lambda_{3}}\right)^{k}+1\right]

\displaystyle=\pi(0)\left[\frac{\lambda_{2}}{\lambda_{3}+\lambda_{4}-\lambda_{2}}+\frac{\lambda_{3}}{\lambda_{2}+\lambda_{4}-\lambda_{3}}+\frac{\lambda_{4}}{\lambda_{2}+\lambda_{3}-\lambda_{4}}+1\right]

\displaystyle=\pi(0)\left[\displaystyle\frac{4\lambda_{2}\lambda_{3}\lambda_{4}}{(\lambda_{3}+\lambda_{4}-\lambda_{2})(\lambda_{2}+\lambda_{4}-\lambda_{3})(\lambda_{2}+\lambda_{3}-\lambda_{4})}\right].

Then we have,

\pi(0)=\displaystyle\frac{(\lambda_{3}+\lambda_{4}-\lambda_{2})(\lambda_{2}+\lambda_{4}-\lambda_{3})(\lambda_{2}+\lambda_{3}-\lambda_{4})}{4\lambda_{2}\lambda_{3}\lambda_{4}}.

Then the queue
\bar{Q}_{1}
be as follow,

\displaystyle\bar{Q}_{1}(t)

\displaystyle=\bar{Q}_{1}(0)+\left(\lambda_{1}-\lambda_{2}\left(\sum\limits_{j\geq 1}\pi_{3}(j)+\sum\limits_{k\geq 1}\pi_{4}(k)\right)\right.

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\qquad\;\;\;\left.-\lambda_{3}\left(\sum\limits_{i\geq 1}\pi_{2}(i)+\sum\limits_{k\geq 1}\pi_{4}(k)\right)-\lambda_{4}\left(\sum\limits_{i\geq 1}\pi_{2}(i)+\sum\limits_{j\geq 1}\pi_{3}(j)\right)\right)t

\displaystyle=\bar{Q}_{1}(0)+\left(\lambda_{1}-\pi(0)\left[\lambda_{2}\left(\frac{\lambda_{3}}{\lambda_{2}+\lambda_{4}-\lambda_{3}}+\frac{\lambda_{4}}{\lambda_{2}+\lambda_{3}-\lambda_{4}}\right)\right.\right.\qquad\qquad\;\;

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad+\lambda_{3}\left(\frac{\lambda_{2}}{\lambda_{3}+\lambda_{4}-\lambda_{2}}+\frac{\lambda_{4}}{\lambda_{2}+\lambda_{3}-\lambda_{4}}\right)\qquad

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad+\left.\left.\lambda_{4}\left(\frac{\lambda_{3}}{\lambda_{2}+\lambda_{4}-\lambda_{3}}+\frac{\lambda_{2}}{\lambda_{3}+\lambda_{4}-\lambda_{2}}\right)\right]\right)t

\displaystyle=\bar{Q}_{1}(0)+\left[\lambda_{1}-\displaystyle\frac{(\lambda_{3}+\lambda_{4}-\lambda_{2})(\lambda_{2}+\lambda_{4}-\lambda_{3})(\lambda_{2}+\lambda_{3}-\lambda_{4})}{4\lambda_{2}\lambda_{3}\lambda_{4}}\right.\qquad\quad

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\left.\left(\frac{2\lambda_{2}\lambda_{3}\lambda_{4}(\lambda_{2}+\lambda_{3}+\lambda_{4})}{(\lambda_{3}+\lambda_{4}-\lambda_{2})(\lambda_{2}+\lambda_{4}-\lambda_{3})(\lambda_{2}+\lambda_{3}-\lambda_{4})}\right)\right]t

\displaystyle=\bar{Q}_{1}(0)+\left(\lambda_{1}-\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}\left(\lambda_{2}+\lambda_{3}+\lambda_{4}\right)\right)t,\quad\forall t>0.\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\quad\;

Therefore, similarly we obtain that for any
i\in{\mathcal{V}},\;\exists t_{0}>0,\;\textrm{any }\bar{Q}_{i}(t)<0,\;\forall t>t_{0}.

2.

Observe that, for any
i,j\in{\mathcal{V}}
, we have
{\mathcal{V}}\backslash\{i,j\}
be a transversal of
\mathbb{H}
and

\sum_{k\in{\mathcal{V}}\backslash\{i,j\}}\mu(k)>1/3,
then
\sum_{k\in{\mathcal{V}}\backslash\{i,j\}}\mu(k)>\mu(i)
and
\sum_{k\in{\mathcal{V}}\backslash\{i,j\}}\mu(k)>\mu(j).
In other words, for any
i,j\in{\mathcal{V}},
we have
\displaystyle\lambda_{{\mathcal{V}}\backslash\{i,j\}}>\lambda_{i}
and
\displaystyle\lambda_{{\mathcal{V}}\backslash\{i,j\}}>\lambda_{j}.

Now, for all
t\geq 0
we have,
\bar{Q}_{1}(t)=\bar{Q}_{1}(0)+\left(\lambda_{1}-\lambda_{3}-\lambda_{4}\right)t,\quad\forall t>0.

Then, as a result of the observation above, there exists
t_{0}>0
such that

\bar{Q}_{1}(t)<0
for all
t\geq t_{0}
. Similarly, we deduce that there exists
t_{1}>0
such that
\bar{Q}_{2}(t)<0
for all
t\geq t_{1}
. We conclude that there exists
t\geq t_{0}\vee t_{1}
the prelimit process
Q_{i},\;i=1,2
have drifts towards 0 whenever any them is striclty positive, then
\bar{Q}_{1}
and
\bar{Q}_{2}
will remain fixed at 0 after hitting that state. Thus the ergodicity of the sytem follows again from
[
18
, Theorem 4.2]
.

∎

Observe that, the complete 3-uniform
k
-partite hypergraph is just the complete 3-uniform hypergraph of order
k,
where all the sets
I_{1},...,I_{k}
are of cardinality 1 (i.e., there are no replica). In particular case the complete 3-uniform
4
-partite hypergraph is the complete 3-uniform hypergraph of order
4.

6.5
Discussion of results and conclusion

In this chapter, we have generalized stochastic matching models to the continuous-time settings. Items of different classes arrive at the system according to an independent Poisson process of intensity
\lambda.
We addressed a new technique that allows us to explicitly derive the stability region of the models at hand, by using fluid limits techniques, to prove the existence of a local steady-state, rather than applying Lyapunov techniques. The scaling consists of speeding up time by the norm of the initial state. The behavior of such rescaled stochastic
processes is analyzed when the scaling parameter goes to infinity. In Corollary
6.2
, we generalized the
[
38
, Theorem 4]
to multigraphs. The main difference of graphs, that is, for any
i\in{\mathcal{V}},\;Q_{i}(0)
is always zero or one, so the
i
-th coordinate of the fluid limit
\bar{Q},
that is, necessarily null at all times.

Moreover, we have studied several examples of multigraphs for which the stationary probability of the marginal process can be explicitly computed. We have studied some cases of pendant graph with a self-loop on a vertex and we distinguish that the stability region is strictly included in the necessary condition
\textsc{Ncond}_{C}.
However, we retrieve the results of Theorem
5.3
dedicated to the exact stability of the model by considering the complete bipartite graphs with a self-loop on a vertex with the
{\mathcal{V}}_{2}
-favorable matching policy.

In Section
6.4
, also by using fluid limit arguments, we provided the exact stability region to the model for complete 3-uniform hypergraphs of order 4.

Chapter 7
Comparison of models for organ transplant applications

7.1
Introduction

A blood type (also called a blood group) is defined as the classification of blood-based on the presence or absence of inherited antigenic substances on the surface of red blood cells (RBCs). A series of related blood types constitute a blood group ABO system, see
[
50
]
.

We set the blood types as follows:

•

Blood Type A
- If the red blood cell contains only "A" molecules.

•

Blood Type B
- If the red blood cell contains only "B" molecules.

•

Blood Type AB
- If the red blood cell contains a mixture of molecules "A" and "B".

•

Blood Type O
- If the red blood cell has neither "A" nor "B" molecules.

Donating Blood by Compatible Type

Blood groups are very important when a transfusion is required. During the transfusion, the patient must
receive a blood that is compatible with his/her own. If the blood types do not match, the red blood cells will clump together and form clots that can block blood vessels and cause death.

If two different blood groups are mixed, the blood cells can clump together in the blood vessels, causing potentially fatal diseases. Therefore, it is important to match blood types before performing transfusions. In emergencies, blood group O may be given because all blood groups will accept it. However, there is always a risk. See
[
26
]
.

The compatibilities of Blood Types Donors are described as follows:

Blood Type

Donate Blood To

Receive Blood From

A

A, AB

A, O

B

B, AB

B, O

AB

AB

A, B, AB, O

O

A, B, AB, O

O

Kidney Transplantation:

Kidneys for transplantation come from two different sources:
a
living donor
or a
deceased donor
. See
[
27
]
.

The Living Donor:

In most cases, the donor is a family member. The donor must be in excellent health, well informed about transplantation, and able to give informed consent. Any healthy person can donate a kidney safely.

Deceased Donor:

It is a person who has suffered brain death. The kidneys are removed and stored until a recipient has been selected.

Regardless of the type of kidney transplant-living donor or deceased donor-special blood tests are needed to find out what type of blood and tissue is present. These test results help to match a donor kidney to the recipient.

In every two above cases, special blood tests and tissue are needed to find which help to match a donor’s kidney. To receive a kidney where the recipient’s markers and the donor’s markers all are the same is a "perfect match" kidney. Perfect match transplants have the best chance of working for many years. Most perfectly matched kidney transplants come from siblings.

Crossmatch:

Throughout life, the body makes substances called
antibodies
that act to destroy foreign materials. The crossmatch is done by mixing the recipient’s blood with cells from the donor. If the crossmatch is positive, it means that there are antibodies against the donor. The recipient should not receive this particular kidney unless special treatment is done before transplantation to reduce the antibody levels. If the crossmatch is negative, it means the recipient does not have antibodies to the donor and that they are eligible to receive this kidney. See
[
41
]
.

Cross matches are performed several times during preparation for a living donor transplant, and a final crossmatch is performed within 48 hours before this type of transplant. The cross matches is a particular organization of kidney transplant with living donors authorized by the law of bioethics of July 7, 2011, and its decree of application published in September 2012. This donation is governed by three principles laid down by the law:

•

The information of the donor,

•

anonymity between the two pairs,

•

simultaneity of surgical interventions.

This solution can be considered when the loved one who wishes to donate is not compatible with the patient.

7.2
The models

In all the aforementioned applications, elements (donors, receivers, or couples donor/receiver) arrive into the healthcare system at random times, and with various specificities.
Compatibilities
between elements (donors with receiver, or couples donor/receiver with other couples) need to be taken into account when performing the matches between them.

Thereafter, we will address the particular case of
living donors
: we assume that the elements entering the system are
couples
of family members consisting of a giver and a receiver and that the giver and the receiver may not be compatible amongst them. This system is modeled as a stochastic matching model in which items (i.e., couples) are gathered into classes. Here, we say for instance that the class of a given item
i
is
(A,B)
if the giver of the couple
i
has blood type
A
and the receiver has blood type
B
. Items of the various classes enter the system following independent Poisson processes of designated intensities and using a simple homogenization argument, we focus on the embedded chain of the corresponding continuous-time system, namely, we work with a
discrete-time stochastic matching model
. Three types of matching can be taken into account:

1.

In the
one by one
matching model, elements can be matched within couples, if possible. This corresponds equivalently, to an extended bipartite matching model (EBM) as defined in Chapter
3
, in which items are single individuals rather than couples, and couples of items (giver/receiver) enter the system simultaneously. Then, the compatibility graph is bipartite, between givers and receivers.

2.

The
two by two
matching model corresponds to the cross-transplant system, namely, couples of items, says of classes
i
and
j
, can be matched if and only if the
giver class of class
i
is compatible with the receiver class of class
j
, and the giver class of class
j
is compatible with the receiver class of class
i
. Then the two transplants are performed simultaneously. In our framework, this corresponds to a general stochastic matching model (GM) in which the compatibility graph is general, and represents the compatibilities between
couples
, in the sense specified above. A
matching
between two couples is made whenever the two matchings are performed together.

3.

The
three by three
matching model allows couples to be matched by groups of three. Then, a match between the three couples of respective classes
i
,
j
and
k
is possible if and only if the giver of
i
is compatible with the receiver of
j
, the giver of
j
is compatible with the receiver of
k
and the giver of
k
is compatible with the receiver of
i
. Then, the three transplants can be performed simultaneously.
These settings thus correspond to a general stochastic model on a hypergraph that is
3
-uniform, namely, matches are performed between groups of three items (i.e., couples) only.

Figure 7.1
:
Paired and pooled organ donation - Cross matching Two-by-Two and Three-by-Three
[
15
]
.

To compare these three concurrent matching architectures, we consider a toy example in which only four couple classes are present:

1:=(A,AB),\,2:=(O,AB),\,3:=(B,AB)\,\mbox{ and }\,4:=(A,B).

(7.1)

This situation may occur if we address only a designated part of the whole transplant network, or if we consider an access control, for instance.
We then set
{\mathcal{V}}=\{1,2,3,4\}
, and consider the various matching structure
\mathbb{S}=({\mathcal{V}},\mathcal{S}),
on
{\mathcal{V}}
that correspond, respectively, to the various types of matchings introduced above.

Remark 7.1
.

As will appear clearly below, similar matching models are obtained if, instead of
(
7.1
), we consider for instance arrivals of the following
couples,

•

1=(A,AB),

2=(O,AB),

3=(B,A)
and
4=(A,B);

•

1=(A,AB),

2=(O,A),

3=(B,A)
and
4=(A,B);

•

1=(B,AB),

2=(O,B),

3=(A,B)
and
4=(B,A);

•

1=(A,AB),

2=(B,AB),

3=(A,B)
and
4=(O,A);

•

And so on….

7.3
Matching one by one

In the above case, the matching one-by-one is preferable for items of classes
1
,
2
, and
3
, since the transplants between the giver and the receiver of each of these compatible couples can be made between family members. However, it is easily seen that, if incoming items of classes
1
,
2
and
3
are matched ‘with themselves’ in a systematic way, then the elements of the couples of class
4
, which are not compatible, will never be matched since a
A
-giver cannot give to a
B
-receiver. Then the resulting system is unstable since
class
4
-items will accumulate to infinity.

7.4
Matching two by two

Now, consider the case of the two by two matching. In this type of matching,
the matching structure
\mathbb{S}
is a graph such that

\mathbb{S}=\mathbb{G}=({\mathcal{V}},{\mathcal{E}})
and whose edges are defined as

{\mathcal{E}}=\left\{\{1,2\},\;\{1,3\},\;\{2,3\},\;\{2,4\},\;\{3,4\}\right\},

as is easily seen. The graph is thus a complete
3
-partite graph of order 4, as depicted in Figure
7.2
below. In other words,
\mathbb{G}
is analog to separable graph of order 3 and
I_{1}=\{2\},\;I_{2}=\{3\}
and
I_{3}=\{1,4\}
are the maximal independent sets partitionning
{\mathcal{V}}
,

1
2
3
4

Figure 7.2
:
The compatible complete
3
-partite graph
\mathbb{G}
of order 4.

From
[
33
, Section 3]
, we get the following necessary condition of stability
for any matching model on
\mathbb{G}
,

\textsc{Ncond}(\mathbb{G})=\left\{\begin{array}[]{lc}\mu(2)&<1/2;\\
\mu(3)&<1/2;\\
\mu(1)+\mu(4)&<1/2.\end{array}\right.

(7.2)

Proposition 7.1
.

Consider the graph
\mathbb{G}=({\mathcal{V}},{\mathcal{E}}).
Then, for all matching policy
\Phi
the sets
\textsc{Stab}(\mathbb{G},\Phi)
and
\textsc{Ncond}(\mathbb{G})
coincide, that is, the general stochastic matching model
(\mathbb{G},\Phi,\mu)
is stable if and only if

\mu
satisfies condition (
7.2
).
In other words we have

\textsc{Stab}(\mathbb{G},\Phi)=\left\{\begin{array}[]{lc}\mu(2)&<1/2;\\
\mu(3)&<1/2;\\
\mu(1)+\mu(4)&<1/2.\end{array}\right.

(7.3)

Proof.

As
\mathbb{G}
is a separable graph of order 3, then from
[
33
, Proposition 2]
, we get that

\forall\Phi,\;\;\mu\in\textsc{Stab}(\mathbb{G},\Phi)\iff\mu\in\textsc{Ncond}(\mathbb{G}).

∎

Proposition 7.2
.

Consider the graph
\mathbb{G}=({\mathcal{V}},{\mathcal{E}}),
and any admissible matching policy
\Phi
. Then the stationary probability of the natural Markov chain
(X_{n})_{n\in{\mathbb{N}}}
reads as follows: for all
x\in\mathbb{X}
,

\pi(x)=\left\{\begin{array}[]{lllc}\alpha\left(\displaystyle\frac{\mu(2)}{1-\mu(2)}\right)^{i}&x=(0,i,0,0)&;\;i\geq 1\\
\\
\alpha\left(\displaystyle\frac{\mu(3)}{1-\mu(3)}\right)^{j}&x=(0,0,j,0)&;\;j\geq 1\\
\\
\alpha\left(\displaystyle\frac{\mu(1)+\mu(4)}{1-(\mu(1)+\mu(4))}\right)^{k+\ell}&x=(k,0,0,\ell)&;\;k\vee\ell\geq 1\end{array}\right.

(7.4)

where the normalizing constant is given by

\alpha=\pi(0,0,0,0)=\displaystyle\frac{(1-2\mu(2))(1-2\mu(3))(1-2(\mu(1)+\mu(4)))}{4\mu(2)\mu(3)(\mu(1)+\mu(4))}.

(7.5)

Proof.

Whatever
\Phi
is, the transition matrix
P
of the class-content is defined as follows,

\left\{\begin{array}[]{llc}P((0,i,0,0),(0,i+1,0,0))&=\mu(2);\\
P((0,i,0,0),(0,i-1,0,0))&=1-\mu(2);\\
P((0,0,j,0),(0,0,j+1,0))&=\mu(3);\\
P((0,0,j,0),(0,0,j-1,0))&=1-\mu(3);\\
P((k,0,0,\ell),(k+1,0,0,\ell))&=\mu(1);\\
P((k,0,0,\ell),(k,0,0,\ell+1))&=\mu(4);\\
P((k,0,0,\ell),(k-1,0,0,\ell))&=\mu(2)+\mu(3)&\textrm{ choice }1;\\
P((k,0,0,\ell),(k,0,0,\ell-1))&=\mu(2)+\mu(3)&\textrm{ choice }4.\end{array}\right.

Then, we get

\pi(x)=\left\{\begin{array}[]{lllc}\pi(0,0,0,0)\left(\displaystyle\frac{\mu(2)}{1-\mu(2)}\right)^{i}&x=(0,i,0,0)&;\;i\geq 1\\
\\
\pi(0,0,0,0)\left(\displaystyle\frac{\mu(3)}{1-\mu(3)}\right)^{j}&x=(0,0,j,0)&;\;j\geq 1\\
\\
\pi(0,0,0,0)\left(\displaystyle\frac{\mu(1)+\mu(4)}{1-(\mu(1)+\mu(4))}\right)^{k+\ell}&x=(k,0,0,\ell)&;\;k\vee\ell\geq 1.\end{array}\right.

Set
\pi(0,0,0,0)=\pi(0),
and for any
i,j,k\geq 1
we set the following,

\pi(0,i,0,0)=\pi_{2}(i),\quad\pi(0,0,j,0)=\pi_{3}(j)\textrm{ and }\pi(k,0,0,\ell)=\pi_{1,4}(k+\ell).

Then, we have

\displaystyle 1

\displaystyle=\sum\limits_{i\geq 1}\pi_{2}(i)+\sum\limits_{j\geq 1}\pi_{3}(j)+\sum\limits_{k\vee\ell\geq 1}\pi_{1,4}(k+\ell)+\pi(0)

\displaystyle=\pi(0)\left[\sum\limits_{i\geq 1}\left(\frac{\mu(2)}{1-\mu(2)}\right)^{i}+\sum\limits_{j\geq 1}\left(\frac{\mu(3)}{1-\mu(3)}\right)^{j}+\sum\limits_{k\vee\ell\geq 1}\left(\frac{\mu(1)+\mu(4)}{1-(\mu(1)+\mu(4))}\right)^{k+\ell}+1\right]

\displaystyle=\pi(0)\left[\frac{\mu(2)}{1-2\mu(2)}+\frac{\mu(3)}{1-2\mu(3)}+\frac{\mu(1)+\mu(4)}{1-2(\mu(1)+\mu(4))}+1\right]

\displaystyle=\pi(0)\left[\displaystyle\frac{4\mu(2)\mu(3)(\mu(1)+\mu(4))}{(1-2\mu(2))(1-2\mu(3))(1-2(\mu(1)+\mu(4)))}\right],

and we conclude that

\pi(0,0,0,0)=\displaystyle\frac{(1-2\mu(2))(1-2\mu(3))(1-2(\mu(1)+\mu(4)))}{4\mu(2)\mu(3)(\mu(1)+\mu(4))}.

(7.6)

∎

Proposition 7.3
.

Consider the graph
\mathbb{G}.

If
\Phi=
fcfm
, then we retrieve the results of

[
36
, Theorem 1]
.

Proof.

The set of admissible queue details
\mathbb{W}
is given by,

\displaystyle\mathbb{W}=

\displaystyle\{\varepsilon\}\cup\left\{2^{k}:k\geq 1\right\}\cup\left\{3^{k}:k\geq 1\right\}

\displaystyle\cup\left\{1^{r_{1}}4^{\ell_{1}}1^{r_{2}}4^{\ell_{2}}...:k,k^{\prime}\geq 0,\;0\leq r_{i}\leq k,\;0\leq\ell_{j}\leq k^{\prime},\;i=\{1,2,...\},\;j=\{1,2,...\}\right\}.

We compute explicitly
\Pi_{W}
, as the following values:

\left\{\begin{array}[]{llll}\Pi_{W}(\varepsilon)&=\alpha\\
\Pi_{W}(2^{k})&=\alpha\left(\frac{\mu(2)}{1-\mu(2)}\right)^{k}\\
\Pi_{W}(3^{k})&=\alpha\left(\frac{\mu(3)}{1-\mu(3)}\right)^{k}\\
\Pi_{W}(1^{r_{1}}4^{\ell_{1}}1^{r_{2}}4^{\ell_{2}}...)&=\alpha\left(\frac{\mu(1)}{\mu(2)+\mu(3)}\right)^{r_{1}}\times\left(\frac{\mu(4)}{\mu(2)+\mu(3)}\right)^{\ell_{1}}\times\left(\frac{\mu(1)}{\mu(2)+\mu(3)}\right)^{r_{2}}\times\left(\frac{\mu(4)}{\mu(2)+\mu(3)}\right)^{\ell_{2}}...\\
&=\alpha\left(\frac{\mu(1)}{\mu(2)+\mu(3)}\right)^{k}\times\left(\frac{\mu(4)}{\mu(2)+\mu(3)}\right)^{k^{\prime}}\end{array}\right.

with

\displaystyle\alpha=\left[1+\frac{\mu(1)}{\mu(2)+\mu(3)-\mu(1)}+\frac{\mu(2)}{1-2\mu(2)}+\frac{\mu(3)}{1-2\mu(3)}+\frac{\mu(4)}{\mu(2)+\mu(3)-\mu(4)}\right.

\displaystyle+\left(\frac{\mu(1)}{\mu(2)+\mu(3)-\mu(1)}\right)\left(\frac{\mu(4)}{\mu(2)+\mu(3)-\mu(1)-\mu(4)}\right)\qquad

\displaystyle+\left.\left(\frac{\mu(4)}{\mu(2)+\mu(3)-\mu(4)}\right)\left(\frac{\mu(1)}{\mu(2)+\mu(3)-\mu(1)-\mu(4)}\right)\right]^{-1},

where we use the fact that the set of independent sets reads

\mathbb{I}\left(\mathbb{G}\right)=\{\{1\},\{2\},\{3\},\{4\},\{1,4\}\}
.
∎

7.5
Matching three by three

We now consider the three by three matching procedure: for any triplet of items of respectives classes
(X_{1},Y_{1}),\;(X_{2},Y_{2})
and
(X_{3},Y_{3}),
then
X_{1}
donates to
Y_{2},

X_{2}
donates to
Y_{3}
and
X_{3}
donates to
Y_{1}.
In this type of matching, the matching structure
\mathbb{S}
is an hypergraph. Specifically,
we set
\mathbb{S}=\mathbb{H}=({\mathcal{V}},{\mathcal{H}})
, where the hyperedges are defined as
H_{1}=\{1,2,3\},\;H_{2}=\{1,4,2\},\;H_{3}=\{4,3,1\},
and
H_{4}=\{4,2,3\}
. In other words, the hypergraph is a complete
3
-uniform hypergraph as depicted in Figure
1.4
.

From Proposition
4.3
the necessary condition of stability for this matching model reads

\mathscr{N}^{-}_{3}(\mathbb{H})=\left\{\mu\in\mathscr{M}({\mathcal{V}})\;:\;\mu(i)<1/3,\;\forall i\in{\mathcal{V}}\right\}.

(7.7)

Proposition 7.4
.

Consider the hypergraph
\mathbb{H}=({\mathcal{V}},{\mathcal{H}})
. Then, for all matching policy
\Phi
the sets
\textsc{Stab}(\mathbb{H},\Phi)
and
\mathscr{N}^{-}_{3}(\mathbb{H})
coincide, in other words the general stochastic matching model
(\mathbb{H},\Phi,\mu)
is stable if and only if

\mu
satisfies condition (
7.7
).
In other words we have

\textsc{Stab}(\mathbb{H},\Phi)=\left\{\mu\in\mathscr{M}({\mathcal{V}})\;:\;\mu(i)<1/3,\;i\in{\mathcal{V}}.\right\}

(7.8)

Proof.

As
\mathbb{H}
is a complete 3-uniform hypergraph of order 4, the result follows from Theorem
4.1
.
∎

7.6
Comparaison between
\textsc{Stab}(\mathbb{G},\Phi)
and
\textsc{Stab}(\mathbb{H},\Phi)

To summarize, we obtain that the stability region of the two-by-two matching system
is
\textsc{Stab}(\mathbb{G},\Phi)
given by (
7.3
), whereas the stability region of the three-by-three
matching system is
\textsc{Stab}(\mathbb{H},\Phi)
, given by (
7.8
).

Let us observe that the two regions are not included in one another. Indeed, it is easily checked that
the probability measure
\mu(1)=0.25,\;\mu(2)=0.35,\;\mu(3)=0.2
and
\mu(4)=0.2
,
is an element
\textsc{Stab}(\mathbb{G},\Phi)
but not of

\textsc{Stab}(\mathbb{H},\Phi).
On the other hand, the probability measure

\mu(1)=0.3,

\mu(2)=0.2,

\mu(3)=0.2
and
\mu(4)=0.3
clearly belongs to

\textsc{Stab}(\mathbb{H},\Phi)
, but is not an element of
\textsc{Stab}(\mathbb{G},\Phi).

Conclusion 1:
Take the stability of the system as a primary performance criterion.
Then, the one-by-one matching is never stable. Second, it is preferable to perform matchings two-by-two in some cases, and matchings three-by-three in other cases.

The intersection between the two stability regions is given by

\textsc{Stab}(\mathbb{G},\Phi)\bigcap\textsc{Stab}(\mathbb{H},\Phi):=\left\{\begin{array}[]{lccc}\mu(i)<1/3&i\in{\mathcal{V}};\\
\mu(1)+\mu(4)<1/2.\end{array}\right.

(7.9)

The question arising now is the following: suppose that
\mu
satisfies (
7.9
), that is, it belongs to both
\textsc{Stab}(\mathbb{G},\Phi)
and
\textsc{Stab}(\mathbb{H},\Phi)
, implying that both systems are stable. Considering a secondary performance criterion, the frequency of construction points, that is, of visits to the zero state,
what is the best matching procedure
between two by two and three by three matchings?

We saw in Section
7.4
that the probability of finding an empty system in steady-state can be given in closed form for the two-by-two matching system. However, such a result is, to date, not available for the three-by-three system. We then resort to simulations to compare the two procedures.

We have simulated one thousand trajectories of the three-by-three system, each one starting from an empty system and consisting of one million arrivals. Then,

Step 1:

We count the total number of construction points over the million arrivals, and we get the average over the thousand trajectories.

Step 2:

We count the total number of empty buffers at the instance 999999 for each of one million arrivals and get the average over the thousand trajectories.

In Table
7.1
hereafter we present our results for different distributions
\mu
, applying for each distribution the two aforementioned steps. We denote the first average by ‘Trajectorial Average’ and the average of the empty buffers at time 999999 by ‘Av. EB.’ It is obvious that the three-by-three matching system is 3-periodic. Thus, starting from the empty state, it can be empty only at times that are multiples of three. So, it is only pertinent to compare the average of construction points for the whole trajectories (fourth column) to the third of the number of construction points seen at time 999999, a multiple of three (see the fifth column).

Conclusion 2:
In Table

we emphasize, first, the speed of convergence to the steady-state (approximated by the final state - column 5), as the results of columns 4 and 5 tend to coincide. Second, when comparing the frequency of construction points for the three-by-three system to the stationary probability of an empty two-by-two system, we see that the first one seems to perform better in some cases, while the second performs better for some other values of
\mu
.

7.7
Discussion of results and conclusion

In this chapter, we have proposed simple modeling of kidney transplant systems using stochastic matching models. We have shown that models on various matching structures (bipartite models, general models on graphs, and models on hypergraphs) are suitable to various contexts. In the context of cross-transplants, a simple case study has shown that there is no clear hierarchy between cross-transplants by pairs of couples (matchings two-by-two) and cross-transplants by triplets of couples (matching three by three) when it comes to comparing the stability regions.
In some cases, one system is stable while the other is not.
Second, we show that the same remark holds if we compare the frequency of construction points in simulated three-by-three systems to the (exact) value of the steady-state probability of the two-by-two system being empty: the first performs better for some values of
\mu
, while the second performs better otherwise. Moreover, we do not understand which is the best matching procedure.

Conclusion and perspectives

In this thesis, we have studied a generalization of stochastic matching models on the graph, by allowing the matching structure to be a hypergraph or multigraph.
This class of models appears to have a wide range of applications in operations management, healthcare, and assemble-to-order systems.
After formally introducing the model, we have proposed a simple Markovian representation, under IID assumptions.
We have then addressed the general question of stochastic stability, viewed as the positive recurrence of the underlying Markov chain. For this class of systems, solving this elementary and central question turns out to be an intricate problem.

In this thesis, the stochastic matching model in discrete-time is formally defined as follows: items enter the system by a single. On other hand, in continuous-time items enter the system according to an independent Poisson process of intensity
\lambda>0.
The arrivals get matched by groups of 2 or more (hypergraphical cases), following compatibilities that are represented by a given hypergraph and by groups of 2 with possible compatibility with itself (multigraphical cases). A matching policy determines the matchings to be executed in the case of a multiple-choice, and the unmatched items are stored in a buffer, waiting for a future match.

1. Conclusion

Stochastic matching models on hypergraphs are in general, difficult to stabilize. Unlike the GM on graphs, the non-emptiness of the stability region on matching models on hypergraphs depends on a collection of conditions on the geometry of the compatibility hypergraph: rank, anti-rank, degree, size of the transversals, existence of cycles, and so on.

Nevertheless, we showed that the ‘house’ of stable systems is not empty, but shelters models on various uniform hypergraphs that are complete, or complete up to a partition of their nodes. We have provided the
exact
stability region of the system
(\mathbb{H},\Phi,\mu)
where
\mathbb{H}=({\mathcal{V}},{\mathcal{H}})
is a complete 3-uniform hypergraphs and for all admissible matching policy
\Phi,
i.e.
\textsc{Stab}(\mathbb{H},\Phi)=\left\{\mu\;;\;\mu(i)<1/3,\textrm{ for all }i\in{\mathcal{V}}\right\}.
Also, we extended the exact stability region for complete 3-uniform
k
-partite hypergraph. Moreover, we demonstrated a lower bound for the stability region for the incomplete 3-uniform hypergraphs for a matching policy
ml
. For this, we resorted to ad-hoc multi-dimensional Lyapunov techniques in discrete-time (each step an item enters the system).

In this thesis, we have also studied a generalization of stochastic matching models on graphs by allowing the self-loops matching, that is, a stochastic matching model on multigraphs. Given a multigraph
G,
its maximal subgraph
\check{G}
obtained by deleting all self-loops in
G
and the minimal blow-up graph
\hat{G}
obtained from
G
by duplicating each node having a self-loop by two nodes having the same neighborhood and replacing each self-loop by an edge between the node and its copy. From that, we transmit and generalize several results that are known for
\check{G}
and
\hat{G}
to their multigraphs.

Also, in this context, we have provided the
exact
stability region under
fcfm
and
MW
policy such that
\beta>0.
Given a multigraph
G=({\mathcal{V}}_{1}\cup{\mathcal{V}}_{2},{\mathcal{E}}),
we introduced a new matching policy called
{\mathcal{V}}_{2}
-favorable, that is, for any arrival items always prioritizes to match with an item in
{\mathcal{V}}_{2}
over an item in
{\mathcal{V}}_{1}.

In addition, we have proved that if
\check{G}
is a complete
p
-partite graph,
(p\geq 2)
, then for
p\geq 3
or
{\mathcal{V}}_{1}\neq 0,
any
{\mathcal{V}}_{2}
-favorable matching policy is maximal.

In this thesis, we have also studied a new technique that allows us to find the stability of the model by using the fluid limits in continuous-time (items of the various classes enter the system following the independent Poisson process of designated intensities). This new method consists of speeding up the time and rescaling the process to get a sort of caricature of the initial stochastic process. We provide further results that are in agreement with the previous results. The advantage of the continuous-time setting is that powerful fluid-limit techniques can be employed, which greatly facilitate the stability analysis.

Indeed, we have also studied several cases of multigraphs such as pendant graphs with a self-loop on such vertex, we proved a lower bound of stability region. In other words, the model is stable if and only if the necessary condition for stability on continuous-time
\textsc{Ncond}_{C}
holds together with certain conditions. Moreover, we retrieved the results for some specific complete bipartite graphs with a self-loop on such vertex for
{\mathcal{V}}_{2}
-favorable. Further, using the fluid limits technique, we provided the exact stability region of the model on complete 3-uniform hypergraphs of order 4.

To illustrate the practical relevance of our results, we also have studied an application of the stochastic matching model that addressed particular cases of living donors in the context of cross-matching. In that case, the items enter the system by couples
(X,Y)
of family members, the first component
X
represents the ‘giver’ and the second component
Y
represents the ‘receiver’. The problem consists of studied a special case in three types of matching on various matching structures:

•

bipartite graphs (matching one-by-one);

•

general graphs (matching two-by-two);

•

complete 3-uniform hypergraphs (matching three-by-three).

We studied the performance criterion between the two-by-two matching system and three-by-three matching system of the frequency of visits to zero state, to distinguish what is the best matching procedure. Motivated by a simulation of one thousand trajectories of three-by-three, we see that the two-by-two seems to perform better in some cases, while three-by-three performs better for some other values of
\mu
. In this instance, we cannot conclude which is the best.

2. Perspectives

All the results obtained in this thesis, are according to some hypotheses: stochastic matching model on hypergraphs and multigraphs, stability region, steady-state, and so on.
There is still much to do regarding this class of systems. Let us give a few directions of research that we are currently following, or aim to follow in a near future:

•

Finding an explicit form of the stationary probability of the chain
\{W_{n};\;n\in{\mathbb{N}}\}
of the model
(\mathbb{H},\textsc{fcfm},\Phi).

•

Comparing the necessary conditions of stability of the model
(\mathbb{H},\textsc{fcfm},\Phi)
between them.

•

Applying the proposed matching model on hypergraphs and multigraphs for several domains of applications.

•

Comparing the simple hypergraphs, of the optimal policy with those obtained by the “Greedy Primal-Dual” optimization algorithms (Nazari and Stolyar, 2017), a dynamic control strategy introduced to maximize the utility of queue networks waiting subject to stability, which turns out to be (asymptotically) optimal in this case.

•

Comparing the matching policies for the models on the hypergraphs and the determination of an optimal matching policy:
mw
? In which way?

•

Determining the exact zones of stability of the random pairing models on particular cases of hypergraphs, in particular for small non-trivial hypergraphs such as the Fano plane (the projective plane of size 7) or the models representing the networks of cross kidney donation with loop.

•

Generalizing the results of Theorem
4.1
, to the complete
k
-uniform hypergraphs. In that case, we conjecture that the stability region is equal to
\mathscr{N}^{-}_{3}(\mathbb{H})=\{\mu,\;\mu(i)<1/k,\;i\in{\mathcal{V}}\}.

We believe that the present thesis represents a good starting point for a fruitful avenue for research on such systems.

Appendix

The transitions for the proof of Theorem
4.1

(i) First, for any
i\in\bar{J},
and any integer
x_{i}\geq 2,
the chain
\{Y_{n};\;n\in{\mathbb{N}}\}
makes the following transitions from state
Y_{n}=x_{i}.\textbf{e}_{i},

Y_{n+1}=\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}Y_{n}+4\textbf{e}_{i}&\mbox{ w.p. }\mu(i)^{4};\\
Y_{n}+3\textbf{e}_{i}+\textbf{e}_{j}&\mbox{ w.p. }4\mu(i)^{3}\mu(j);\\
Y_{n}+2\textbf{e}_{i}+2\textbf{e}_{j}&\mbox{ w.p. }6\mu(i)^{2}\mu(j)^{2};\\
Y_{n}+\textbf{e}_{i}+3\textbf{e}_{j}&\mbox{ w.p. }4\mu(i)\mu(j)^{3};\\
Y_{n}+4\textbf{e}_{j}&\mbox{ w.p. }\mu(j)^{4};\\
Y_{n}+\textbf{e}_{i}&\mbox{ w.p. }12\mu(i)^{2}\mu(j)\mu(k);\\
Y_{n}+\textbf{e}_{j}&\mbox{ w.p. }12\mu(i)\mu(j)^{2}\mu(k);\\
Y_{n}-2\textbf{e}_{i}&\mbox{ w.p. }10\mu(j)^{2}\mu(k)\mu(\ell)\\
&\mbox{ (\scriptsize{the input has 2 $j$, 1 $k$ and 1 $\ell$, but does not end in $jj$});}\\
Y_{n}-\textbf{e}_{i}+2\textbf{e}_{j}&\mbox{ w.p. }2\mu(k)\mu(\ell)\mu(j)^{2}\\
&\mbox{ (\scriptsize{the input is of the form $ijjj$})};\\
Y_{n}-2\textbf{e}_{i}&\mbox{ w.p. }6\mu(j)^{2}\mu(k)^{2};\\
Y_{n}-\textbf{e}_{i}+2\textbf{e}_{j}&\mbox{ w.p. }4\mu(j)^{3}\mu(k);\\
Y_{n}+\textbf{e}_{j}&\mbox{ w.p. }24\mu(i)\mu(k)\mu(\ell)\mu(j);\\
Y_{n}-2\textbf{e}_{i}&\mbox{ w.p. }24\mu(j)\mu(k)\mu(\ell)\mu(m).\end{array}\right.

(7.10)

(ii) For any
i\in J,
and any integer
x_{i}\geq 2,
the transitions of
\{Y_{n};\;n\in{\mathbb{N}}\}
from the state
x_{i}.\textbf{e}_{i}
can be retrieved as a similar fashion to (
7.10
). Set
H=\{i,j,k\}\subset J,
we have the following transitions,

Y_{n+1}=\left\{\begin{array}[]{llll}Y_{n}+4\textbf{e}_{i}&\mbox{ w.p. }\mu(i)^{4};\\
Y_{n}+3\textbf{e}_{i}+\textbf{e}_{\ell}&\mbox{ w.p. }4\mu(i)^{3}\mu(\ell)&\ell\in{\mathcal{V}}\backslash\{i\};\\
Y_{n}+2\textbf{e}_{i}+2\textbf{e}_{\ell}&\mbox{ w.p. }6\mu(i)^{2}\mu(\ell)^{2}&\ell\in{\mathcal{V}}\backslash\{i\};\\
Y_{n}+\textbf{e}_{i}+3\textbf{e}_{\ell}&\mbox{ w.p. }4\mu(i)\mu(\ell)^{3}&\ell\in{\mathcal{V}}\backslash\{i\};\\
Y_{n}+4\textbf{e}_{\ell}&\mbox{ w.p. }\mu(\ell)^{4}&\ell\in{\mathcal{V}}\backslash\{i\};\\
Y_{n}+\textbf{e}_{j}&\mbox{ w.p. }12\mu(i)\mu(j)^{2}\mu(\ell)&\ell\in\overline{H};\\
Y_{n}+\textbf{e}_{\ell}&\mbox{ w.p. }12\mu(i)\mu(j)\mu(\ell)^{2}&\ell\in\overline{H};\\
Y_{n}+\textbf{e}_{\ell}&\mbox{ w.p. }12\mu(i)\mu(\ell)^{2}\mu(m)&\ell\neq m\in\overline{H};\\
Y_{n}+\textbf{e}_{j}&\mbox{ w.p. }6\mu(i)\mu(j)\mu(\ell)\mu(m)&\ell\neq m\in\overline{H}&\textrm{ends with }j;\\
Y_{n}+\textbf{e}_{\ell}&\mbox{ w.p. }18\mu(i)\mu(j)\mu(\ell)\mu(m)&\ell\neq m\in\overline{H}&\textrm{otherwise};\\
Y_{n}+\textbf{e}_{\ell}&\mbox{ w.p. }24\mu(i)\mu(\ell)\mu(m)\mu(p)&\ell\neq m\neq p\in\overline{H};\\
Y_{n}+\textbf{e}_{j}+3\textbf{e}_{k}&\mbox{ w.p. }4\mu(j)\mu(k)^{3};\\
Y_{n}-\textbf{e}_{i}+2\textbf{e}_{\ell}&\mbox{ w.p. }4\mu(j)\mu(\ell)^{3}&\ell\in\overline{H};\\
Y_{n}-\textbf{e}_{i}+2\textbf{e}_{k}&\mbox{ w.p. }2\mu(j)\mu(k)^{2}\mu(\ell)&\ell\in\overline{H}&\textrm{ends with }kk;\\
Y_{n}-\textbf{e}_{i}+\textbf{e}_{j}+\textbf{e}_{k}&\mbox{ w.p. }10\mu(j)\mu(k)^{2}\mu(\ell)&\ell\in\overline{H}&\textrm{otherwise};\\
Y_{n}-2\textbf{e}_{i}&\mbox{ w.p. }12\mu(j)\mu(k)\mu(\ell)^{2}&\ell\in\overline{H};\\
Y_{n}-\textbf{e}_{i}+2\textbf{e}_{\ell}&\mbox{ w.p. }2\mu(j)\mu(\ell)^{2}\mu(m)&\ell\neq m\in\overline{H}&\textrm{ends with }\ell\ell;\\
Y_{n}-2\textbf{e}_{i}&\mbox{ w.p. }10\mu(j)\mu(\ell)^{2}\mu(m)&\ell\neq m\in\overline{H}&\textrm{otherwise};\\
Y_{n}-\textbf{e}_{i}+\textbf{e}_{j}+\textbf{e}_{k}&\mbox{ w.p. }4\mu(j)\mu(k)\mu(\ell)\mu(m)&\ell\neq m\in\overline{H}&\textrm{ends with }jk;\\
Y_{n}-2\textbf{e}_{i}&\mbox{ w.p. }20\mu(j)\mu(k)\mu(\ell)\mu(m)&\ell\neq m\in\overline{H}&\textrm{otherwise};\\
Y_{n}-2\textbf{e}_{i}&\mbox{ w.p. }24\mu(j)\mu(\ell)\mu(m)\mu(p)&\ell\neq m\neq p\in\overline{H};\\
Y_{n}+2\textbf{e}_{i}+\textbf{e}_{j}+\textbf{e}_{k}&\mbox{ w.p. }12\mu(i)^{2}\mu(j)\mu(k);\\
Y_{n}+\textbf{e}_{i}&\mbox{ w.p. }12\mu(i)^{2}\mu(j)\mu(\ell)&\ell\in\overline{H};\\
Y_{n}+\textbf{e}_{i}&\mbox{ w.p. }12\mu(i)^{2}\mu(\ell)\mu(m)&\ell\neq m\in\overline{H};\\
Y_{n}+2\textbf{e}_{j}+2\textbf{e}_{k}&\mbox{ w.p. }6\mu(j)^{2}\mu(k)^{2};\\
Y_{n}-2\textbf{e}_{i}&\mbox{ w.p. }6\mu(j)^{2}\mu(\ell)^{2}&\ell\in\overline{H};\\
Y_{n}-\textbf{e}_{i}+2\textbf{e}_{j}&\mbox{ w.p. }2\mu(j)^{2}\mu(\ell)\mu(m)&\ell\neq m\in\overline{H}&\textrm{ends with }jj;\\
Y_{n}-2\textbf{e}_{i}&\mbox{ w.p. }10\mu(j)^{2}\mu(\ell)\mu(m)&\ell\neq m\in\overline{H}&\textrm{otherwise};\\
Y_{n}-\textbf{e}_{i}+2\textbf{e}_{j}&\mbox{ w.p. }4\mu(j)^{3}\mu(\ell)&\ell\in\overline{H};\\
Y_{n}+\textbf{e}_{i}+\textbf{e}_{j}+2\textbf{e}_{k}&\mbox{ w.p. }12\mu(i)\mu(j)\mu(k)^{2};\\
Y_{n}+\textbf{e}_{j}&\mbox{ w.p. }24\mu(i)\mu(j)\mu(k)\mu(\ell)&\ell\in\overline{H};\\
Y_{n}-\textbf{e}_{i}+2\textbf{e}_{\ell}&\mbox{ w.p. }4\mu(\ell)^{3}\mu(m)&\ell\neq m\in\overline{H};\\
Y_{n}-2\textbf{e}_{i}&\mbox{ w.p. }6\mu(\ell)^{2}\mu(m)^{2}&\ell\neq m\in\overline{H};\\
Y_{n}-\textbf{e}_{i}+2\textbf{e}_{\ell}&\mbox{ w.p. }2\mu(\ell)^{2}\mu(m)\mu(p)&\ell\neq m\neq p\in\overline{H}&\textrm{ends with }\ell\ell;\\
Y_{n}-2\textbf{e}_{i}&\mbox{ w.p. }10\mu(\ell)^{2}\mu(m)\mu(p)&\ell\neq m\neq p\in\overline{H}&\textrm{otherwise};\\
Y_{n}-2\textbf{e}_{i}&\mbox{ w.p. }24\mu(\ell)\mu(m)\mu(p)\mu(s)&\ell\neq m\neq p\neq s\in\overline{H}.\end{array}\right.

(7.11)

Then we decduce that,

\Delta^{\prime}_{i}:={\mathbb{E}}\left[Q\left(Y_{n+1}\right)-Q\left(Y_{n}\right)|Y_{n}=x_{i}.\textbf{e}_{i}\right]=\\
\begin{aligned} &\;(8a_{i}+16)\mu^{4}(i)+4(6a_{i}+10)\mu^{3}(i)\sum_{\ell\in{\mathcal{V}}}\mu(\ell)+6(4a_{i}+8)\mu^{2}(i)\sum_{\ell\in{\mathcal{V}}}\mu^{2}(\ell)+4(2a_{i}+10)\mu(i)\sum_{\ell\in{\mathcal{V}}}\mu^{3}(\ell)\\
&+16\sum_{\ell\in{\mathcal{V}}}\mu^{4}(\ell)+24\mu(i)\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}j\in H:\\
\ell\in\overline{H}\end{subarray}}\mu^{2}(j)\mu(\ell)+24\mu(i)\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}j\in H:\\
\ell\in\overline{H}\end{subarray}}\mu(j)\mu^{2}(\ell)+24\mu(i)\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\ell,m\in\overline{H}\end{subarray}}\mu^{2}(\ell)\mu(m)\\
&+12\mu(i)\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}j\in H:\\
\ell,m\in\overline{H}:\\
\textrm{ends with }j\end{subarray}}\mu(j)\mu(\ell)\mu(m)+36\mu(i)\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}j\in H:\\
\ell,m\in\overline{H}:\\
\textrm{otherwise}\end{subarray}}\mu(j)\mu(\ell)\mu(m)+48\mu(i)\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\ell,m,p\in\overline{H}\end{subarray}}\mu(\ell)\mu(m)\mu(p)\qquad\\
&+44\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}j,k\in H\end{subarray}}\mu(j)\mu^{3}(k)-4(2a_{i}+5)\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}j\in H:\\
\ell\in\overline{H}\end{subarray}}\mu(j)\mu^{3}(\ell)-2(2a_{i}+5)\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}j,k\in H:\\
\ell\in\overline{H}:\\
\textrm{ends with }kk\end{subarray}}\mu(j)\mu^{2}(k)\mu(\ell)\\
&-10(2a_{i}+3)\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}j,k\in H:\\
\ell\in\overline{H}:\\
\textrm{otherwise}\end{subarray}}\mu(j)\mu^{2}(k)\mu(\ell)-12(4a_{i}+4)\mu(j)\mu(k)\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\ell\in\overline{H}\end{subarray}}\mu^{2}(\ell)\\
&-2(2a_{i}+5)\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}j\in H:\\
\ell\in\overline{H}:\\
\textrm{ends with }\ell\ell\end{subarray}}\mu(j)\mu^{2}(\ell)\mu(m)-10(4a_{i}+4)\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}j\in H:\\
\ell\in\overline{H}:\\
\textrm{otherwise}\end{subarray}}\mu(j)\mu^{2}(\ell)\mu(m)\\
&-4(2a_{i}+3)\mu(j)\mu(k)\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\ell,m\in\overline{H}:\\
\textrm{ends with }jk\end{subarray}}\mu(\ell)\mu(m)-20(4a_{i}+4)\mu(j)\mu(k)\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\ell,m\in\overline{H}:\\
\textrm{otherwise}\end{subarray}}\mu(\ell)\mu(m)\\
&-24(4a_{i}+4)\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}j\in H:\\
\ell,m\in\overline{H}\end{subarray}}\mu(j)\mu(\ell)\mu(m)\mu(p)+12(4a_{i}+6)\mu^{2}(i)\mu(j)\mu(k)\\
&+12(2a_{i}+1)\mu^{2}(i)\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}j\in H:\\
\ell\in\overline{H}\end{subarray}}\mu(j)\mu(\ell)+12(2a_{i}+1)\mu^{2}(i)\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\ell,m\in\overline{H}\end{subarray}}\mu(\ell)\mu(m)+48\mu^{2}(j)\mu^{2}(k)\\
&-6(4a_{i}+4)\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}j\in H:\\
\ell\in\overline{H}\end{subarray}}\mu^{2}(j)\mu^{2}(\ell)-2(2a_{i}+5)\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}j\in H:\\
\ell\in\overline{H}:\\
\textrm{ends with }jj\end{subarray}}\mu^{2}(j)\mu(\ell)\mu(m)\\
&-10(4a_{i}+4)\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}j\in H:\\
\ell\in\overline{H}:\\
\textrm{otherwise}\end{subarray}}\mu^{2}(j)\mu(\ell)\mu(m)-4(2a_{i}+5)\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}j\in H:\\
\ell\in\overline{H}\end{subarray}}\mu^{3}(j)\mu(\ell)+12(2a_{i}+6)\mu(i)\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}j,k\in H\end{subarray}}\mu(j)\mu^{2}(k)\\
&+48\mu(i)\mu(j)\mu(k)\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\ell\in\overline{H}\end{subarray}}\mu(\ell)-4(2a_{i}+5)\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\ell,m\in\overline{H}\end{subarray}}\mu^{3}(\ell)\mu(m)-6(4a_{i}+4)\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\ell,m\in\overline{H}\end{subarray}}\mu^{2}(\ell)\mu^{2}(m)\\

\textrm{otherwise}\end{subarray}}\mu^{2}(\ell)\mu(m)\mu(p)\\
&-24(4a_{i}+4)\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\ell,m,p,s\in\overline{H}\end{subarray}}\mu(\ell)\mu(m)\mu(p)\mu(s)=\nu_{i}(\mu)x_{i}+\beta^{\prime}_{i}(\mu),\end{aligned}

(7.12)
